The singular set in the Stefan problem
Alessio Figalli, Xavier Ros-Oton, Joaquim Serra

Abstract
. In this paper we analyze the singular set in the Stefan problem and prove the following results: • The singular set has parabolic Hausdorff dimension at most n − 1 n-1 . • The solution admits a C ∞ C^{\infty} -expansion at all singular points, up to a set of parabolic Hausdorff dimension at most n − 2 n-2 . • In ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , the free boundary is smooth for almost every time t t , and the set of singular times 𝒮 ⊂ ℝ \mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R} has Hausdorff dimension at most 1 / 2 1/2 . These results provide us with a refined understanding of the Stefan problem’s singularities and answer some long-standing open questions in the field.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35R35; 35B65.

1.
Introduction

The Stefan problem, dating back to the XIXth century
[
St
,
LC
]
, aims to describe phase transitions, such as ice melting to water, and it is among the most classical and well-known free boundary problems.
In its simplest formulation, this problem consists in finding the evolution of the temperature

\theta(x,t)
of the water when a block of ice is submerged inside.
Then, the function
\theta\geq 0
satisfies
\partial_{t}\theta-\Delta\theta=0
in the region
\{\theta>0\}
, while the evolution of the free boundary
\partial\{\theta>0\}
(the interphase ice/water) is dictated by the Stefan condition
\partial_{t}\theta=|\nabla_{x}\theta|^{2}
on
\partial\{\theta>0\}
.

Let
\chi_{A}
denotes the characteristic function of a set
A
.
After the transformation
u(x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\theta(x,\tau)d\tau
, one can note that

\{u>0\}=\{\theta>0\}
. Also, as explained in
[
Duv73
,
Bai74
]
(see also
[
Fig18a
]
), the Stefan problem becomes locally equivalent to the so-called “parabolic obstacle problem”:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{split}\partial_{t}u-\Delta u&=-\chi_{\{u>0\}}\\
u&\geq 0\\
\partial_{t}u&\geq 0\\
\partial_{t}u&>0\quad{\rm in}\,\{u>0\}\end{split}\right.\qquad\qquad\quad\textrm{in}\quad\Omega\times(0,T)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}.

The regularity of the free boundary for the Stefan problem (
1.1
) was developed in the groundbreaking paper
[
Caf77
]
.
The main result therein establishes that the free boundary (i.e., the interface
\partial\{u>0\}
) is
C^{\infty}
in space and time, outside some closed set
\Sigma\subset\Omega\times(0,T)
of
singular points
at which the contact set
\{u=0\}
has zero density.

1.1.
The singular set

The fine understanding of singularities is a central research topic in several areas related to nonlinear PDEs and Geometric Analysis.
A major question in such context is to establish estimates for the
size
of the singular set — see
[
Sim68
,
CKN82
,
Whi00
,
Alm00
]
for some famous examples.

For the Stefan problem (
1.1
), a variant of the techniques used in the study of the elliptic obstacle problem yields the following result: for every
t
, let
\Sigma_{t}
denote the singular points at time
t
(so that
\Sigma=\cup_{t\in(0,T)}\Sigma_{t}\times\{t\}
).
Then
\Sigma_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is locally contained in a
(n-1)
-dimensional
C^{1}
manifold
[
LM15
,
Bla06
]
.
Furthermore, the whole singular set
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
is contained in a
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold which is
C^{1}
in space and
C^{0,1/2}
in time; see
[
LM15
]
for more details.
This result is optimal in space, in the sense that the singular set could be
(n-1)
-dimensional for a fixed time
t=t_{0}
.
However, it is not clear what the size of the singular set
in time
should be.

The first natural question in this direction is to estimate the parabolic Hausdorff dimension
1
1

1

The parabolic Hausdorff dimension is, by definition, the Hausdorff dimension associated with the “parabolic distance”
d_{\rm par}\bigl((x_{1},t_{1}),(x_{2},t_{2})\bigr)=\sqrt{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}+|t_{1}-t_{2}|}
.
Notice that, if we denote by
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(E)
the standard Hausdorff dimension of a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
, then
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(E)\leq{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(E)
.
On the other hand, the time axis has parabolic Hausdorff dimension 2, while it has standard Hausdorff dimension 1.

of the singular set
\Sigma
, denoted
{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma)
.
The results in
[
LM15
,
Bla06
]
imply that
{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma)\leq n+\frac{1}{2}
, and no better estimate was known.
Here, by refining our understanding of singular points, we establish the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set, let
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))
solve the Stefan problem (
1.1
), and let

\Sigma\subset\Omega\times(0,T)
be the set of singular points.
Then

{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma)\leq n-1.

As mentioned above, the singular set could be
(n-1)
-dimensional in space for a fixed time
t=t_{0}
. Hence our result gives the optimal bound for the parabolic Hausdorff dimension of
\Sigma
.
Also, since the time axis has parabolic Hausdorff dimension 2, Theorem
1.1
implies that, in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the free boundary is smooth for almost every time
t\in(0,T)
.
Therefore, it is natural to ask ourselves if a similar result holds in the physical space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and, more in general, “how often” singular points may appear.

In
[
Caf78
]
, Caffarelli showed that any
C^{1}
curve contained inside
\Sigma
cannot evolve with time (i.e., it must be contained in a fixed time slice
\{t=t_{0}\}
). However, apart from this result, nothing else was known concerning this question.
Here, we prove the following estimate on the size of singular times in the physical space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, let
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))
solve the Stefan problem (
1.1
), and let

(1.2)

\mathcal{S}:=\big\{t\in(0,T)\ :\ \exists\,(x,t)\in\Sigma\big\}

denote the set of “singular times”.
Then,

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{S})\leq\frac{1}{2}.

In particular, for almost every time
t\in(0,T)
, the free boundary is a
(n-1)
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of class
C^{\infty}
.

This is the first result on the size of the set of singular times for the Stefan problem. In particular, prior to this result, it was not even known if solutions to the Stefan problem (
1.1
) could have singularities for
all
times
t\in(0,T)
(not even when
n=2
).

We do not know if the dimensional bound
1/2
is sharp for the Stefan problem in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, but this bound is definitely critical
2
2

2

The reader familiar with fluid equations may note that
1/2
is the same bound currently known for the dimension of singular times in the Navier-Stokes equation; see the classical result of Caffarelli, Kohn, and Nirenberg
[
CKN82
]
. We do not see any connection between the fact that the numbers are the same. Actually, while for Navier-Stokes many people would like to prove that singular points do not exist, in the Stefan problem singular points exist and can be rather large. Also, given the analysis performed in our paper, the estimate
1/2
is sharp in many points (see Remark
1.5
).
, and we would not be surprised if our bound turned out to be optimal.

Our Theorem
1.2
above follows from more general result valid in arbitrary dimension
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
As explained in more detail below, we prove that the singular set is
C^{\infty}
, outside a small subset of parabolic dimension at most
n-2
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))
solve the Stefan problem (
1.1
). Then there exists
\Sigma^{\infty}\subset\Sigma
such that

{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\infty})\leq n-2,\qquad\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\{t\in(0,T)\ :\ \exists\,(x,t)\in\Sigma^{\infty}\}\big)=0,

and

\Sigma^{\infty}\subset\Omega\times(0,T)
can be covered by countably many
(n-1)
-dimensional submanifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
of class
C^{\infty}
.
3
3

3

Here, the submanifolds that cover
\Sigma^{\infty}
are of class
C^{\infty}
as subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with the usual Euclidean distance, not with respect the parabolic distance. So, our statement is much stronger than the previously known results (for instance,
[
LM15
]
proved
C^{1}
regularity of
\Sigma
with respect to the parabolic distance, which implies only
C^{1/2}
regularity in time).

In a sense, this result says that the singular set can be split into two separate pieces: one which is very smooth and extremely rare in time (the set
\Sigma^{\infty}
), and one which is small (of dimension at most
n-2
).

As a consequence, we also deduce the following corollary in 2 dimensions:

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))
solve the Stefan problem (
1.1
).
Let
\mathcal{S}
be as in (
1.2
).
Then

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{S})=0.

In the next section, we briefly explain the general strategy behind the proofs of our results.

1.2.
Ideas of the proof

To prove Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.3
we need to introduce a variety of new ideas with respect to the existing literature, combining tools from geometric measure theory (GMT), PDE estimates, dimension reduction-type arguments, and new monotonicity formulas.
Let us give a quick overview of the main steps in the proofs.

Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
be a singular point. It is well known that

(1.3)

u(x_{\circ}+x,t_{\circ}+t)=p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x)+o(|x|^{2}+|t|),

where
p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x)
is a quadratic polynomial of the form
\frac{1}{2}x^{T}Ax
, with
A\geq 0
and
{\rm tr}(A)=1
.
In particular, the set of singular points can be descomposed as
\Sigma=\cup_{m=0}^{n-1}\Sigma_{m}
, where

\Sigma_{m}:=\big\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma\,:\,{\rm dim}(\{p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=0\})=m\big\},\qquad m=0,\ldots,n-1.

Moreover, for each
m
, the set
\Sigma_{m}\cap\{t=t_{\circ}\}
can be covered by a
C^{1}
manifold of dimension
m
.
Unfortunately, the previous expansion implies only
C^{1/2}
regularity in time for the covering manifolds.
In particular, because of this, (
1.3
) implies a non-sharp bound on the parabolic Hausdorff dimension of
\Sigma_{m}
.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, our strategy is to refine (
1.3
) in the time variable, by developing a parabolic version of
[
FS17
]
.
This is the content of the first part of the paper.
A key tool for this is a truncated version of the parabolic frequency function

\phi(r,w):=\frac{r^{2}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}|\nabla w|^{2}G\,dx}{\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}w^{2}G\,dx},

where
G
is the time-reversed heat kernel.
We will see that (a truncated version of)
\phi(r,w)
is essentially monotone in
r
for

w=u(x_{\circ}+\cdot,t_{\circ}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}.

Thanks to this fact, assuming with no loss of generality that
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=(0,0)
, we can prove that

(1.4)

\frac{(u-p_{2})(rx,r^{2}t)}{\|u-p_{2}\|_{r}}\longrightarrow q(x,t)\qquad\text{as }r\to 0

along subsequences, where
q
is a parabolically homogeneous function.

We then show the following:

(i)

If
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{m}
with
m\leq n-2
, then the function
q
is a
quadratic
caloric polynomial.
This means that the expansion (
1.3
) cannot be improved at these points! Hence, to get an improved dimensional bound on
\Sigma_{m}

we employ a barrier argument in the spirit of
[
FRS20
]
. More precisely, since
m\leq n-2
, we can exploit the fact that
\Sigma_{m}
has zero capacity to build a refined barrier and show that

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma_{m})\leq m,\qquad 0\leq m\leq n-2.

(ii)

If
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}
, then
q
is a homogeneous solution of the parabolic thin obstacle problem.
We denote by
\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
the subset at which the homogeneity is less than
3
.

(a)

If
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
, we show that
\partial_{t}q\not\equiv 0
and that
q
is
convex
in all directions that are tangential to
\{p_{2}=0\}
.
This allows us to perform a dimension reduction that, combined with a barrier argument, implies

{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3})\leq n-2.

(b)

If
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}\setminus\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
we show that
q
is always 3-homogeneous, hence

(1.5)

u(x_{\circ}+\cdot,t_{\circ}+\cdot)=p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}+O(|x|^{3}+|t|^{3/2}),

and the same barrier argument as in (ii)-(a) implies that

{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma_{n-1}\setminus\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3})\leq n-1.

Combining these estimates, Theorem
1.1
follows.

We wish to remark that, although these proofs require a series of delicate new estimates, the strategy behind this first result is a generalization of the ideas in
[
FS17
,
FRS20
]
to the parabolic setting.
However, to prove Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
, completely new ideas are needed.

Indeed, in the elliptic setting one, can show that, outside a small set of dimension
n-2
, the “second blow-up”
q
is a cubic harmonic polynomial and the frequency function is still (almost) monotone for the function
w=u-p_{2}-q
.
This fact was crucially used in
[
FRS20
]
and it allowed us to prove that, in the elliptic setting, an expansion of the form

u=p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}+o(|x|^{5-\varepsilon})

holds, up to a set of dimension
n-2
.

Unfortunately, these methods completely break down in the parabolic setting, as the “second blow-up”
q(x,t)
is
never
a cubic caloric polynomial (as we shall see, it is typically of the form
t|x_{n}|+\frac{1}{6}|x_{n}|^{3}
), and the frequency function
\phi(r,w)
is
never (almost) monotone
for
w=u-p_{2}-q
.
4
4

4

This issue is already present in dimension
1
, and it can be understood as follows: since
u\sim\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}+t|x_{n}|+\frac{1}{6}|x_{n}|^{3}
, in a parabolic cylinder
\mathscr{C}_{r}:=B_{r}\times[-r^{2},0]
the set
\{u=0\}
has volume
r^{n+3}
(roughly, it behaves as the set
\mathscr{C}_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|\lesssim|t|\}
). This error becomes critical when considering a frequency formula of order at least 3, and a completely different strategy needs to be found.

Thus, if we want to improve (
1.5
), we need to develop completely new methods.

One of the main difficulties in the present paper is actually to pass from a cubic expansion (
1.5
) to an expansion of order
3+\beta
for some
\beta>0
. More precisely, the goal is to prove that

(1.6)

u(x,t)={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}x_{n}^{2}+a|x_{n}|(t+{\textstyle\frac{1}{6}}x_{n}^{2})+p_{3}^{odd}(x,t)+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{3+\beta}\big),

for some
a>0
and
p_{3}^{odd}
an odd cubic caloric polynomial. One of our key results here (which is probably the most delicate part of the whole paper) is that, up to a change of coordinates,
(
1.6
) holds up to an
(n-2)
-dimensional set.

This is a new paradigm of dimension reduction, where we do
not
have a frequency or similar powerful monotonicity formula, and it is based on barriers, maximum principle, compactness arguments, parabolic regularity estimates, non-homogeneous blow-ups, and new GMT covering arguments.
This is developed in the second part of the paper, see Sections
10
-
12
, and in particular Propositions
11.3
and
12.2
.

Once we have proven (
1.6
), we need to push the expansion to higher order.
For this, we develop a barrier argument to show that the set
\{u>0\}
splits into two separate connected components inside the set
\Omega^{\beta}:=\{|x|^{2+\beta}<-t\}
.

Note that,
under the parabolic scaling
(x,t)\to(rx,r^{2}t)
, the set
\Omega^{\beta}
converges to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)
as
r\to 0.
In other words, we have “broken the parabolic scaling”.
This means that we may expect the solution
u
to behave, inside
\Omega^{\beta}
, as two (almost) independent solutions of the Stefan problem inside each connected component of
\{u>0\}
.
In other words,
u
should behave as the sum of two solutions of the Stefan problem for which
(0,0)
is a regular point!

The last step, which is carried out in the third part of the paper, is to prove a
C^{\infty}
regularity result near
regular
points, which is robust enough to be applied in our setting.
More precisely, we want to show that if
\bar{u}
is a solution of the Stefan problem such that
\{\bar{u}=0\}
is sufficiently close to
\{x_{n}\leq 0\}
inside
\Omega^{\beta}
, then we have a
C^{\infty}
expansion for
\bar{u}
at
(0,0)
.
We then apply this result to our solution
u
restricted to each connected component of
\{u>0\}\cap\Omega^{\beta}
, and we obtain a
C^{\infty}
-type regularity for
u
.

As a corollary of this
C^{\infty}
expansion we are able to prove that, outside a
(n-2)
-dimensional set, if
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
and
(x_{1},t_{1})
are singular points then

|t_{\circ}-t_{1}|=o(|x_{\circ}-x_{1}|^{k})\quad\textrm{for every}\ k\gg 1.

This implies Theorem
1.3
, and finally Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.4
follow as immediate consequences.

Remark 1.5
.

In view of our proof strategy, the dimensional bound
1/2
in Theorem
1.2
for the set of singular times in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
critical
in at least two ways.

On the one hand, in the lower strata
\Sigma_{m}
with
m\leq n-2
, both (
1.3
) and the bound
{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma_{m})\leq m
are optimal.
In particular, in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the projection of
\Sigma_{1}
on the
t
axis has dimension at most
1/2
, and it looks unlikely to us that one can improve this bound.

On the other hand, the dimension bound
1/2
is
also
critical when we look at
\Sigma_{2}
.
Indeed, the set of points at which (
1.6
) does
not
hold has parabolic dimension at most
n-2(=1)
. So, also for this set, its projection on the
t
axis is expected to have dimension
1/2
.
Whether this bound can be improved is very unclear to us, and if possible, it would require a completely new understanding of this type of points.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
we introduce some notation that will be used throughout the paper.
Then, in Section
3
, we first recall some classic results on the Stefan problem and then establish some new basic properties of solutions.
In Sections
4
and
5
we introduce some new parabolic functionals and monotonicity formulas of Weiss and Almgren-type.
This allows us, in Section
6
, to consider the second blow-up at singular points (see (
1.4
)), and then prove in Section
7
that this blow-up is 3-homogeneous at “most” singular points.
In Section
8
we construct some appropriate barriers, adapted to each type of singular point, which allow us to prove Theorem
1.1
.
We then study cubic blow-ups in Section
9
.
As explained before, a key and difficult part in the proof of our results is to pass from the cubic blow-ups to an estimate of order
3+\beta
, with
\beta>0
(see (
1.6
)).
This is accomplished in Sections
10
,
11
, and
12
.
Finally, we show in Section
13
that this implies a
C^{\infty}
estimate at “most” singular points, and in Section
14
we finally give the proof of Theorem
1.3
and its consequences.

2.
Notation used throughout the paper

In this section we introduce the notation and the mathematical objects that will be used consistently throughout the paper. In particular, the letters
G
and
\zeta
always refer in the sequel to the Gaussian and the spatial cut-off introduced below, and — since they appear often in the paper — we will not recall this every time.

2.1.
Operators

We define the following useful operators that will be used in all the paper.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{\small\bf H}:=(\Delta-\partial_{t})&&(\mbox{that is, }\text{\small\bf H}f:=\Delta f-\partial_{t}f)\\
Z:=(x\cdot\nabla+2t\partial_{t})&&(\mbox{that is, }Zf:=x\cdot\nabla f+2t\partial_{t}f).\end{array}

2.2.
Gaussian kernel

We introduce the “reversed heat kernel” in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)
:

G(x,t):=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}\right).

2.3.
A bilinear form

Given
r\in(0,1]
, we denote by
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{r}
the following bilinear form defined for pairs of functions
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,0)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
:

\langle f,g\rangle_{r}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(f\cdot g)(x,-r^{2})\,G(x,-r^{2})\,dx.

In the special case
r=1
, we sometimes use the notation
\langle\,,\,\rangle:=\langle\,,\,\rangle_{1}

2.4.
Functionals
D
and
H

Throughout the paper we shall use the following dimensionless quantities defined for all functions
w:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,0)\to\mathbb{R}
sufficiently regular (in our application,
w
will be at least
C^{1,1}
in space and
C^{0,1}
in time):

D(r,w):=2r^{2}\langle\nabla w,\nabla w\rangle_{r}=2r^{2}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}|\nabla w|^{2}G,\qquad H(r,w):=\langle w,w\rangle_{r}=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}w^{2}G.

2.5.
Parabolic rescaling

We introduce here a useful notation for parabolic rescaling and normalization.
Given a function
w:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,0)\to\mathbb{R}
, for
r>0
we define
w_{r}
and
\tilde{w}_{r}
as

(2.1)

w_{r}(x,t):=w(rx,r^{2}t)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\tilde{w}_{r}(x):=\frac{w_{r}}{H(1,w_{r})^{\frac{1}{2}}}=\frac{w(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)}{H(r,w)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Note that
H(1,\tilde{w}_{r})=1
.
Also, the following commutation properties of the parabolic rescaling with
H
and
Z
hold:

\text{\small\bf H}(f_{r})=r^{2}(\text{\small\bf H}f)_{r}\quad\mbox{and}\quad Z(f_{r})=(Zf)_{r}.

Furthermore,

\langle f,g\rangle_{r}=\langle f_{r},g_{r}\rangle,\qquad\mbox{where }\langle\,,\,\rangle=\langle\,,\,\rangle_{1}.

2.6.
Homogeneous functions

We say that a function
f(x,t)
is (parabolically)
\lambda
-homogeneous whenever
f(rx,r^{2}t)=r^{\lambda}f(x,t)
for all
r>0
. Equivalently,
f
is
\lambda
-homogeneous whenever
Zf=\lambda f
.

2.7.
Spatial cut-off

Throughout the paper, we will save the letter
\zeta
to denote a spatial cut-off as follows:

(2.2)

\mbox{ $\zeta=\zeta(x)\quad$ with $\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{1/2})$, \ $\zeta\geq 0$, \ and \ $\zeta\equiv 1$ in $B_{1/4}$}.

This cut-off function will be crucial, since the functionals
D
and
H
that appear in monotonicity formulae need functions defined in the whole space. Hence, we shall multiply our solution and its derivatives (which are defined only in a bounded domain) by
\zeta
in order to define functions defined in the whole space (of course, by doing so we will introduce errors in the equation but these will be exponentially small).

2.8.
Parabolic cylinders

Given
r>0
, we define the parabolic cylinder
\mathscr{C}_{r}
as

\mathscr{C}_{r}:=B_{r}\times(-r^{2},0).

2.9.
Spatial projection and projection onto time axis

We use the notation
\pi_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\pi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to refer to the canonical projections:

(2.3)

\pi_{x}(x,t):=x\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\pi_{t}(x,t):=t.

3.
Classical and new facts about solutions to the Stefan problem

It is well known, by the results of Caffarelli
[
Caf77
]
, that solutions to the Stefan problem are
C^{1,1}
in space and
C^{0,1}
in time.
So, we consider here
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
a solution of the parabolic obstacle problem

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\text{\small\bf H}u=\chi_{\{u>0\}}\\
u\geq 0\\
\partial_{t}u>0\quad\mbox{in }\{u>0\}\end{cases}\qquad\textrm{in}\quad B_{1}\times(-1,1).

Note that, since
u
is nonnegative, (
3.1
) implies that
u
is nondecreasing in time inside
B_{1}\times(-1,1)
.

By Caffarelli
[
Caf77
]
, any solution
u
of (
3.1
) with
(0,0)\in\partial\{u=0\}
satisfies

(3.2)

\sup_{B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,0)}|D^{2}u|+|\partial_{t}u|\leq C\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sup_{B_{r}(0)}u(\,\cdot\,,0)\geq cr^{2}\quad\forall\,r\in(0,1),

where
C,c
are positive dimensional constants.

Notice that, if
u
is a solution of (
3.1
), then

u^{x_{\circ},t_{\circ},r}:=r^{-2}u(x_{\circ}+r\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+r^{2}\,\cdot\,)

is also a solution (in the corresponding rescaled and translated domain).

The classical theory defines the following two type of points on the
free boundary

\partial\{u>0\}
:

•

Regular points
:
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\partial\{u>0\}
is a
regular point
if

\lim_{r\to 0}u^{x_{\circ},t_{\circ},r}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\max\{0,\boldsymbol{e}\cdot x\}\big)^{2}

for some
\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.

•

Singular points
:
x_{\circ}\in\partial\{u>0\}
is a
singular point
if

\lim_{r\to 0}u^{x_{\circ},t_{\circ},r}(t,x)\to p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x),

where
p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
is a convex quadratic
2
-homogeneous polynomial which does not depend on time.

When
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=(0,0)
is a singular point, we simplify the notation by writing
p_{2}
instead of
p_{2,0,0}
.
Throughout the paper we will denote

\Sigma:=\{\mbox{singular points in }B_{1}\times(-1,1)\}\subset\partial\{u>0\}.

The following result follows from
[
Caf77
]
, from
[
KN77
]
for the higher regularity near regular points, and from
[
Bla06
]
for the uniqueness of blow-up at singular points:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
).
Then, every free boundary point is either regular or singular.
Moreover, the set of regular points is relatively open inside the free boundary, and it is a
C^{\infty}

(n-1)
-dimensional manifold inside
B_{1}\times(-1,1)
.

As a consequence of the parabolic Monneau-type monotonicity formula proved in
[
Bla06
,
LM15
]
, we have the following result for the singular set.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
), and let
\Sigma\subset B_{1}\times(-1,1)
denote the set of singular points.
Then,
\pi_{x}(\Sigma)\subset B_{1}
can be locally covered by a
(n-1)
-dimensional
C^{1}
manifold.

Proof.

This follows from the more general result of
C^{1}
regularity in space and
C^{0,1/2}
in time given in
[
LM15
, Theorem 1.9]
.
∎

We will also need the following estimate, whose proof is contained in
[
Caf77
,
CF79
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
), and let
(0,0)
be a free boundary point.
Then

|\partial_{t}u|+(D^{2}u)_{-}\leq C|\log r|^{-\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{ in }B_{r}\times(-r^{2},r^{2}),

where
C,\varepsilon>0
depend only on
n
.

After this summary of some well-known properties, we now prove some new results that will be crucial in our analysis.
We begin with the following local semiconvexity property in time.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
). Then,

\partial_{tt}u\geq-C\quad\mbox{ in }B_{1/2}\times(-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}),

where
C
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

To prove the proposition above, we need the parabolic version of the standard
L^{1}
to
L^{1}_{weak}
estimate of Calderón and Zygmund for elliptic PDE
[
Jon64
]
:

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
w:\mathscr{C}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
be any solution of
\text{\small\bf H}w=f
in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, with
f\in L^{1}
. Then

\sup_{\theta>0}\theta\big|\{|D^{2}w|+|\partial_{t}w|>\theta\}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1/2}\big|\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|w\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}),

for some constant
C
depending only on
n
.

We will also need the following result (see e.g.
[
Wa92
, Theorem 4.16]
):

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
w:\mathscr{C}_{1}\to R
satisfy
\text{\small\bf H}w\leq 0
. Then

\sup_{\mathscr{C}_{1/2}}w\leq C\bigg(\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}(w_{+})^{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}},

for some
\varepsilon>0
and
C
depending only on
n
.

We can now prove the desired semiconvexity in time:

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Fix
r_{1},r_{2}
such that
1/2<r_{1}<r_{2}<1
, and for
r\in(0,1)
define the domains
\Omega_{r}:=B_{r}\times(-r,r)
.
For
h\in\mathbb{R}
and given a function
w
, we define the discrete time derivative as

\delta_{t,h}w(x,t):=h^{-1}\big(w(x,t+h)-w(x)\big)
.

Now, consider
v^{h}:=\delta_{t,h}\partial_{t}u
. By Proposition
3.3
the function
v^{h}
is continuous.
Also, by (
3.1
),
v^{h}\geq 0
on
\{u=0\}
.
Notice in addition that
\text{\small\bf H}v^{h}\leq 0
in
\{u>0\}
. Indeed, inside the open set
\{u>0\}
we have
\text{\small\bf H}(\partial_{t}\delta_{t,h}u)=0
and hence we have (in the sense of distributions)

\text{\small\bf H}(\partial_{t}\delta_{t,h}u)(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)=\frac{\partial_{t}(\chi_{\{u>0\}})(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,+h)}{h}\geq 0,

where the right hand side is a nonnegative measure since
\partial_{t}(\chi_{\{u>0\}})
is a nonnegative measure by the monotonicity property of
u
.

This proves that
(v^{h})_{-}
is subcaloric, and using Lemma
3.6
we get

{\rm ess\,sup}_{\Omega_{1/2}}(v^{h})_{-}\ \leq\ \sup_{\theta>0}\theta\big|\{(v^{h})_{-}>\theta\}\cap\Omega_{r_{1}}\big|=C\sup_{\theta>0}\theta|\{|\partial_{t}\hat{w}|>\theta\}\cap\Omega_{r_{1}}\big|,\quad\mbox{ where }\hat{w}:=\delta_{t,h}u.

Note that the function
\hat{w}
satisfies
\text{\small\bf H}\hat{w}=\delta_{t,h}(\chi_{\{u>0\}})\geq 0
and
\hat{w}\geq 0
, since
u
and
\{u>0\}
are nondecreasing in time.
Moreover, by the parabolic Calderón-Zygmund type estimate (Theorem
3.5
), we have

\sup_{\theta>0}\theta|\{|\partial_{t}\hat{w}|>\theta\}\cap\Omega_{r_{1}}\big|\leq C(\|\text{\small\bf H}\hat{w}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{r_{2}})}+\|\hat{w}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{r_{2}})}).

Now, fix
\xi=\xi(x,t)
a smooth nonnegative cut-off, compactly supported in
\Omega_{1}
and such that
\xi\equiv 1
in
\Omega_{r_{2}}
.
For
h\ll(1-r_{2})
, we have

\|\text{\small\bf H}\hat{w}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{r_{2}})}\leq\int_{\Omega_{1}}\text{\small\bf H}\hat{w}\,\xi=\int_{\Omega_{1}}(\text{\small\bf H}\delta_{t,h}u)\,\xi=\int_{\Omega_{1}}u\,\delta_{t,-h}(\Delta\xi+\partial_{t}\xi)\leq C\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})},

and similarly

\|\hat{w}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{r_{2}})}\leq\int_{\Omega_{1}}\hat{w}\,\xi=\int_{\Omega_{1}}\delta_{t,h}u\,\xi=\int_{\Omega_{1}}u\,\delta_{t,-h}\xi\leq C\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})}.

Hence, combining all these estimates together, we have shown that

{\rm ess\,sup}_{\Omega_{1/2}}(v^{h})_{-}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})}.

Letting
h\downarrow 0
, we conclude the proof.
∎

We will also need the following relation between the derivatives of
u
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
). Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(D^{2}u)_{-}\leq C\,\partial_{t}u\quad\mbox{ in }B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2).

Proof.

Given
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)
, and fixed
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we consider

w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}:=\partial_{t}u+c_{1}\left(\textstyle\frac{|x-x_{\circ}|^{2}}{4n}-\frac{(t-t_{\circ})}{2}-u\right)-c_{2}(\partial_{ee}u)_{-},

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are small positive constants to be chosen.
Note that
w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
is nonnegative on
\partial\{u>0\}
.

Let
\Omega:=B_{1/4}(x_{\circ})\times(t_{\circ}-1/4,t_{\circ})
and denote by
\partial_{par}\Omega
its parabolic boundary.
Since
\partial_{t}u>0
in
\{u>0\}
, it follows by the first bound in (
3.2
) that

w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq c_{1}\big({\textstyle\frac{1}{64n}}-C_{0}\varrho^{2}\big)-c_{2}C_{0}\quad\mbox{in }\partial_{par}\Omega\cap\big(\{u=0\}+(B_{\rho}\times(0,\rho^{2}))\big)

where
C_{0}
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and
\{u=0\}+(B_{\rho}\times(0,\rho^{2}))
denotes the Minkowski sum of sets.
Hence, choosing
\varrho>0
sufficiently small and
c_{1}\geq CC_{0}c_{2}
, we will have
w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}>0
on this piece of boundary.

On the other hand, since by assumption
\partial_{t}u>0
in
\{u>0\}
, we have

w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq\partial_{t}u-C(c_{1}+c_{2})\geq c_{u}-C(c_{1}+c_{2})\quad\mbox{in }\Omega\setminus\big(\{u=0\}+(B_{\rho}\times(0,\rho^{2}))\big),

where
c_{u}>0
is a constant (depending on
u
).

Hence, choosing
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
sufficiently small so that
c_{u}-C(c_{1}+c_{2})>0
and
c_{1}\geq CC_{0}c_{2}
, it follows by the maximum principle that

w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0\quad\mbox{in }\Omega.

In particular, evaluating at
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
we obtain

\partial_{t}u(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})-c_{1}u(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})-c_{2}(\partial_{ee}u)_{-}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0.

Since
u(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\geq 0
, and both
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in B_{1/2}\times(-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})
and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
can be chosen arbitrarily, the lemma follows.
∎

Finally, we shall also need the following result.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
). There exists a dimensional constant
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
such that the following holds: Assume that

\big|u-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(x\cdot e)^{2}\big|\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial_{ee}u\geq-\varepsilon_{\circ}

for some
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Then

\partial_{ee}u\geq 0\quad\mbox{in }B_{1/2}\times(-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}).

Proof.

We may assume
e=\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
. Write
x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
set
v^{h}(x^{\prime},x_{n},t):=h^{-2}\big(u(x^{\prime},x_{n}+h,t)+u(x^{\prime},x_{n}-h,t)-2u(x^{\prime},x_{n},t)\big)
.
We note that

\text{\small\bf H}v^{h}\leq 0\quad\mbox{in }\{u>0\}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad v^{h}\geq 0\quad\mbox{in }\{u=0\}.

Now, fix
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)
and consider the function

w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}:=v^{h}+256\,n\,\varepsilon_{\circ}\left(\textstyle\frac{|x-x_{\circ}|^{2}}{4n}-\frac{(t-t_{\circ})}{2}-u\right)\quad\text{inside }U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}:=\{|x-x_{\circ}|\leq 1/4,-1/4\leq t-t_{\circ}\leq 0\}.

Note that
w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
is supercaloric in
U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cap\{u>0\}
.
Therefore, if we show that —for
\varepsilon_{\circ}
small—
w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
is nonnegative on
\partial_{par}U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cup(U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cap\{u=0\})
, then it will follow by the maximum principle that
w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0
inside
U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
, and in particular

v^{h}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\geq w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\geq 0.

Since
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)
is arbitrary, and
v^{h}\to\partial_{nn}u
as
h\downarrow 0
, the lemma will follow.
So, we are left with proving that
w_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0
on
\partial_{par}U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cup(U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cap\{u=0\})

Since
v^{h}\geq 0
on
\{u=0\}
, it follows that
w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0
on
U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\cap\{u=0\})
.
Hence, it only remains to show that
w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0
on
\partial_{par}U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
—provided
\varepsilon_{\circ}
is chosen sufficiently small.
Note that, on
\partial_{par}U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
, we have
\frac{|x-x_{\circ}|^{2}}{4n}-\frac{(t-t_{\circ})}{2}>\frac{1}{64n}
.
Let us divide the parabolic boundary in two pieces: the piece (I) where
\{u\leq\frac{1}{128n}\}
, and (II) where
\{u\geq\frac{1}{128n}\}
.
On the piece (I), by the assumption
\partial_{nn}u\geq-\varepsilon_{\circ}
we obtain

w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=v^{h}+256\,n\,\varepsilon_{\circ}\left(\textstyle\frac{|x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime}|^{2}}{4(n-1)}-\frac{(t-t_{\circ})}{2}-u\right)\geq-2\varepsilon_{\circ}+256\,n\,\varepsilon_{\circ}\left(\frac{1}{64n}-\frac{1}{128n}\right)\geq 0.

To estimate
w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
on the piece (II) we note that, since
\big|u-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}x_{n}^{2}\big|\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}
, it follows that

\{u>\frac{1}{128n}\}\subset\left\{|x_{n}|>\frac{1}{10n^{1/2}}\right\},\qquad\{u>0\}\supset\left\{|x_{n}|>\frac{1}{20n^{1/2}}\right\},\qquad\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})}\leq 1,

provided
\varepsilon_{\circ}
is taken sufficiently small.
Thus, using standard interior regularity for the heat equation and interpolation
5
5

5

Note that
u-\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
is caloric inside
\left\{|x_{n}|>\frac{1}{20n^{1/2}}\right\}
and it has small
L^{\infty}
norm.
we obtain that
|\partial_{nn}(u-\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2})|\leq C_{n}\varepsilon_{\circ}
inside
\left\{|x_{n}|>\frac{1}{10n^{1/2}}\right\}
.
In particular
v^{h}\geq\frac{1}{2}
in
\left\{|x_{n}|>\frac{1}{10n^{1/2}}\right\}
for
\varepsilon_{\circ}
sufficiently small.
Therefore, recalling that
|u|\leq 1
inside
U_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
,
it follows that

w^{h}_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=v^{h}+256\,n\,\varepsilon_{\circ}\left(\textstyle\frac{|x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime}|^{2}}{4(n-1)}-\frac{(t-t_{\circ})}{2}-u\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}-C_{n}\varepsilon_{\circ}\geq 0\qquad\text{on (II)},

provided we choose
\varepsilon_{\circ}
small.
∎

4.
Parabolic functionals and useful formulae

In this section we establish formulae which are valid for arbitrary functions
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
having
C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
regularity.
Later on we will apply these formulae for example to
w(x,t)=(u-p_{2})(x,t)\zeta(x)
.
Recall that
Z
,
G
,
H
,
D
, and
\zeta
, were defined in Section
2
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
f\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
. Then

\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}\langle f,G\rangle_{r}=\langle Zf,G\rangle.

Proof.

Recall that

\langle f,G\rangle_{r}=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}fG,\qquad\langle Zf,G\rangle=\int_{\{t=-1\}}Zf\,G,\qquad Z=x\cdot\nabla+2t\cdot\partial_{t}.

Also, thanks to the scaling property
G(rx,r^{2}t)r^{n}=G(x,t)
, we have

\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}f(x,t)G(x,t)dx=\int_{\{t=-1\}}f(rx,rt)G(rx,r^{2}t)r^{n}\,dx=\int_{\{t=-1\}}f(rx,r^{2}t)G(x,t)\,dx.

Then, the result follows by differentiating with respect to
r
the expression above, and evaluating at
r=1
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
f,g\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
.
Then

(4.1)

2\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle=\langle Zf,g\rangle-2\langle\text{\small\bf H}f,g\rangle=\langle f,Zg\rangle-2\langle f,\text{\small\bf H}g\rangle.

Proof.

Noting that
\nabla G=-\frac{x}{2}G
and
Z=x\cdot\nabla-2\cdot\partial_{t}
on
\{t=-1\}
, using integration by parts we obtain

\begin{split}2\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle&=2\int_{\{t=-1\}}\nabla f\cdot\nabla g\,G=-2\int_{\{t=-1\}}f{\rm div}(\nabla g\,G)\\
&=-2\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big(f\Delta g\,G+f\nabla g\cdot\nabla G\big)=-\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big(2f\Delta g\,G-fx\cdot\nabla g\,G\big)\\
&=-\int_{\{t=-1\}}f\big(2\Delta g-2\partial_{t}g-(-2\partial_{t}g+x\cdot\nabla g)\big)G=-\int_{\{t=-1\}}f\big(2\text{\small\bf H}g-Zg\big)G\\
&=-2\langle f,\text{\small\bf H}g\rangle+\langle f,Zg\rangle.\end{split}

By symmetry, also the other identity
2\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle=-2\langle\text{\small\bf H}f,g\rangle+\langle Zf,g\rangle
holds.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
w\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
.
Then

(4.2)

H^{\prime}(1,w)=2\langle w,Zw\rangle=2D(1,w)+4\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle.

Proof.

The first equality is an immediate application of Lemma
4.1
with
f=w^{2}
.
To prove the second equality, we use (
4.1
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

As a consequence of Lemma
4.3
we obtain the following identity:

(4.3)

D(r,w)=\langle w,Zw\rangle-2\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
w\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
. Then

D^{\prime}(1,w)=2\langle Zw,Zw\rangle-4\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.1
with
f=|\nabla w|^{2}
we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}D(r,w)\bigg|_{r=1}&=\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}2\bigg(r^{2}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}|\nabla w|^{2}G\bigg)=\sum_{i}4\langle Z\partial_{i}w,\partial_{i}w\rangle+2D(1,w)\\
&=\sum_{i}4\langle Z\partial_{i}w+\partial_{i}w,\partial_{i}w\rangle=\sum_{i}4\langle\partial_{i}Zw,\partial_{i}w\rangle=4\langle\nabla Zw,\nabla w\rangle\\
&=2\langle Zw,Zw\rangle-4\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle,\end{split}

where the last equality follows from Lemma
4.2
.
∎

We now introduce the frequency functions

\phi(r,w):=\frac{D(r,w)}{H(r,w)}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\phi^{\gamma}(r,w):=\frac{D(r,w)+\gamma r^{2\gamma}}{H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}}.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
w\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
. Then

\frac{d}{dr}\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)\geq\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle_{r}\langle w,w\rangle_{r}-\langle w,Zw\rangle_{r}^{2}\big)+\big(2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big)^{2}+E^{\gamma}(r,w)}{\big(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}},

where

(4.4)

E^{\gamma}(r,w):=2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big(D(r,w)+\gamma r^{2\gamma}\big)-2r^{2}\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\big).

Proof.

By scaling it is enough to compute, for
a>0
,

\frac{d}{dr}\phi^{\gamma,a}(r,w)\bigg|_{r=1}\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\phi^{\gamma,a}(r,w):=\frac{D(r,w)+\gamma(ar)^{2\gamma}}{H(r,w)+(ar)^{2\gamma}}.

By Lemmas
4.1
,
4.3
, and
4.5
, and by (
4.3
), we have

A_{1}:=\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}\big(D(r,w)+\gamma(ar)^{2\gamma}\big)=2\langle Zw,Zw\rangle-4\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle+2\gamma^{2}a^{2\gamma},

D(1,w)+\gamma a^{2\gamma}=\langle w,Zw\rangle-2\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle+\gamma a^{2\gamma},

A_{2}:=\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}\big(H(r,w)+(ar)^{2\gamma}\big)=2\langle w,Zw\rangle+2\gamma a^{2\gamma},

H(1,w)+(ar)^{2\gamma}=\langle w,w\rangle+a^{2\gamma}.

Therefore,

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}\phi^{\gamma,a}(r,w)&=\frac{A_{1}\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)-A_{2}\big(D(1,w)+\gamma a^{2\gamma}\big)}{\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}}\\
&=2\,\frac{X^{2}+2\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big(\langle Zw,w\rangle+\gamma a^{2\gamma}\big)-2\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big(\langle w,w\rangle+a^{2\gamma}\big)}{\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}X^{2}:=&\,\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle+\gamma^{2}a^{2\gamma}\big)\big(\langle w,w\rangle+a^{2\gamma}\big)-\big(\langle w,Zw\rangle+\gamma a^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}\\
\geq&\,\langle Zw,Zw\rangle\langle w,w\rangle-\langle w,Zw\rangle^{2}\geq 0.\end{split}

Using again (
4.3
), this gives

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\bigg|_{r=1}\phi^{\gamma,a}(r,w)&\geq 2\,\frac{\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle\langle w,w\rangle-\langle w,Zw\rangle^{2}\big)+4\big(\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big)^{2}}{\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}}\\
&\qquad+2\,\frac{2\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big(D(1,w)+\gamma a^{2\gamma}\big)-2\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)}{\big(H(1,w)+a^{2\gamma}\big)^{2}}.\end{split}

Applying the previous equality with
w
replaced by
w_{r}=w(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)
and
a
replaced by
r
, the lemma follows.
∎

5.
Monotonicity formulae for the Stefan problem

Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the set of nonnegative
2
-homogeneous polynomials in the
x
variable —i.e.
p=p(x)
— which satisfy
\Delta p=1
.
In particular, such polynomials always satisfy the same equation as the parabolic obstacle problem.

Remark 5.1
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}
.
Then, since
\text{\small\bf H}u=\chi_{\{u>0\}}
and
\text{\small\bf H}p\equiv\Delta p\equiv 1
, we have

(5.1)

(u-p)\text{\small\bf H}(u-p)=p\chi_{\{u=0\}}\geq 0,\qquad Z(u-p)\,\text{\small\bf H}(u-p)=Zp\,\chi_{\{u=0\}}=2p\,\chi_{\{u=0\}}\geq 0.

Note that the solution
u(x,t)
that we are considering is defined only for
x\in B_{1}
. However the formulae given in Section
4
are for function
w
defined for all
x
all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Morally, we would like to apply the formulae in Section
4
with
w
replaced by
u-p
. Since this is not possible, we will instead use make use of the cut-off function

\zeta
in (
2.2
) and set
w:=(u-p)\zeta
, with
w=0
outside
B_{1/2}
.
However, such
w
is not a exact solution and errors coming from this truncation need to be taken into account.

Note that if
u
is a solution of the parabolic obstacle problem in
\mathscr{C}_{1}=B_{1}\times(-1,0)
and
p\in\mathcal{P}
, then by Remark
5.1
we have
6
6

6

From now on, given a function
v:\mathscr{C}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
, the product
\zeta\,v
will always be seen as a function defined in all
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,0)
, where we extended the product by
0
outside of
B_{1}
.

(5.2)

\langle\zeta\,Z(u-p),\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}(u-p)\rangle_{r}=2\langle\zeta\,(u-p),\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}(u-p)\rangle_{r}\geq 0.

The following lemma will be useful to control truncation errors, which are exponentially small as
r\downarrow 0
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
v\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{1})
. Then, for all
r\in(0,1/2)
we have

\big|\langle\zeta\,v,\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\rangle_{r}-\langle\zeta\,v,\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{r}\big|+\big|\langle Z(\zeta v),\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\rangle_{r}-\langle\zeta\,Zw,\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{r}\big|\leq CM_{v}^{2}\,{\textstyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{(8r)^{2}}\right)},

where

M_{v}:=\sup_{B_{1/2}\times(-1/2,0)}|v|+|\nabla v|+|\partial_{t}v|+|\Delta v|,

and
C
depends only on
\zeta
.

Proof.

We note that, since
\zeta=\zeta(x)
,

\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)=\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}v+\Delta\zeta\,v+2\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla v\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z(\zeta v)=\zeta\,Zv+(x\cdot\nabla\zeta)v.

Thus

\begin{split}\langle\zeta\,v,\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\rangle_{r}=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\zeta\,v\,\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\,G=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\zeta\,v\,\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}v\,G+\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\zeta\,v\,(\Delta\zeta\,v+2\nabla v\cdot\nabla\zeta)\,G,\end{split}

and similarly

\begin{split}\langle Z(\zeta v),\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\rangle_{r}&=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}Z(\zeta v)\,\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta v)\,G\\
&=\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\zeta\,Zv\,\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}v\,G+\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\big(\zeta\,Zv(\Delta\zeta\,v+2\nabla v\cdot\nabla\zeta)+(x\cdot\nabla\zeta)\,v\,\text{\small\bf H}v\big)\,G.\end{split}

Since
\Delta\zeta
,
\nabla\zeta
, and
Z\zeta
vanish in
B_{1/4}
, and

G(x,-r^{2})=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}r^{n}}\exp\left({\textstyle-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4r^{2}}}\right)\leq C_{n}\exp\left({\textstyle-\frac{1}{(8r)^{2}}}\right)\quad\mbox{ for }|x|\geq\frac{1}{4},

the lemma follows.
∎

We next prove the following Weiss’ type monotonicity formula (as well as a useful consequence of it).

Lemma 5.3

(Weiss’-type formula)
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
a singular point. Given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, set
w:=(u-p)\zeta
. Let

W_{\lambda}(r,w):=r^{-2\lambda}\big(D(r,w)-\lambda H(r,w)\big).

Then:

(a)
For all
\lambda\geq 2

\hskip 71.13188pt\frac{d}{dr}W_{\lambda}(r,w)\geq 2r^{-2\lambda-1}\langle Zw-\lambda w,Zw-\lambda w\rangle_{r}^{2}-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}},\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}),

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
, and
\lambda
.

(b)
Moreover,
W_{2}(0^{+},w)=0
and

(5.3)

\hskip 71.13188ptD(r,w)-2H(r,w)\geq-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}},\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}),

where
C>0
depend only on
n
and
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
.

Proof.

As we shall see, (b) is a rather direct consequence of (a). We begin with the first part.

\bullet

Proof of (a).
To compute
W^{\prime}_{\lambda}(r,w)
we use Lemmas
4.3
and
4.5
, as well as (
4.3
) (rescaled), to obtain

\begin{split}W^{\prime}_{\lambda}(r,w)&=\frac{1}{r^{2\lambda}}\left(D^{\prime}(r,w)-\lambda H^{\prime}(r,w)\big)-\frac{2\lambda}{r}D(r,w)+\frac{2\lambda^{2}}{r}H(r,w)\right)\\
&=\frac{2}{r^{2\lambda+1}}\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle_{r}-2r^{2}\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}-\lambda\langle w,Zw\rangle_{r}-\lambda D(r,w)+\lambda^{2}H(r,w)\big)\\
&=\frac{2}{r^{2\lambda+1}}\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle-2r^{2}\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}-\lambda\langle w,Zw\rangle_{r}-\lambda(\langle w,Zw\rangle_{r}-2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r})+\lambda^{2}\langle w,w\rangle_{r}\big)\\
&=\frac{2}{r^{2\lambda+1}}\big(\langle Zw-\lambda w,Zw-\lambda w\rangle_{r}+2r^{2}\big\{\lambda\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle-r^{2}\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle\big\}\big).\end{split}

Set
\tilde{w}:=u-p_{2}
, so that
w=\zeta\tilde{w}.

Using (
5.2
) (see also Remark
5.1
) with
p=p_{2}
, and by Lemma
5.2
applied to
\tilde{w}
, we have

(5.4)

\lambda\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}-\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}=\lambda\langle\zeta\tilde{w},\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta\tilde{w})\rangle_{r}-\langle Z(\zeta\tilde{w}),\text{\small\bf H}(\zeta\tilde{w})\rangle_{r}\\
\geq\lambda\langle\zeta\tilde{w},\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}\tilde{w}\rangle_{r}-\langle\zeta\,Z\tilde{w},\text{\small\bf H}\tilde{w}\rangle_{r}-C_{\zeta}\big(M_{\tilde{w}}\big)^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{(8r)^{2}}}=(\lambda-2)\langle\zeta\tilde{w},\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}\tilde{w}\rangle_{r}-Ce^{-\frac{2}{r}},

where
M_{\tilde{w}}
is defined as in (
5.2
). Recalling (
3.2
) and that the cut-off
\zeta
is fixed, it follows that
M_{\tilde{w}}
(and therefore also the constant
C
above) depends only on
n
and
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
.
Since
\lambda\geq 2
,
-r^{-2\lambda+1}e^{-\frac{2}{r}}\geq-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}}
, and
\langle\zeta\tilde{w},\zeta\,\text{\small\bf H}\tilde{w}\rangle_{r}\geq 0
(see Remark
5.1
), the conclusion follows.

\bullet

Proof of (b).
Since
(0,0)
is a singular point we have that
w_{r}(x)=(u-p)(rx,r^{2}t)=(p_{2}-p)(rx)+o(r^{2})
, and therefore

W_{2}(0^{+},w)=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}W(1,r^{-2}w_{r})=W_{2}(1,p_{2}-p)=0.

As a consequence, (
5.3
) follows by integrating
(a)
with
\lambda=2
between
0
and
r
.
∎

We can now prove our approximate frequency formula.

Proposition 5.4

(Frequency formula)
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
a singular point. Given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, set
w:=(u-p)\zeta
.
Then, for all

\gamma\in(2,\infty)
we have

\frac{d}{dr}\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)\geq\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{\big(\langle Zw,Zw\rangle_{r}\langle w,w\rangle_{r}-\langle w,Zw\rangle_{r}^{2}\big)+\big(2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big)^{2}}{\bigl(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\bigr)^{2}}-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}},\quad\forall\,r\in(0,1),

and

r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\geq-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}},\quad\forall\,r\in(0,1),

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
, and
\gamma
.

Proof.

Since
-r^{-4\gamma}e^{-\frac{2}{r}}\geq-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}}
, thanks to Lemma
4.6
it suffices to show that
E^{\gamma}(r,w)\geq-Ce^{-\frac{2}{r}}
.
In particular, it is enough to prove that

\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big(D(r,w)+\gamma r^{2\gamma}\big)-\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\big)\geq-Ce^{-\frac{2}{r}}.

On the one hand, by Lemma
5.3
, for
\gamma>2
and
r
sufficiently small we have

0\leq\frac{1}{2}D(r,w)+\frac{\gamma}{2}r^{2\gamma}-H(r,w)-2r^{2\gamma}\leq C.

Thus, using (
5.2
) and Lemma
5.2
(as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
) we obtain

\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big(D(r,w)+\gamma r^{2\gamma}-2H(r,w)-2r^{2\gamma}\big)\geq-Ce^{-\frac{2}{r}}.

On the other hand, we similarly have
0\leq H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\leq C
, so by using (
5.2
) and Lemma
5.2
we obtain

\big(2\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}-\langle Zw,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}\big)\big(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}\big)\geq-Ce^{-\frac{2}{r}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

We now begin to discuss a series of consequences from the previous bounds.
The first observation is that, as an immediate consequence of (
5.3
), the following holds:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
is a singular point. Given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, set
w:=(u-p)\zeta
.
Then, for all
\gamma>2
, it holds
\phi^{\gamma}(0^{+},w)\geq 2
.

We also have the following:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
a singular point. Given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, set
w:=(u-p)\zeta
, and let
\gamma>2
.

(a)

We have

\frac{d}{dr}\frac{H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}}{r^{4}}\geq-Ce^{-\frac{1}{2r}}.

(b)

Assume
\phi^{\gamma}(0^{+},w)=\lambda\leq 3
, and that there exist
\delta>0
and
R\in(0,1)
such that
\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)\leq\lambda+\frac{\delta}{2}
for all
r\in(0,R)
. Then

c\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2\lambda}\leq\frac{H(R,w)+R^{2\gamma}}{H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}}\leq C_{\delta}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2\lambda+\delta},

where
c,C_{\delta}>0
depend only on
n
,
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
\gamma
, and (only in the case of
C_{\delta}
)
\delta
.

Proof.

It is a minor modification of the proof of
[
FRS20
, Lemma 4.1]
. We define

F(r):=\frac{2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}}{H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}},

and use that, by Proposition
5.4
,

\frac{d}{dr}\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)\geq\frac{2}{r}\big(F(r)\big)^{2}-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}}.

In particular, thanks to Lemma
5.5
, it follows that

(5.5)

\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)\geq 2-Ce^{-\frac{1}{r}}.

In particular, from Lemma
4.3
we have

(5.6)

\begin{split}\frac{\frac{d}{dr}(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma})}{(H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma})}&=\frac{2}{r}\,\frac{D(r,w)+\gamma r^{2\gamma}+2r^{2}\langle w,\text{\small\bf H}w\rangle_{r}}{H(r,w)+r^{2\gamma}}=\frac{2}{r}\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)+\frac{2}{r}F(r)\\
&\geq\frac{2}{r}\phi^{\gamma}(r,w)-\frac{C}{r}e^{-\frac{1}{r}}\geq\frac{4}{r}-Ce^{-\frac{1}{2r}},\end{split}

where the first inequality follows from Proposition
5.4
, while the second one from (
5.5
).

Then, (a) is a direct consequence of (
5.6
).
On the other hand, (b) follows exactly as in the proof of
[
FRS20
, Lemma 4.1]
, that is, integrating the first line of (
5.6
) and using the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality to control
\left|\int_{r}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho}F(\rho)d\rho\right|
.
∎

It is well known (see
[
Bla06
]
) that the singular set
\Sigma
can be split into the following sets:

(5.7)

\Sigma_{m}:=\big\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\mbox{ singular point with }{\rm dim}\big(\{p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=0\}\big)=m\big\}\,,\quad 0\leq m\leq n-1.

Recall that, as an immediate consequence of
[
LM15
, Theorem 1.9]
, we have the following.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}(B_{1}\times(-1,1))
solve (
3.1
).
Then
\pi_{x}(\Sigma_{m})\subset B_{1}
can be locally covered by an
m
-dimensional
C^{1}
manifold.

We next show the following:

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{m}
.
Given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, set
w:=(u-p_{2})\zeta
.

(a)

If
0\leq m\leq n-2
, then
\phi^{\gamma}(0^{+},w)=2
for all
\gamma>2
.

(b)

If
m=n-1
, then
\phi^{\gamma}(0^{+},w)\leq 3
for all
\gamma>3
.

Proof.

As we shall see, the proofs of the two results are very similar.

\bullet

Proof of (a).
By Lemma
5.6
(b) it is enough to show that if
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{m}
, with
m\leq n-2
, then for each
\epsilon>0
we have

(5.8)

\liminf_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}}|u(\cdot,-r^{2})-p_{2}|}{r^{2+\epsilon}}>0.

We will use a subsolution to show that, since
(0,0)
is a singular point ant
\partial_{t}u>0
outside of the contact set
\{u=0\}
, then

(5.9)

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\partial B_{r}}\partial_{t}u(x,t)\,dx\geq c_{\epsilon}r^{\epsilon}\qquad\forall\,t\in(-r^{2},0).

Then using that both
u
and
\partial_{t}u
are nonnegative, (
5.8
) follows immediately by integrating (
5.9
) with respect to
t
.

To show (
5.9
), set
L:=\{p_{2}=0\}
and define the “parabolic cone”

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}:=\left\{(x,t)\ :\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},t<0,\ {\rm dist}(x,L)\geq\delta(|x|+|t|^{1/2})\right\}.

Note that, since
r^{-2}u_{r}-p_{2}\to 0
locally uniformly, for all
\delta>0
there exists
r_{\delta}>0
such that

\mathcal{C}_{\delta/2}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\delta}}\subset\{u>0\}

(recall that
\mathscr{C}_{r_{\delta}}=B_{r_{\delta}}\times(-r_{\delta}^{2},0)
).
We now consider the following eigenvalue problem and make the following claim:

Claim:
For any
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta
such that the following holds: There exist

N\in(0,\epsilon)
and a positive

N
-homogenous function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)\to(0,+\infty)
(i.e.,
\Phi(rx,r^{2}t)=r^{N}\Phi(x,t)
for all
r>0
) satisfying

\text{\small\bf H}\Phi=0
in
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}

and

\text{\small\bf H}\Phi=0
on
\partial{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}}
.

To prove this, we look for
\Phi
of the form
\Phi(x,t)=t^{N/2}\phi(x/t^{1/2})
, where
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
solves the following eigenvalue problem for the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}}:=\{x_{n}\geq\delta(|x|+1)\}
:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{OU}\phi+\frac{N_{\delta}}{2}\phi=0\quad&\mbox{in }\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}}\\
\phi(x)=0&\mbox{on }\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}},\end{cases}

where

(5.10)

\mathcal{L}_{OU}\phi(x):=\Delta\phi(x)-\frac{x}{2}\cdot\nabla\phi(x)=e^{|x|^{2}/4}{\rm div}(e^{-|x|^{2}/4}\nabla\phi)

is the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator.

Note that, as
\delta\to 0
, the first eigenvalue
N_{\delta}
small is very close to the first eigenvalue
N_{0}
of
\mathcal{L}_{OU}
in the limiting domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus L
.
Also, since
{\rm dim}(L)\leq n-2
,
L
has zero harmonic capacity. Therefore, it follows by the Raleigh quotient characterization

(5.11)

\frac{N_{0}}{2}=\inf_{\phi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus L)}\frac{\int|\nabla\phi|^{2}\,e^{-|x|^{2}/4}}{\int\phi^{2}\,e^{-|x|^{2}/4}}

that
N_{0}=0
, hence
N_{\delta}
can be made arbitrarily small provided we choose
\delta
small enough. This proves the claim.

We now note that

\overline{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}}\cap\partial_{par}\mathscr{C}_{r_{\delta}}\subset\subset\{u>0\},

and that
\partial_{t}u
is positive and supercaloric inside
\{u>0\}
. Hence, we can use
\Phi
as lower barrier to show that

\partial_{t}u\geq c\Phi\qquad\mbox{in }\mathscr{C}_{r_{\delta}},

for some
c>0
, and (
5.9
) follows.

\bullet

Proof of (b).

This part is similar to the argument used for (a). However in this case, since
{\rm dim}(L)=n-1
,
L
is a set of positive harmonic capacity and therefore
N_{0}>0
. By Talenti’s rearrangement (with Gaussian weights), one can easily show that the infimum in (
5.11
) is attained by the function
|x_{n}|
, and thus
N_{0}=1
. Therefore, in this case we can find a
N
-homogeneous function
\Phi
as above, but with
N\leq 1+\epsilon
.
Using this
\Phi
as lower barrier for
\partial_{t}u
, we conclude that

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\partial B_{r}}|\partial_{t}u|(x,t)\,dx\geq c_{\epsilon}r^{1+\epsilon}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\liminf_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}}|u(\cdot,-r^{2})-p_{2}|}{r^{3+\epsilon}}>0

(cp. (
5.9
)-(
5.8
)), which leads to
\phi(0^{+},u-p_{2})\leq 3
.
∎

Choosing
\gamma>3,
as a direct consequence of Lemmas
5.8
and
5.6
we obtain the following:

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
a singular point. Then, for any
\delta>0,

H\big(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta\big)\gg r^{3+\delta}

as
r\downarrow 0
.

Moreover, the limit
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\phi\big(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta\big)
exists and equals
\phi^{\gamma}(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta)
for any
\gamma>3
.
Finally, given
K>1
, there exists
C_{K}>1
such that the following holds:

\frac{1}{C_{K}}\leq\frac{H(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta)}{H(\theta r,(u-p_{2})\zeta)}\leq C_{K}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,1),\,\theta\in[K^{-1},K].

6.
The 2nd blow-up

After the preliminary results from the previous sections, we now start investigating the structure of “2nd blow-ups” at singular points, namely, blow-ups of
u-p_{2}
.

The following lemma gives estimates for the difference of two solutions.
Note that both
u
and
p_{2}
satisfy the same equation
\text{\small\bf H}v=\chi_{\{v>0\}}
. Although we will first apply the following lemma to
u
and
p_{2}
, we give a more general version (for difference of approximate solutions) which will be very useful later on in the paper. Recall that
\mathscr{C}_{r}=B_{r}\times(-r^{2},0)
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
u_{i}:\mathscr{C}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2
, solve

(6.1)

\begin{cases}\text{\small\bf H}u_{i}=\chi_{\{u_{i}>0\}}(1+\varepsilon_{i}(x,t))\quad\textrm{in}\quad\mathscr{C}_{2}\\
u_{i}\geq 0\\
\partial_{t}u_{i}\geq 0,\end{cases}

with
|\varepsilon_{i}(x,t)|\leq\bar{\varepsilon}<\frac{1}{100}
, and denote
w:=u_{1}-u_{2}
.
Then

(6.2)

\|w\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}+\bar{\varepsilon}\big)

and

(6.3)

\left(\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}|\nabla w|^{2}+|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|+|\partial_{t}w|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}+\bar{\varepsilon}\big),

where
C
is a dimensional constant.

Proof.

We first prove (
6.2
).
On the one hand, we note that inside
\{u_{1}=0\}
it holds

(6.4)

\text{\small\bf H}w=1+\varepsilon_{1}-\text{\small\bf H}u_{2}\geq 1-\bar{\varepsilon}-(1+\varepsilon_{2})\chi_{\{u_{2}>0\}}\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon},\qquad w=-u_{2}\leq 0,

therefore
w_{+}=\max(0,w)
satisfies
\text{\small\bf H}w_{+}\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}
in
\mathscr{C}_{2}
. Since
(u_{2}-u_{1})_{+}=w_{-}
, by symmetry we also have
\text{\small\bf H}w_{-}\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}
. Thus,

(\Delta-\partial_{t})|w|\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}\qquad\mbox{ in }\mathscr{C}_{2},

so the parabolic Harnack inequality yields

\sup_{B_{1}\times(-1,0)}|w|\leq C_{n}\bigg(\int_{B_{2}\times(-2,0)}|w|+\bar{\varepsilon}\bigg)\leq C_{n}\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{2}\times(-2,0))}+\bar{\varepsilon}\big),

which proves (
6.2
).

We next prove (
6.3
).
Notice that
w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|w|
, since

(u_{1}-u_{2})\,\text{\small\bf H}(u_{1}-u_{2})=\begin{cases}-|u_{1}-u_{2}|(|\varepsilon_{1}|+|\varepsilon_{2}|)\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|u_{1}-u_{2}|\quad&\mbox{if }u_{1}>0,\ u_{2}>0\\
u_{1}(1+\varepsilon_{1}-0)\geq 0&\mbox{if }u_{1}>0,\ u_{2}=0\\
-u_{2}(0-1-\varepsilon_{2})\geq 0&\mbox{if }u_{1}=0,\ u_{2}>0\\
0&\mbox{if }u_{1}=0,\ u_{2}=0.\end{cases}

Similarly
\partial_{t}w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|
, since

\partial_{t}(u_{1}-u_{2})\,\text{\small\bf H}(u_{1}-u_{2})=\begin{cases}-|\partial_{t}(u_{1}-u_{2})|(|\varepsilon_{1}|+|\varepsilon_{2}|)\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}(u_{1}-u_{2})|\quad&\mbox{if }u_{1}>0,\ u_{2}>0\\
\partial_{t}u_{1}(1+\varepsilon_{1}-0)\geq 0&\mbox{if }u_{1}>0,\ u_{2}=0\\
-\partial_{t}u_{2}(0-1-\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\geq 0&\mbox{if }u_{1}=0,\ u_{2}>0\\
0&\mbox{if }u_{1}=0,\ u_{2}=0.\end{cases}

On the one hand, choosing

(6.5)

\tilde{\eta}=\tilde{\eta}(x)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{5/3})\quad\mbox{nonnegative and such that $\tilde{\eta}\equiv 1$ in $\overline{B}_{4/3}$},

we obtain

(6.6)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\tilde{\eta}+2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(w\,\text{\small\bf H}w+|\nabla w|^{2})\tilde{\eta}=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}\Delta(w^{2})\tilde{\eta}=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\Delta\tilde{\eta}\leq C\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}w^{2}.

Integrating in
t
the last inequality, and recalling that
w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|w|
, using (
6.5
) we obtain

\int_{-3/2}^{0}\biggl(\int_{B_{4/3}\times\{t\}}\big(|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)\biggr)\,dt\leq C_{n}\sup_{t\in(-3/2,0)}\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}\big(|w|^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}|w|\big).

Now from the
L^{\infty}
estimate in Step 1 (plus a standard covering argument, to obtain a control in
\mathscr{C}_{3/2}
instead of
\mathscr{C}_{1}
), we obtain that

\sup_{t\in(-3/2,0)}\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}\big(|w|^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}|w|\big)\leq C_{n}\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}+\bar{\varepsilon}\big)^{2},

and therefore

(6.7)

\int_{-3/2}^{0}\biggl(\int_{B_{4/3}\times\{t\}}\big(|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)\biggr)\,dt\leq C_{n}\big(\|w\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}+\bar{\varepsilon}\big)^{2}.

On the other hand, choosing now
\eta=\eta(x)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{4/3})
such that
\eta\equiv 1
in
\overline{B}_{1}
, and using
\text{\small\bf H}w(\partial_{t}w)\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|
, for any
M>0
we have

\begin{split}2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}+&\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}=2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}+\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}2\nabla w\cdot(\nabla\partial_{t}w)\eta^{2}\\
&=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}\Big(2(\partial_{t}w-\Delta w)(\partial_{t}w)\eta^{2}-4\partial_{t}w\nabla w\cdot\nabla\eta\,\eta\Big)\\
&\leq\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}\Big(4\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|\eta^{2}+2M|\nabla w|^{2}|\nabla\eta|^{2}+\frac{2}{M}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}\Big).\end{split}

Since
4\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{t}w|^{2}
, choosing
M=4
and using that
\eta
is supported in
B_{4/3}
, we obtain

\int_{B_{1}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}\leq C_{n}\bigg(\int_{B_{4/3}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg).

Multiplying this inequality by a nonnegative smooth function
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-3/2,0])
such that
\psi|_{[-1,0]}\equiv 1
, and integrating over
[-3/2,0]
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{3/2}\biggl(\int_{B_{1}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}\biggr)\psi(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{3/2}\biggl(\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}\biggr)\psi^{\prime}(t)\,dt+C_{n}\bigg[\int_{0}^{3/2}\biggl(\int_{B_{4/3}}|\nabla w|^{2}\biggr)\psi(t)\,dt+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{n}\bigg[\int_{0}^{3/2}\biggl(\int_{B_{4/3}}|\nabla w|^{2}\biggr)\,dt+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg].

Combining this bound with (
6.7
), the result follows.
∎

As a consequence, we find:

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
),
(0,0)
a singular point, and let
w:=u-p_{2}
.
Then, for all
r\in(0,1)
,

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|\nabla w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|\nabla\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}.

Also, for all
e\in\{p_{2}=0\}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we have

\|\nabla\partial_{e}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}.

The constant
C
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
6.1
with
u_{1}=r^{-2}u_{r}
,
u_{2}=p_{2}
, and
\bar{\varepsilon}=0
, to obtain

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}+\|\nabla w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}.

Now, to prove the
L^{\infty}
bound on
\partial_{t}w_{r}
we observe that
\partial_{t}(u-p_{2})=\partial_{t}u\geq 0
and
\text{\small\bf H}\partial_{t}(u-p_{2})=\partial_{t}\chi_{\{u>0\}}\geq 0
. Hence
\partial_{t}w_{r}
is nonnegative and subcaloric, and therefore the weak Harnack inequality implies that

\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1/2})}\leq C_{n}\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{n}\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}.

Also, since
(\partial_{t}w_{r})\,\text{\small\bf H}(\partial_{t}w_{r})\geq 0
, similarly to (
6.7
) (replacing
w
by
\partial_{t}w_{r}
) we obtain

\|\nabla\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1/2})}\leq C\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|\partial_{t}w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}.

By a covering argument we can replace
\mathscr{C}_{1/2}
by
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, the first part of the corollary follows.

Finally, given
e\in\{p_{2}=0\}
, since
\partial_{e}p_{2}\equiv 0
we obtain that
\partial_{e}w_{r}=0
on
\{u=0\}
and that
\text{\small\bf H}\partial_{e}w_{r}=0
in
\{u>0\}
. This yields
\partial_{e}w_{r}\text{\small\bf H}\partial_{e}w_{r}=0
, and arguing as before we obtain

\|\nabla\partial_{e}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1/2})}\leq C\|\partial_{e}w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|\nabla w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}.

Again by a covering argument we can replace
\mathscr{C}_{1/2}
by
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

The next lemma will be very useful in the sequel.
We recall that
\zeta
denotes the cut-off function defined in Section
2.7
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
),
(0,0)
a singular point, and let
w:=u-p_{2}
. Then, for all
r\in(0,1)
we have

\frac{1}{C}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\leq\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq CH(r,w\zeta)^{1/2},

for some constant
C>1
depending only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

To prove Lemma
6.3
, we will use the following simple consequence of the Gaussian log-Sobolev inequality.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}dm=1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla f|^{2}dm\leq 4,

where
dm=G(x,-1)dx=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4}\right)dx
is the Gaussian measure.
Then, for some dimensional
R_{\circ}>0
, we have

\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}dm\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Given
\lambda
large (to be fixed later), choose
R_{\circ}
so that

\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}dm\leq\frac{1}{4}.

By assumption

\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}dm+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}\leq\lambda\}}dm+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm=1.

Suppose now, by contradiction, that
\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}dm<1/2
.
Since

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}\leq\lambda\}}dm\leq\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}dm\leq\frac{1}{4},

we have

(6.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm\geq\frac{1}{4}.

Recall the Gaussian log-Sobolev inequality:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}\log F^{2}dm\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla F|^{2}dm+\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr)\,\log\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr).

To reach a contradiction, we apply this inequality to
F:=|f|\chi_{\{|f|>e^{1/2}\}}
.
Indeed, since
|\nabla F|\leq|\nabla f|
,
\log(F^{2})\geq 1
,
F^{2}\leq e+f^{2}
, and
F=f
inside
\{f^{2}\geq\lambda\}
, we get

\displaystyle\log\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}}F^{2}\log(F^{2})\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}\log F^{2}dm

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla F|^{2}dm+\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr)\,\log\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla f|^{2}dm+\biggl(e+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}dm\biggr)\,\log\biggl(e+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}dm\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq 4+(e+1)\log(e+1).

Combining this bound with (
6.8
), we get

\log\lambda\leq 16+4(e+1)\log(e+1),

a contradiction if
\lambda
is chosen large enough.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
6.3
.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

We divide the proof into three steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. We first show that

(6.9)

\frac{1}{C}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\leq\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2R_{\circ}})},

where
R_{\circ}
is the dimensional constant from Lemma
6.4
.

Let
\tilde{w}_{r}:=\frac{(w\zeta)_{r}}{H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}}
.
Since
\phi(r,w\zeta)\leq 4
for
r
small, we have

\int_{\{t=-1\}}(\tilde{w}_{r})^{2}G=1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\int_{\{t=-1\}}|\nabla\tilde{w}_{r}|^{2}G\leq 4.

Then, using Lemma
6.4
we obtain

\int_{\{|x|\leq R_{\circ},\ t=-1\}}(\tilde{w}_{r})^{2}G\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Replacing
r
by
\theta^{1/2}r
with
\theta\in(1,4)
, by scaling we obtain

\int_{\{|x|\leq 2R_{\circ},\ t=-\theta\}}(\tilde{w}_{r})^{2}G\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Hence, after integrating with respect to
\theta\in(1,4)
, we find

\int_{\{|x|\leq 2R_{\circ},\ -4\leq t\leq-1\}}(\tilde{w}_{r})^{2}G\geq\frac{1}{C}.

Since
\{|x|\leq 2R_{\circ},\ -4\leq t\leq-1\}\subset\mathscr{C}_{2R_{\circ}}
, (
6.9
) follows.

\bullet

Step 2
. We now prove that, for any given
M>1
, there exists a constant
C_{M}\geq 1,
depending only on
n
and
M
, so that the following holds for all in
r\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
:

(6.10)

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq C_{M}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq M\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}.

To prove this, note that the
L^{\infty}
estimate from Corollary
6.2
gives

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})},

where
C_{\circ}
is dimensional.
Therefore, whenever
\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq M\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}
, we have

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}=2^{n+2}C_{\circ}\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq 2^{n+2}C_{\circ}M\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}.

In particular, for any
\tau>0
small, we have

\int_{\{x\in B_{1},-1<t<-\tau\}}w_{r}^{2}\geq\int_{\{x\in B_{1},-1<t<0\}}w_{r}^{2}-\tau\|w_{r}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq(1-\tau 2^{2n+4}C_{\circ}^{2}M^{2})\|w_{r}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}.

Choosing
\tau:=(2^{2n+5}C_{\circ}^{2}M^{2})^{-1}
and using that
G\geq c_{\tau}>0
inside
\{x\in B_{1},-1<t<-\tau\}
, we get

\frac{1}{2}\|w_{r}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq\int_{\{x\in B_{1},-1<t<-\tau\}}w_{r}^{2}\leq C_{1}\int_{\{x\in B_{1},-1<t<-\tau\}}w_{r}^{2}G\leq C_{2}\int_{\tau}^{1}H(\theta^{2}r,w\zeta)d\theta.

Since the last term can be bounded by
C_{3}H(r,w\zeta)
(thanks to Corollary
5.9
), this proves the contrapositive of (
6.10
) with
C_{M}=(2C_{3})^{1/2}
.

\bullet

Step 3
. We now conclude the proof of the lemma combining Steps 1 and 2.

First, (
6.9
) rescaled yields

\frac{1}{C}H(c_{\circ}r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\leq\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})},

where
c_{\circ}=\frac{1}{2R_{\circ}}
, that combined with

H(c_{\circ}r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\geq\frac{1}{C}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}
(see Corollary
5.9
) gives

\frac{1}{C}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\leq\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}.

This proves the first inequality in the statement.

To prove the second inequality, assume by contradiction that

\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq C_{M}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}
, with
C_{M}\geq 1
as in (
6.10
) and
M
large enough to be chosen. Then, by (
6.10
),

\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq M\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq MC_{M}H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}.

Let
N>1
be a large constant to be fixed.
Since
H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\geq C_{4}^{-1}H(2r,w\zeta)^{1/2}
(by Corollary
5.9
), choosing
M=NC_{4}
we obtain

\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq NC_{M}H(2r,w\zeta)^{1/2}.

This allows us to apply again (
6.10
) (with the same
M
) and with
r
replaced by
2r
, and we obtain

\|w_{4r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq M\|w_{2r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq MNC_{M}H(2r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\geq MNC_{M}C_{4}^{-1}H(4r,w\zeta)^{1/2}=N^{2}C_{M}H(4r,w\zeta)^{1/2}.

Iterating this argument
\ell
times, this yields

\|w_{2^{\ell}r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq N^{\ell}C_{M}H(2^{\ell}r,w\zeta)^{1/2}

Choosing
\ell
such that
\frac{1}{8}\leq 2^{\ell}r\leq\frac{1}{4}
, and using that at scales of order
1
both quantities
\|w_{2^{\ell}r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}
and
H(2^{\ell}r,w\zeta)^{1/2}
are comparable to
1
, we obtain

C\geq N^{\ell}C_{M}\geq N^{\ell},

a contradiction if
N
is chosen large enough.
∎

The following Lipschitz estimate for the rescaled difference
u-p_{2}
will be useful in the sequel. We recall that
w_{r}
has been defined in (
2.1
),
while
\Sigma_{n-1}
is defined as in (
5.7
).

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
),
and
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}
. Then, given
R\geq 1
, for all
r\in\left(0,\frac{1}{10R}\right)
and
\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\cap\{p_{2}=0\}
, we have

\sup_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}\big((\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}w_{r})_{-}+|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+|\nabla w_{r}|\big)\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})},

for some constant
C
depending only on
n
,
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
, and
R
.

Proof.

Given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, a vector
\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, and
h\in(0,1)
, let

\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}f:=\frac{f(\,\cdot\,+h\boldsymbol{e},\,\cdot\,)+f(\,\cdot\,-h\boldsymbol{e},\,\cdot\,)-2f}{h^{2}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{t,h}f:=\frac{f\big(\,\cdot\,,\cdot+h\big)-f}{h}.

Note that, for
\boldsymbol{e}\in\{p=0\}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we have
\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}p\equiv 0
. Thus, since
\text{\small\bf H}u=1
outside of
\{u=0\}
and
\text{\small\bf H}u\leq 1
in
B_{1}\times(-1,0)
we have

\text{\small\bf H}\big(\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}w\big)=\frac{\text{\small\bf H}u\big(\,\cdot\,+h\boldsymbol{e},\,\cdot\,\big)+\text{\small\bf H}u\big(\,\cdot\,-h\boldsymbol{e},\,\cdot\,\big)-2\text{\small\bf H}u}{h^{2}}\leq 0\qquad\mbox{ in }\{u>0\}.

Similarly,

\text{\small\bf H}\big(\delta_{t,h}w\big)\leq 0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\text{\small\bf H}\big(\delta_{t,-h}w\big)\leq 0\qquad\mbox{ in }\{u>0\}.

On the other hand, since
u\geq 0
, we have

\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}w=\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}u(\,\cdot\,)\geq 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\delta_{t,\pm h}w=\delta_{t,\pm h}u(\,\cdot\,)\geq 0\quad\mbox{ inside }\{u=0\}.

As a consequence, the negative part of the second order incremental quotient
(\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}w_{r})_{-}
is subcaloric, and so is its limit
(\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}^{2}w_{r})_{-}

(recall that
u\in C^{1,1}_{x}\cap C^{0,1}_{t}
, and thus
(\delta^{2}_{\boldsymbol{e},h}w_{r})_{-}\to(\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}^{2}w_{r})_{-}
and
a.e. as
h\to 0
). Similarly, both
(\delta_{t}w_{r})_{-}
and
(-\delta_{t}w_{r})_{-}
are subcaloric, and thus so is
|\partial_{t}w_{r}|
.

Therefore, by weak Harnack inequality (see Lemma
3.6
), for any
\varepsilon>0
we have

\sup_{\mathscr{C}_{4/3}}\big(|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+(\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}^{2}w_{r})_{-}\big)\leq C_{n}\biggl(\int_{\mathscr{C}_{5/4}}(|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+|\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}w_{r}|)^{\varepsilon}\biggr)^{1/\varepsilon}.

Also, by standard interpolation inequalities, the
L^{\varepsilon}
norm with
\varepsilon<1
can be controlled by the weak
L^{1}
norm, namely

\biggl(\int_{\mathscr{C}_{5/4}}(|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+|\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}w_{r}|)^{\varepsilon}\biggr)^{1/\varepsilon}\leq C(n,R)\,\sup_{\theta>0}\,\theta\bigl|\bigl\{(|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+|\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}w_{r}|)>\theta\bigr\}\cap\mathscr{C}_{5/4}\bigr|.

Furthermore, by the parabolic Calderón-Zygmund theory (see Theorem
3.5
), the right hand side above is controlled by

\|\text{\small\bf H}w_{r}\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{5/3})}+\|w_{r}\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{5/3})}.

In addition, since
\text{\small\bf H}w_{r}\leq 0
in
\mathscr{C}_{5/3}
, the norm
\|\text{\small\bf H}w_{r}\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{5/3})}
is controlled by the
L^{1}
norm of
w_{r}
inside
\mathscr{C}_{2}
: indeed, if
\chi
is a smooth nonnegative cut-off function that is equal to
1
in
\mathscr{C}_{5/3}
and vanishes outside
\mathscr{C}_{2}
, then integration by parts gives

(6.11)

\|\text{\small\bf H}w_{r}\|_{L^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{5/3})}\leq-\int_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}\chi\,\text{\small\bf H}w_{r}=-\int_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}(\Delta+\partial_{t})\chi\,w_{r}\leq C_{n}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}|w_{r}|\leq\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}{(\mathscr{C}_{2})}}.

This proves that

(6.12)

\sup_{\mathscr{C}_{4/3}}\big(|\partial_{t}w_{r}|+(\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}}^{2}w_{r})_{-}\big)\leq C_{n}\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}{(\mathscr{C}_{2})}}.

Finally, since
\{p_{2}=0\}
is
(n-1)
-dimensional (recall
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}
), as a consequence of (
6.12
) and
(\Delta-\partial_{t})w_{r}\leq 0
we deduce that

\partial_{\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}}w_{r}\leq C\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}{(\mathscr{C}_{2})}}\quad\mbox{in }\mathscr{C}_{4/3},\quad\mbox{ where }\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}\in\{p=0\}^{\perp}\mbox{ with }|\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}|=1.

The above semiconcavity estimate,
combined with the semiconvexity bound in (
6.12
), implies the desired uniform bound on
\|\nabla w_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}
.
∎

As a consequence of the previous lemma, we get the following result
(recall that, as a consequence of Lemma
5.8
and Corollary
5.9
, we know that
\phi(0^{+},w\zeta)\in[2,3]
).

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
),
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}
, and set
w:=u-p_{2}
and

\lambda^{2nd}:=\phi(0^{+},w\zeta)\in[2,3]
.
Then

(6.13)

\{u(\cdot\,,t)=0\}\cap B_{r}\subset\big\{x\,:\,{\rm dist}(x,\{p_{2}=0\})\leq Cr^{\lambda^{2nd}-1}\big\}

for all
r\in(0,1/2)
and
t\geq-r^{2}
.
In addition, the constant
C
depends only on
n
and
\|u(\,\cdot\,,0)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}
.

Proof.

As a consequence of Lemmas
5.6
and
6.5
we obtain

\|\nabla u-\nabla p_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r}\times\{-r\}))}\leq Cr^{\lambda^{2nd}-1}.

Thus, on
\{u=0\}\cap\bigl(B_{r}\times\{-r^{2}\}\bigr)
we have
|\nabla p_{2}(x)|=\big|{\rm dist}(x,\{p_{2}=0\})\big|\leq Cr^{\lambda^{2nd}-1}
. Since
\{u=0\}
shrinks with time, we obtain (
6.13
).
∎

Using the previous results, we can now prove the following:

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a solution of (
3.1
), and
(0,0)
be a singular point.
Let
w:=u-p_{2}
,
m:={\rm dim}(\{p_{2}=0\})\in\{0,1,2,\dots n-1\}
,
\lambda^{2nd}:=\phi(0^{+},w\zeta)
, and define

(6.14)
