\tilde{w}_{r}:=\frac{(u-p_{2})_{r}}{H\big(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta\big)}.

Then, for every sequence
r_{k}\downarrow 0
there is a subsequence
r_{k_{\ell}}
such that

\tilde{w}_{r_{k_{\ell}}}\rightarrow q\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\nabla\tilde{w}_{r_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup\nabla q\qquad\mbox{in }L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])

as
\ell\to\infty
, where
q\not\equiv 0
is a
\lambda^{2nd}
-homogeneous function. Moreover, for any
e
unit vector tangent to
\{p_{2}=0\}
, the following “growth estimates” hold:

(6.15)

\sup_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}\big(|W|+R^{2}|\partial_{t}W|\big)+\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}R^{2}|\nabla W|^{2}+R^{4}|\nabla\partial_{e}W|^{2}+R^{6}|\nabla\partial_{t}W|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\delta}R^{\lambda^{2nd}+\delta}

with
W=\tilde{w}_{r_{k_{\ell}}}
(for all
R
satisfying
1\leq R\ll r_{k_{\ell}}^{-1}
) and for
W=q
(for all
R\geq 1
).

In addition, we have:

(a)

If
0\leq m\leq n-2
then
\lambda^{2nd}=2
, and —in some appropriate coordinates—

(6.16)

\qquad p_{2}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\mu_{i}x_{i}^{2}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad q(x)=At+\nu\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}x_{j}^{2}\,,

where
\mu_{i}>0
,
\nu
and
A
are nonnegative, and
A-2(n-m)\nu+2\sum_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}=0
.

(b)

If
m=n-1
then
\tilde{w}_{r_{k_{\ell}}}\rightarrow q
in
C^{0}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
,
\lambda^{2nd}\in[2+\alpha_{\circ},3]
for some dimensional constant
\alpha_{\circ}>0
, and
q
solves the parabolic thin obstacle problem

(6.17)

\begin{cases}\text{\small\bf H}q\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad q\text{\small\bf H}q=0\quad&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)\\
\text{\small\bf H}q=0&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)\setminus\{p_{2}=0\}\\
\,q\geq 0&\mbox{on }\{p_{2}=0\}\\
\partial_{t}q\geq 0&\mbox{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0).\end{cases}

Proof.

Given
r_{k}\downarrow 0
, by the
H^{1}
space-time estimates of Corollary
6.2
we obtain (up to subsequence)

\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\rightarrow q\quad\mbox{and }\quad(\nabla,\partial_{t})\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\rightharpoonup(\nabla,\partial_{t})q\quad\mbox{in }L^{2}_{loc}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]\big),

for some
q\in L^{2}_{loc}((-\infty,0],H^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
Moreover, since
\phi(r_{k},w\zeta)\leq C
we have
\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big|\nabla(\widetilde{w\zeta})_{r_{k}}\big|^{2}G\leq C
, from which it follows that

\int_{\{t=-1\}}|\nabla q|^{2}G\leq C
.

Furthermore, by Lemma
5.6
(b) applied with
\gamma=4
,
Lemma
6.3
combined with Corollary
6.2
implies the validity of the “growth estimates”
(
6.15
) for
W=\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}
, for all
R
satisfying
1\leq R\ll r_{k}^{-1}
. Taking the limit as
k\to\infty
, since all the seminorms in the estimates are lower semicontinuous, we obtain that (
6.15
) holds also
W=q
(for all
R\geq 1
).
It is also easy to see, using the monotonicity of the frequency in Proposition
5.4
, that
q
is
\lambda^{2nd}
-homogeneous.

Let us show that, in addition,
q
satisfies

(6.18)

\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{2}-p)q\,G\,dx\geq 0\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad p\in\mathcal{P}.

Indeed, for any fixed
p\in\mathcal{P}
, Lemma
5.6
(a) gives

\frac{1}{r^{4}}(H\big(r,(u-p)\zeta\big)+r^{8}\big)+Ce^{-c/r}\geq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{r^{4}}(H\big(r,(u-p)\zeta\big)\big),

and therefore

\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big(r^{-2}u_{r}-p)\zeta_{r}\big)^{2}G+Cr^{4}\geq\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{2}-p)^{2}G.

This allows us to proceed exactly as in the proof of
[
FS17
, Lemma 2.11]
(with obvious modifications), and (
6.18
) follows.

Now in order to conclude the proof, we consider two cases.

(a) If
m\leq n-2
then
w_{r_{k}}\in H^{1}_{\rm loc}
it is caloric outside of an infinitesimal neighborhood of
\{p_{2}=0\}\times(-\infty,0)
.
Since
{\rm dim}(\{p_{2}=0\})\leq n-2
which has zero capacity, we deduce that
\text{\small\bf H}q\equiv 0
in all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)
.
Also,
by Lemma
5.8
(a) we know that
\lambda^{2nd}=2
.
Then, using that
\partial_{t}q\geq 0
and (
6.18
), we easily conclude that
q
must be of the form (
6.16
).

(b) If
m=n-1
, then by Lemma
5.8
(b) we have
\lambda^{2nd}\in[2,3]
. Moreover, using the Lipschitz estimate in Lemma
6.5
, we deduce that (up to subsequence)
\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\to q
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)
.
Clearly
\partial_{t}q\geq 0
(since
\partial_{t}\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\geq 0
).
Moreover,
q
is a solution of (
6.17
) because
\text{\small\bf H}w_{r_{k}}\leq 0
implies
\text{\small\bf H}w_{r_{k}}\rightharpoonup^{*}\ \text{\small\bf H}q\leq 0
as measures, and thus
w_{r_{k}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{r_{k}}\geq 0
implies
q\text{\small\bf H}q\geq 0
.
Also,
\text{\small\bf H}q\leq 0
is supported on
\{p_{2}=0\}
where
q\geq 0
(since on
\{p_{2}=0\}
we have
w_{r_{k}}=u_{r_{k}}\geq 0
).

Now, we notice that there are no
2
-homogeneous solutions
q
to (
6.17
) and satisfying (
6.18
) (see
[
FS17
, Proposition 2.10]
for a very similar argument), and therefore it must be
\lambda^{2nd}>2
.
Finally, the fact that
\lambda^{2nd}\geq 2+\alpha_{\circ}
can be proved either by compactness, or using
[
Shi20
, Proposition 9]
to deduce that there are no
\lambda
-homogeneous solutions to (
6.17
) for
\lambda\in(2,2+\alpha_{\circ})
.
∎

7.
The set
\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}

Let us define the sets

(7.1)

\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}:=\big\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}\ :\ \phi\big(0^{+},u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\big)<3\big\}

and

(7.2)

\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}:=\Sigma_{n-1}\setminus\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}.

In this section we investigate the structure of the possible blow-ups
q
at points of
\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
.
We start proving the following .

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
with
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
. Let
\tilde{w}_{r}
be defined by (
6.14
) and suppose that
\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\rightharpoonup q
in
W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
, for some sequence
r_{k}\downarrow 0
.
Then:

(i)

q
is
\lambda^{2nd}
-homogeneous, where
\lambda^{2nd}\in(2,3)
, and
\int_{\{t=-1\}}q^{2}G=1
;

(ii)

q
satisfies the growth estimates in (
6.15
);

(iii)

q
is a solution of (
6.17
);

(iv)

\partial_{t}q\not\equiv 0
;

(v)

\partial_{ee}q\geq 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all
e\in\{x_{n}=0\}
and
\partial_{tt}q\geq 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In the proof of Lemma
7.1
(iv) we will use the following result, which gives a classification of all convex solutions to the Signorini problem.
(Notice that, previously, this was only known for homogeneities
\lambda\leq 2
.)

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
q=q(x)
be a
\lambda
-homogeneous solution of the elliptic Signorini problem, namely

\Delta q\leq 0
and
q\Delta q=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\Delta q=0
on
\{x_{n}\neq 0\}
,
q\geq 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
.

Assume in addition that
q
is convex with respect to all directions parallel to
\{x_{n}=0\}
. Then, either
\lambda=3/2
, or
q
is a harmonic polynomial and
\lambda
is an integer.

Proof.

Since
q
is homogeneous and convex with respect to the first
n-1
variables, this implies that the set
K:=\{q=0\}\subset\{x_{n}=0\}
is convex.
Now, if
K
has empty interior as a subset of
\{x_{n}=0\}
, then it will be contained in a
(n-2)
-dimensional subspace, so it follows form a standard capacity argument that
q
must be harmonic in all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Hence,
q
is a homogenous harmonic polynomial and
\lambda
will be an even integer.
On the other hand, if
K=\{x_{n}=0\}
, then
q
would be an odd harmonic polynomial and
\lambda
will be an odd integer.

Consider now the case when
K\neq\{x_{n}=0\}
has non-empty interior inside
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
Up to replacing
q(x^{\prime},x_{n})
by
q(x^{\prime},x_{n})+q(x^{\prime},-x_{n})
, we may assume that
q
is even.
Pick a direction
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
such that
-e\in{\rm int}(K)
.
Then, for any point
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
we have
(z^{\prime},0)-et\in K
for
t>0
large enough.
We claim that this implies that
\partial_{e}q\geq 0
in all
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Indeed, if it was
\partial_{e}q(z)<0
at some point
z=(z^{\prime},z_{n})
with
z_{n}>0
then, by convexity of
q
in the direction
e=(e^{\prime},0)
, we would have

q(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\geq q(z^{\prime},z_{n})-\partial_{e}q(z^{\prime},z_{n})s\quad\mbox{for all }s>0.

Hence,
q(z^{\prime}-s_{\circ}e^{\prime},z_{n})>0
for
s=s_{\circ}
sufficiently large.
However, since
(z-s_{\circ}e^{\prime},0)\in K
, then (recall that
q
is even)

(7.3)

q(z-s_{\circ}e^{\prime},0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{n}q(z-s_{\circ}e^{\prime},0^{+})\leq 0.

Since
\Delta q\leq 0
and
\partial_{ii}q\geq 0
for all
i\leq n-1
we have that
q
is concave in the variable
x_{n}
, a contradiction.

Once we have shown that
\partial_{e}q\geq 0
in all
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all
e\in-{\rm int}(K)
, we can use the argument from
[
CRS17
,
RS17
]
in order to classify blow-ups.
Indeed, we look at two different directions in the monotonicity cone,
e
and
e^{\prime}
, and observe that

\partial_{e}q|_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}
and
\partial_{e^{\prime}}q|_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}
are both positive harmonic functions in the same cone, and they both vanishing on the boundary.
Therefore, by uniqueness of positive harmonic functions in cones, one must be a multiple of the other.
This argument, applied to
n-1
independent directions of the monotonicity cone, leads to the fact that
q
depends only on two euclidean variables, namely,
x_{n}
and a linear combination of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}
.
One then reduces the classification problem to the well-known situation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in which all homogeneous solutions are explicit and the only convex ones have homogeneity
3/2
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
7.1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Note that (i)-(iii) follow from Proposition
6.7
.

To show (iv), we recall that, by Proposition
3.7
, we have

(D^{2}u)_{-}\leq C\,\partial_{t}u.

Hence, if
e\in\{x_{n}=0\}
,

\big(\partial_{ee}(u-p_{2})\big)_{-}=(\partial_{ee}u)_{-}\leq C\,\partial_{t}u=C\,\partial_{t}(u-p_{2}),

and, as a consequence,

(7.4)

(\partial_{ee}\tilde{w}_{r})_{-}\leq C\,\partial_{t}\tilde{w}_{r},\quad\mbox{ and thus }(\partial_{ee}q)_{-}\leq C\,\partial_{t}q.

Assume now by contradiction that
\partial_{t}q\equiv 0
.
Then
q=q(x)
is convex with respect to all directions in
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
However, using Lemma
7.2
, we obtain a contradiction with the fact that, by assumption,
q
is homogeneous of degree
\lambda\in(2,3)
.

We now prove (v). By differentiating (
6.17
) with respect to
t
, and using that
q
is homogeneous, it follows that
\varphi_{1}:=\partial_{t}q(x,-1)
solves

(7.5)

\mathcal{L}_{OU}\varphi_{1}=\lambda_{1}\varphi_{1}\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\big(\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{q=0\}\big),\qquad\varphi_{1}=0\quad\mbox{on }\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{q=0\},

where
\lambda_{1}:=\frac{\lambda^{2nd}-2}{2}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{OU}
is as in (
5.10
).
Let
dm=G(x,-1)dx
be the Gaussian measure.
Since
\varphi_{1}
belongs to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},dm)
(as a consequence of the estimates in (
6.15
)), then
\varphi_{1}
must be the first eigenfunction of
\mathcal{L}_{OU}
, and
\lambda_{1}
the first eigenvalue.

Also, as shown in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
6.5
, the function
\big(\partial_{ee}(u-p_{2})\big)_{-}\geq 0
is subcaloric.
Therefore,
f_{r}:=(\partial_{ee}\tilde{w}_{r})_{-}
satisfies

f_{r}\,\text{\small\bf H}f_{r}\geq 0
, and we obtain (cp. (
6.7
))

R^{2}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}|\nabla f_{r}|^{2}\leq C_{n}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{2R}}f_{r}^{2}.

Since
f_{r}\leq C\,\partial_{t}\tilde{w}_{r}
(by (
7.4
)), the estimates in (
6.15
) yield

(7.6)

\bigg(R^{2}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}|\nabla(\partial_{ee}\tilde{w}_{r})_{-}|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{2R}}|\partial_{t}\tilde{w}_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\delta}R^{\lambda^{2nd}+\delta-2}.

Set
\psi:=\big(\partial_{ee}q(x,-1)\big)_{-}
. Then,
\psi
is a
(\lambda^{2nd}-2)
-homogeneous
nonnegative subsolution of (
7.5
). Also, since

\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\to q
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}
, it follows by (
7.6
) that
\psi
belongs to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},dm)
.
By uniqueness of the first eigenfunction, it follows that

\psi
is a nonnegative multiple of
\varphi_{1}
.

We claim that
\psi\equiv 0
. Indeed, since

\varphi_{1}
is the first eigenfuction, it is positive everywhere (except on the boundary).
Hence, if by contradiction
\psi
is not zero everywhere, then we get
(\partial_{ee}q(x,-1)\big)_{-}>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\big(\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{q(x,-1)=0\}\big)
, and in particular
\partial_{ee}q<0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}\setminus\{q(x,-1)=0\}
. However, since
q=|\nabla q|=0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\partial\{q(x,-1)=0\}
, this contradicts the fact that
q\geq 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
. Hence
\psi\equiv 0
, or equivalently

\partial_{ee}q\geq 0
, as desired.

Finally, to show that
\partial_{tt}q\geq 0
, we recall that, thanks to Proposition
3.4
, we have

\partial_{tt}(u-p_{2})=\partial_{tt}u\geq-C.

After scaling, we obtain

\partial_{tt}\tilde{w}_{r}\geq-C\frac{r^{4}}{H\big(r,(u-p_{2})\zeta\big)^{1/2}}=o(1)\qquad\mbox{as }r\downarrow 0,

and letting
r\to 0
the result follows.
∎

We can now prove a “dimension reduction” lemma for the set
\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
.
We say that a set of points
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
has rank at most
m
if, for any finite subset
\{y_{i_{j}}\}_{1\leq j\leq N}
, it holds
{\rm span}(y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{N}})\leq m
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
with
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
, let
\tilde{w}_{r}
be defined as in (
6.14
), and suppose that
\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\to q
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
, for some sequence
r_{k}\downarrow 0
.
Suppose in addition that
(x_{k}^{(j)},t_{k}^{(j)})
are sequences of singular points with
t_{k}^{(j)}\geq 0
such that
y_{k}^{(j)}:=\frac{x_{k}^{(j)}}{r_{k}}\to y_{\infty}^{(j)}\neq 0
. Then

y_{\infty}^{(j)}\in\{x_{n}=0\}
, and the set
\big\{y_{\infty}^{(j)}\big\}_{j}
has rank at most
n-2
.

Before giving the proof of Lemma
7.4
we need a couple of auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
). Then:

(a)
The singular set is closed —more precisely
\Sigma\cap\overline{B}_{\varrho}
is closed for any
\varrho<1
. Moreover,

\Sigma\cap\overline{B}_{\varrho}\ni(x_{k},t_{k})\to(x_{\infty},t_{\infty})\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad p_{2,x_{k},t_{k}}\to p_{2,x_{\infty},t_{\infty}}.

(b)
The function

\Sigma\ni(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\mapsto\phi(0^{+},u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,\,,t_{\circ})-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})

is upper semi-continuous.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

(a) In
[
Caf77
]
, Caffarelli proved that the regular set is relatively open inside the free boundary, hence the singular set is closed.
Moreover, it follows from Lemma
5.6
(a) that
p_{2,x_{k},t_{k}}\to p_{2,x_{\infty},t_{\infty}}
whenever
(x_{k},t_{k})\to(x_{\infty},t_{\infty})
(cp.
[
Fig18b
, Theorem 8.1]
).

(b) Fixed
\gamma>3
, this follows from the (almost) monotonicity of the frequency
\phi^{\gamma}
and Corollary
5.9
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
),
(x_{k},t_{k})\in\Sigma
, and assume that
(x_{k},t_{k})\to(0,0)\in\Sigma
.
Also, assume that
t_{k}\geq-C^{\prime}|x_{k}|^{2}
for some
C^{\prime}>0
. Then

\textstyle{\rm dist}\left(\frac{x_{k}}{|x_{k}|},\{p_{2}=0\}\right)\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $k\to\infty$}.

Proof.

Let
r_{k}:=|x_{k}|
,
y_{k}:=x_{k}/r_{k}
, and
s_{k}:=t_{k}/r_{k}^{2}
.
We know that
U_{k}:=r_{k}^{-2}u_{r_{k}}
converges locally uniformly to
p_{2}
as
k\to\infty
. Moreover, since
U_{k}
has a singular point at
(s_{k},t_{k})
and
U_{k}
is increasing in time, it follows that
U_{k}(y_{k},s)=0
for all
s\leq s_{k}
. In particular, thanks to our assumption,
U_{k}(y_{k},-C^{\prime})=0
.
This implies that, for any accumulation point

y_{\infty}
of
\{y_{k}\}
, we must have
p_{2}(y_{\infty})=0
. The lemma follows.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
7.3
.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

The fact that
y_{\infty}^{(j)}\in\{x_{n}=0\}
follows from Lemma
7.5
.
Also, since
(x_{k}^{(j)},t_{k}^{(j)})
is a singular point, we have
u(x_{k}^{(j)},t_{k}^{(j)})=0
.
Hence, since
t_{k}\geq 0
and
u
is increasing in time, we deduce that
(u-p_{2})(x_{k}^{(j)},t)\leq 0
for all
t\leq 0
, and therefore

\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}(y_{k}^{(j)},t)\leq 0\quad\mbox{for all }t\leq 0.

Since
\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\to q
locally uniformly, it follows that
q(y_{\infty}^{(j)},t)\leq 0
, but since
y_{\infty}^{(j)}\in\{x_{n}=0\}
(where
q
is nonnegative, see (
6.17
)), it must be

q(y_{\infty}^{(j)},t)\equiv 0\quad\mbox{for all }t\leq 0.

Now, the fact that
q
is homogeneous, monotone in
t
, and convex with respect to the first
n-1
variables (see Lemma
7.1
), implies that the cone
K\subset\{x_{n}=0\}
generated by
y_{\infty}^{(j)}
is contained inside
\{q=0\}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
.

Assume now by contradiction that the rank of
\big\{y_{\infty}^{(j)}\big\}_{j}
is
n-1
. Then
K
has non-empty interior inside
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
In particular, if we pick a direction
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
such that
-e\in{\rm int}(K)
, then for any point
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
we have

(z^{\prime},0)-se\in K
for
s>0
large enough.

Set
\hat{q}(x):=q(x,-1)
. We claim that

\partial_{e}\hat{q}\geq 0\qquad\textrm{inside}\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}

for any such direction
e
.
Indeed, suppose by contradiction that
\partial_{e}\hat{q}(z,-1)<0
at some point
z=(z^{\prime},z_{n})
with (with no loss of generality)
z_{n}>0
.
Then, by convexity of
q
in the direction
e=(e^{\prime},0)
, we have

\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\geq\hat{q}(z^{\prime},z_{n})-\partial_{e}\hat{q}(z^{\prime},z_{n})s.

Hence for all
s>s_{\circ}
(with
s_{\circ}
large enough depending on
z
) we have

(7.7)

\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\geq c_{\circ}s\qquad\mbox{where }c_{\circ}>0,

and

(7.8)

(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},0)\in K\subset\{\hat{q}=0\}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}.

However, since
q
is convex in the first
n-1
variables, we have

(7.9)

\partial_{nn}q\leq\Delta q\leq\partial_{t}q\leq C_{\delta}R^{\lambda^{2nd}-2+\delta}\quad\mbox{in }B_{R}\times(-R^{2},0).

Therefore, since
\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},0)=0
and
\partial_{n}\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},0^{+})\geq 0
(by (
7.8
) and (
6.17
)), integrating (
7.9
) along the segment joining
(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},0)
and
(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})
we obtain

\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\leq C_{\delta}R^{\lambda^{2nd}-2+\delta}\,\frac{|z_{n}|^{2}}{2}\qquad\mbox{whenever}\quad\big|(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\big|\leq R.

This implies that
\hat{q}(z^{\prime}-se^{\prime},z_{n})\leq C_{\delta}s^{\lambda^{2nd}-2+\delta}|z_{n}|^{2}
for
s
sufficiently large. However, since
\lambda^{2nd}<3
, choosing
\delta>0
such that
\lambda^{2nd}-2+\delta<1
, this contradicts (
7.7
).

Thus, we have shown that
\partial_{e}\hat{q}\geq 0
. Then, differentiating (
6.17
) with respect to
e
and using that
q
is homogeneous, we deduce that
\psi:=\partial_{e}\hat{q}
is a nonnegative solution of

\mathcal{L}_{OU}\psi=\lambda\psi\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\big(\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{\hat{q}=0\}\big),\qquad\psi=0\quad\mbox{on }\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{\hat{q}=0\}.

Also, since
\partial_{e}q
is
(\lambda^{2nd}-1)
-homogeneous, its associated eigenvalue is
\lambda=\frac{\lambda^{2nd}-1}{2}
.
On the other hand, we showed in the proof of Lemma
7.1
(v) that the positive function
\varphi_{1}(x):=\partial_{t}q(x,-1)
is the first eigenfunction of this problem and has eigenvalue
\frac{\lambda^{2nd}-2}{2}
.
Since the first eigenfunction is the only one which does not change sign, this provides the desired contradiction.
∎

We can now prove that
\pi_{x}\big(\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}\big)
has Hausdorff dimension at most
n-2
(recall (
2.3
)). Note that this is not standard, since Lemma
7.3
only shows that,
around any point
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
, the set
\pi_{x}(\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}\cap\{t\geq t_{\circ}\}\big)\cap B_{r}(x_{\circ})
is contained in a
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of some
(n-2)
-plane. The fact that we can only control points “in the future”
\{t\geq t_{\circ}\}
forces us to prove some new appropriate GMT result that incorporates this feature.
We state it as an abstract result.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
, and suppose that for all
(x,t)\in E
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\varrho_{x,t,\varepsilon}>0
such that, for all
r\in(0,\varrho_{x,t,\varepsilon})
,

(7.10)

\pi_{x}\big(E\cap(\overline{B_{r}}(x)\times[t,\infty])\big)\subset(x+L+\overline{B_{r\varepsilon}})\quad\mbox{for some $L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ linear space, with ${\rm dim}(L)=m$}.

Then

{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E)\big)\leq m.

To prove this proposition, we need the following classical GMT lemma, whose proof can be found for instance in
[
Ros21
, Lemma 9]
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Given
\alpha>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
, depending only on
n
and
\alpha
, such that the following holds:
Let
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and suppose that there exists
\varrho>0
such that, for all
x\in F
and
r\in(0,\varrho)
,

F\cap\overline{B_{r}}(x)\subset(x+L+\overline{B_{r\varepsilon}})\quad\mbox{for some $L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ linear space, with ${\rm dim}(L)=m$}.

Then
\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}(F)=0
.

To do this, for any arbitrarily small
\delta>0
we will construct a cover of
\pi_{x}(E)
by countably many balls
B_{r_{i}}(x_{i})
of diameter
\leq\delta
such that
\sum_{i}r_{i}^{m+\alpha}

can be made arbitrarily small.
This implies that
\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}(\pi_{x}(E))=0
for all
\alpha>0
and hence
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E)\big)\leq m
.

We can now prove Proposition
7.6
.

Proof of Proposition
7.6
.

Up to taking countable unions, we may assume that
E\subset\overline{B_{1/2}}(z)\times[-1,1]
.
Also, since

E=\bigcup_{\ell}E_{\ell},\qquad\textrm{where}\quad E_{\ell}=\big\{(x,t)\in E:\rho_{x,t,\varepsilon}>{\textstyle\frac{1}{\ell}}\big\},

it suffices to prove that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})\big)\leq m
for each
\ell
.
To this aim, given
\alpha>0
arbitrarily small, we shall prove that
\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}\big(\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})\big)=0
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
(depending only on
n
and
\alpha
, to be chosen later).
By assumption, for all
(x,t)\in E_{\ell}
and for all
r\in(0,1/\ell]
,

(7.11)

\pi_{x}\big(E_{\ell}\cap(\overline{B_{r}}(x)\times[t,\infty])\big)\subset(x+L_{x,t,r}+\overline{B_{r\varepsilon}})\quad\mbox{for some $L_{x,t,r}$ linear and $m$-dimensional}.

Note that (
7.11
) holds also for all
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
in the closure
\overline{E_{\ell}}
of
E_{\ell}
, provided we define
L_{\bar{x},\bar{t},r}
as an arbitrary limit of the hyperplanes
L_{x,t,\varepsilon}
as
(x,t)\in E_{\ell}
converges to
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
.

Now, given
(x,t)\in E_{\ell}
and
r\leq 1/\ell
,
by compactness there exists
(\bar{x},\bar{t})\in\overline{B_{r}}(x)\times[-1,1]\cap\overline{E_{\ell}}
such that

\bar{t}\leq\pi_{t}\big(\overline{B_{r}}(x)\times[-1,1]\cap\overline{E_{\ell}}\big)\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, applying (
7.11
) at the point
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
, we get

\pi_{x}\big(E_{\ell}\big)\cap B_{r}(x)\subset\pi_{x}\left(\overline{E_{\ell}}\cap(\overline{B_{r}}(\bar{x})\times[\bar{t},\infty))\right)\subset\bar{x}+L_{\bar{x},\bar{t},r}+\overline{B_{r\varepsilon}}.

Thus, choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, Lemma
7.7
applied with
F=\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})
implies that
\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}\big(\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})\big)=0
, as desired.
∎

Finally, as an immediate consequence of Lemma
7.3
and Proposition
7.6
, we deduce the following:

Proposition 7.8
.

The set
\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
satisfies
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3})\big)\leq n-2
.

8.
Quadratic cleaning of the singular set and proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we prove that, for any given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}
such that, for any singular point
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma
, it holds

(8.1)

\Sigma\cap\big\{|x-x_{\circ}|\leq r,\ t\geq t_{\circ}+Cr^{2-\varepsilon}\big\}=\emptyset.

As we shall see, this estimate will allow us to prove Theorem
1.1
.

We begin with the following:

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a solution of (
3.1
) and
(0,0)
a singular point.
Assume that
H(r,u-p_{2})^{1/2}\leq\omega(r)
, where
\omega(r)=o(r^{2})
as
r\downarrow 0
.
Suppose in addition that
\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
is an eigenvector
D^{2}p_{2}
with maximal eigenvalue, and that there exists
c>0
such that

(8.2)

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq\frac{r}{10}\}}\partial_{t}u(\cdot,-r^{2})\geq c\,r^{\beta}\quad\mbox{ for all $r\in(0,1)$, for some $\beta\in(0,1]$}.

Then there exists
C>0
such that

\{u=0\}\cap\big(B_{r/2}\times[C\omega(r)r^{-\beta}+r^{2},1)\big)=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,1).

Proof.

Since
\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})=-\chi_{\{u=0\}}\leq 0
, the function
u-p_{2}
is supercaloric.
Also, since
\partial_{t}u\geq 0
, then
u-p_{2}
is nondecreasing in time. Thus, thanks to the bound
H(r,u-p_{2})^{1/2}\leq\omega(r)
we get

(8.3)

u\geq p_{2}-C_{1}\omega(r)\quad\textrm{in}\quad B_{r}\times[-r^{2}/2,1).

In particular, since
\omega(r)=o(r^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
is an eigenvector
D^{2}p_{2}
with maximal eigenvalue, for any fixed
\delta>0
small, we obtain

(8.4)

\{u=0\}\cap\big(B_{r}\times[-r^{2}/2,1)\big)\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq r\delta^{2}\}\qquad\forall\,r\ll 1.

Thus
\text{\small\bf H}(\partial_{t}u)=0
inside
\big(B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|>r\delta^{2}\}\big)\times[-r^{2}/2,1)
,
and therefore (
8.2
) and Harnack inequality imply that
\partial_{t}u(\cdot,-r^{2}/4)\geq 2c_{2}r^{\beta}
inside
B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|>r\delta\}
, for some
c_{2}=c_{2}(n,\delta)>0
.

Combining this bound with the estimate
\partial_{tt}u\geq-C
(see Proposition
3.4
), we get

(8.5)

\partial_{t}u\geq c_{2}r^{\beta}\quad\mbox{in }\big(B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|>r\delta\}\big)\times[-r^{2}/4,c_{3}r],

for some
c_{3}>0
(recall that
\beta\leq 1
).
In particular, combining (
8.3
) and (
8.5
), we obtain

(8.6)

u(\cdot,-r^{2}/4+h)\geq p_{2}-C_{1}\omega(r)+c_{2}r^{\beta}h\qquad\mbox{in }B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|>r\delta\},

for all
h\in[0,c_{3}r]
.
Choosing
h\geq r^{2}/4+2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta}
, and using again (
8.3
), since
u
is nondecreasing we obtain

(8.7)

u\geq p_{2}+C_{1}\omega(r)\bigl(-1+2\chi_{\{|x_{n}|>r\delta\}}\bigr)\qquad\forall\,(x,t)\in B_{r}\times[2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta},1).

Now, let
h^{\delta}
be the solution to

\begin{cases}\text{\small\bf H}h^{\delta}=0\quad&\mbox{in }B_{1}\times(0,\infty)\\
h^{\delta}=2&\mbox{on }\big(\partial B_{1}\cap\{|x_{n}|>\delta\}\big)\times[0,\infty)\\
h^{\delta}=0&\mbox{on }\big(\partial B_{1}\cap\{|x_{n}|<\delta\}\big)\times[0,\infty)\\
h^{\delta}=0&\mbox{at }t=0.\end{cases}

Since
h^{\delta}\to 2
as
\delta\to 0
, it follows that
h^{\delta}\geq\frac{3}{2}
inside
B_{1/2}
for all
t\geq 1
, provided
\delta
is small enough.
Now we can observe that

\psi(x,t):=p_{2}(x)+C_{1}\omega(r)\bigg(-1+h^{\delta}\Big(\frac{x}{r},\frac{t-2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta}}{r^{2}}\Big)\bigg)

satisfies
\text{\small\bf H}\psi=1
in
B_{r}\times[2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta},\infty)
and, by (
8.7
), we have
u\geq\psi
on the parabolic boundary
\partial_{par}\bigl(B_{r}\times[2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta},1)\bigr)
. Hence, by the maximum principle,

u\geq\psi\quad\mbox{in }B_{r},\mbox{ for }t\geq 2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta}.

Evaluating at
t=2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta}+r^{2}
(and using that
h^{\delta}\geq\frac{3}{2}
in
B_{1/2}
for all
t\geq 1
) we obtain

u\geq\psi=p_{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{2}\omega(r)>0\qquad\mbox{in }B_{r/2},\mbox{ for }t\geq 2C_{1}c_{2}^{-1}\omega(r)r^{-\beta}+r^{2},

and the result follows.
∎

The (almost) quadratic cleaning (
8.1
) will be proved by applying Lemma
8.1
, with different
\omega
and
\beta
, in each of the following cases (recall (
5.7
), (
7.1
), and (
7.2
)):

(i)

If
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{m}
for some
m\leq n-2
, we will use
\omega(r)=o(r^{2})
and
\beta=\varepsilon
;

(ii)

if
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
, we will use
\omega(r)=r^{\lambda^{2nd}}
and
\beta=\lambda^{2nd}-2+\varepsilon
;

(iii)

if
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
, we will use
\omega(r)=r^{3}
and
\beta=1
(in this case we can prove the exact quadratic cleaning without loosing
\varepsilon
in the exponent)

We start with the easiest case (i).

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{m}
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\{u=0\}\cap\bigl(B_{r}\times[r^{2-\varepsilon},1)\bigr)=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,r_{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

As shown in the proof of Lemma
5.8
(a) (see (
5.9
)), we have
{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}}\partial_{t}u(\cdot,-r^{2})\geq c_{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}
in
B_{r}
, for all
r
sufficiently small. Then, the result follows using Lemma
8.1
with
\omega(r)=o(r^{2})
and
\beta=\varepsilon
.
∎

We now consider the case (iii), which is also easier than (ii) .

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\varrho>0
, and let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\cap\bigl(B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho^{2},1)\bigr)
. Then

\{u=0\}\cap\big(B_{r}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}+Cr^{2},1)\big)=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,\varrho),

where
C
is independent of
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
.

To prove Proposition
8.3
, we will need the following result.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\cap B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho^{2},1)
,
and assume (with no loss of generality, up to a rotation in space) that
p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
.
There exist positive
\hat{c}
and
\hat{r}
, independent of
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
, such that

(8.8)

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq\frac{r}{10}\}}\partial_{t}u(x_{\circ}+\cdot,t_{\circ}-r^{2})\geq\hat{c}\,r\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,\hat{r}).

Proof.

Proposition
6.6
applied with
\lambda^{2nd}=3
to the function
\varrho^{-2}u(x_{\circ}+\varrho\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\varrho^{2}\,\cdot\,,)

implies that

(8.9)

\{u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)=0\}\cap B_{\varrho}\subset\bigl\{|x_{n}|\leq C_{1}(|x^{\prime}|^{2}-t)\bigr\}\qquad\forall\,t\in[-\varrho^{2},0].

where
C_{1}
depends only on
n
,
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and
\varrho
.
We now consider a barrier of the form

\phi(x,t):=g\big(x_{n}-C_{1}(|x^{\prime}|^{2}-t)\big).

Note that

\text{\small\bf H}\phi=\big(1+4C_{1}^{2}|x^{\prime}|^{2}\big)g^{\prime\prime}-C_{1}(2n-1)g^{\prime}.

Hence, choosing
g(s)=\left(e^{As}-1\right)\chi_{(0,1)}(s)+\left(e^{A}-1\right)\chi_{(1,\infty)}(s)
with
A
large (as in the standard barrier from Hopf’s Lemma), we see that
\phi\geq 0
is subcaloric inside the domain

D:=\{x_{n}-C_{1}(|x^{\prime}|^{2}-t)<1\}.

Hence, since
\partial_{t}u
is positive and caloric inside
\{u>0\}
, it follows by (
8.9
) and the maximum principle that

\partial_{t}u\geq\bar{c}\,\phi\qquad\text{in }B_{\hat{r}}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}-\hat{r}^{2},t_{\circ}],

where
\bar{c},\hat{r}>0
may depend on
u
and
\varrho
, but may be chosen independently of
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
.
Using the explicit formula for
\phi
, (
8.8
) follows easily.
∎

As a consequence, we get:

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\cap B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1)
. Then

\frac{r^{3}}{C}\leq H\big(r,(u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2})\big)^{1/2}\leq Cr^{3}

for all
r\in(0,\varrho)
, where
C>0
may depend on
u
and
\varrho
, but is independent of
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
.

Proof.

Choosing
\gamma=4
,
Corollary
5.9
and Lemma
5.6
(b) applied with
R=\varrho
imply that

H(r,u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\leq Cr^{6}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,\varrho).

Viceversa, the opposite inequality follows easily by integrating in time the estimate in Lemma
8.4
, using that
\partial_{t}(u-p_{2})=\partial_{t}u\geq 0
.∎

We can now prove Proposition
8.3
.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

In view of Lemma
8.4
, the result follows from Lemma
8.1
with
\omega(r)=Cr^{3}
and
\beta=1
.
∎

Finally, we consider the remaining case (ii).

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\{u=0\}\cap\big(B_{r}\times[r^{2-\varepsilon},1)\big)=\emptyset\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad r\in(0,r_{\varepsilon}).

To prove Proposition
8.6
we will need the following:

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{<3}
, and assume that
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq\frac{r}{10}\}}\partial_{t}u(\,\cdot\,,-r^{2})\geq r^{\lambda^{2nd}-2+\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{ in }B_{r},\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad r\in(0,r_{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

Let
w=u-p_{2}
. For any
r_{k}\downarrow 0
there exists a subsequence
r_{k_{\ell}}\downarrow 0
such that

\tilde{w}_{r_{k_{\ell}}}=\frac{w_{r_{k_{\ell}}}}{H(r_{k_{\ell}},w\zeta)^{1/2}}\rightarrow q,

where
q
satisfies the properties stated in Lemma
7.1
.
In particular, for any such limit
q
, we have
\partial_{t}q\geq 0
and
\partial_{t}q\not\equiv 0
. This implies the existence of a constant
c>0
such that

\int_{B_{1}\times[-2,-3/2]\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq 1/5\}}\frac{\partial_{t}w_{r_{k_{\ell}}}}{H(r_{k_{\ell}},w\zeta)^{1/2}}\geq c.

Also, by a compactness argument, the constant
c
can be chosen to be independent of any subsequence, therefore

\int_{B_{1}\times[-2,-3/2]\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq 1/5\}}\frac{\partial_{t}w_{r}}{H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}}\geq c>0\qquad\forall\,r>0.

Hence, since
w_{r}
is caloric in
\big(B_{2}\times(-2,0)\big)\cap\big\{|x_{n}|\geq{\textstyle\frac{1}{20}}\big\}
for
r\ll 1
, the classical Harnack inequality for the heat equation implies that

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(B_{r}\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq\frac{r}{10}\})\times\{-r^{2}\}}\frac{\partial_{t}u}{H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}}={\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(B_{1}\cap\{|x_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{10}\})\times\{-1\}}\frac{\partial_{t}w_{r}}{H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}}\geq c>0.

Recalling that
H(r,w\zeta)^{1/2}\geq c_{\varepsilon}r^{\lambda^{2nd}+\varepsilon/2}
(this follows from Lemma
5.6
(b) with
\gamma>3
), the result follows.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
8.6
.

Proof of Proposition
8.6
.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
8.5
, we get
H(r,u-p_{2})\leq Cr^{2\lambda^{2nd}}
.
Hence, in view of Lemma
8.7
, the result follows from Lemma
8.1
with
\omega(r)=Cr^{\lambda^{2nd}}
and
\beta=\lambda^{2nd}-2+\varepsilon
.
∎

Combining Propositions
8.2
,
8.3
, and
8.6
, we immediately deduce the following:

Corollary 8.8
.

For any
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\rho=\rho(x_{\circ},t_{\circ},\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
such that

\{u=0\}\cap\big(B_{r}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}+r^{2-\varepsilon},1)\big)=\emptyset\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad r\in(0,\rho).

Finally, to prove Theorem
1.1
, we will also need the following simple GMT lemma:

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-1,1)
with

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E)\big)\leq\beta.

Assume that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in E
there exists
\rho=\rho(\varepsilon,x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
such that

\big\{(x,t)\in B_{\rho}(x_{\circ})\times(-1,1)\ :\ t-t_{\circ}>|x-x_{\circ}|^{2-\varepsilon}\big\}\cap E=\emptyset.

Then
\dim_{\rm par}(E)\leq\beta
.

Proof.

Fix
\beta^{\prime}>\beta
.
We need to show that, for any given
\delta>0
, the set
E
can be covered by countably many of cylinders
B_{r_{i}}(x_{i})\times(t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},t_{i}+r_{i}^{2})
so that

(8.10)

\sum_{i}r_{i}^{\beta^{\prime}}\leq\delta.

Choose
\varepsilon:=(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)/3
,
\beta^{\prime\prime}:=\beta+\varepsilon
, and decompose

E=\cup_{\ell\geq 1}E_{\ell},\quad\mbox{where}\quad E_{\ell}:=\big\{(x,t)\in E\ :\ 2^{-\ell+1}\geq\rho(\varepsilon,x,t)>2^{-\ell}\big\}.

Now, given a pair of points
(x,t)
and
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
belonging to
E_{\ell}
, with
|x-x^{\prime}|<2^{-\ell}
,
by applying Corollary
8.8
at both points we deduce that

|t-t^{\prime}|\leq|x-x^{\prime}|^{2-\varepsilon}
.
This proves that, for all
(x,t)\in E_{\ell}
and
r\in(0,2^{-\ell})
, we have

(8.11)

E_{\ell}\cap\pi^{-1}_{x}(B_{r}(x))\subset B_{r}\times(t-r^{2-\varepsilon},t+r^{2-\varepsilon}).

Now, the assumption
\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E)\big)\leq\beta
implies
H^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(\pi_{x}(E_{\ell}))=0
.
Thus, for any given
\ell
, there exists a family of balls
\{B_{r_{k}}(x_{k})\}_{k}
,
with
r_{k}\in(0,2^{-\ell})
, such that

\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})\subset\bigcup_{k}B_{r_{k}}(x_{k})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\sum_{k}r_{k}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\leq\delta 2^{-\ell}.

Noting that
(-r^{2-\varepsilon},r^{2+\varepsilon})
can be covered by
r^{-\varepsilon}
many intervals of length
2r^{2}
, (
8.11
) implies that

E_{\ell}\cap\pi^{-1}_{x}(B_{r_{k}}(x_{k}))
can be covered by cylinders of the form

\{B_{r_{k}}(x_{k})\times(t_{j,k}-r_{k}^{2},t_{j,k}+r_{k}^{2})\}_{j\in I_{k}}
, with
\#I_{k}\leq r_{k}^{-\varepsilon}
.
This gives a covering of
E_{\ell}
such that

\sum_{k}\sum_{j\in I_{k}}r_{k}^{\beta^{\prime}}\leq r_{k}^{-\varepsilon}\sum_{k}r_{k}^{\beta^{\prime}}=\sum_{k}r_{k}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\leq\delta 2^{-\ell}.

Taking the union over
\ell\geq 1
of all these coverings, since
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\delta 2^{-\ell}=\delta
, we obtain a covering for
E
satisfying (
8.10
).
∎

We are now in position to prove one of our main results.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The result follows from Proposition
3.2
, Corollary
8.8
, and Lemma
8.9
.
∎

9.
Cubic blow-ups

The following lemma classifies possible 2nd blow-ups at points of
\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
q
be a 3-homogeneous solution of (
6.17
), with
\{p_{2}=0\}=\{x_{n}=0\}
.
Then

(9.1)

q(x,t)=a|x_{n}|\bigg(x_{n}^{2}+6bt-3\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n-1}b_{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}\bigg)+\bar{a}x_{n}\bigg(x_{n}^{2}+6\bar{b}t-3\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n-1}\bar{b}_{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}\bigg)\,,

where
a\geq 0
,
(b_{\alpha\beta})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}
is nonnegative definite, and
b={\rm trace}(b_{\alpha\beta})
,
\bar{b}={\rm trace}(\bar{b}_{\alpha\beta})
.

As we shall see below, Lemma
9.1
follows easily from the following result.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous and
\lambda
-homogeneous function with polynomial growth, such that
q|_{\{x_{n}=0\}}\geq 0
and
\text{\small\bf H}q
is a locally bounded signed measure concentrated on
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
If
\lambda>0
is an odd integer, then
q\equiv 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
.

Proof.

For simplicity we give the proof in the case
\lambda=3
, which anyhow is the only one relevant for our purposes. The interested reader will notice that the proof for
\lambda=5,7,9...
is identical, but using the functions
Q
constructed in
[
FRS20
, Lemma B.5]
.

Let
x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})
and define

Q(x):=|x_{n}|\big(3|x^{\prime}|^{2}-(n-1)x_{n}^{2}\big).

Note that, since
\text{\small\bf H}q=\mu
and
q
is
\lambda
-homonegenous,
q(x,-1)
satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{OU}q+\frac{\lambda}{2}q=\mu\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}

with
\lambda=3
, where
\mathcal{L}_{OU}
is the operator defined in (
5.10
).
On the other hand, an explicit computation shows that

\mathcal{L}_{OU}Q+\frac{\lambda}{2}Q=6|x^{\prime}|^{2}\mathcal{H}^{n-1}|_{\{x_{n}=0\}}\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Hence, since
\mu
is concentrated on
\{x_{n}=0\}
,
using integration by parts (which is justified by the polynomial growth of
q
and exponential decay of the Gaussian kernel) and denoting
dm=G(x,-1)dx
the Gaussian density,
we obtain

\displaystyle 6\int_{\{x_{n}=0\}}q|x^{\prime}|^{2}G(x,-1)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}q\,\big(\mathcal{L}_{OU}+{\textstyle\frac{\lambda}{2}}\big)Q\,dm=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big(\mathcal{L}_{OU}+{\textstyle\frac{\lambda}{2}}\big)q\,Qdm=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x,-1)\,Q\,d\mu=0,

where in the last equality we used that
\mu
is supported on
\{x_{n}=0\}
, where
Q
vanishes.

Thus
\int_{\{x_{n}=0\}}q|x^{\prime}|^{2}G(x,-1)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}=0
, and since
q\geq 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
, this forces
q\equiv 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
, as wanted.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
9.1
.

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

Thanks to Lemma
9.2
, we know that
q
vanishes on
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
Thus
q|_{\{x_{n}>0\}}
and
q|_{\{x_{n}<0\}}
are 3-homogenous caloric functions vanishing on
\{x_{n}=0\}
and therefore, by Liouville Theorem,
q
must be of the form (
9.1
) satisfying
b={\rm trace}(b_{\alpha\beta})
and
\bar{b}={\rm trace}(\bar{b}_{\alpha\beta})
.
Recalling that
q
solves (
6.17
), and hence it is a supercaloric function, we obtain the extra conditions that
a\geq 0
and that
(b_{\alpha\beta})
is nonnegative definite.
∎

Our next result is the following monotonicity formula.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
with
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})^{2}
. Let
Q
be a 3-homogeneous solution of (
6.17
).
Then

(9.2)

\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}(u-p_{2})\zeta\,Q\,G\right)\geq-C\|Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\,,

where
C
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

Proof.

After scaling we have

\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}(u-p_{2})\zeta\,Q\,G=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}QG,

therefore

\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}(u-p_{2})\zeta\,Q\,G\right)=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}Z\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}\,Q\,G-\frac{3}{r^{4}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}\,Q\,G,

where
Z
is defined in Section
2.1
.
Recall the integration by parts identities from Lemma
4.2
,

\int_{\{t=-1\}}Zf\,g\,G=\int_{\{t=-1\}}f\,Zg\,G+2\int_{\{t=-1\}}(\text{\small\bf H}f\,g-f\,\text{\small\bf H}g)\,G.

Since
ZQ=3Q
,
\text{\small\bf H}Q=2At\,\mathcal{H}^{n-1}|_{\{x_{n}=0\}}\leq 0
,
u-p_{2}=u\geq 0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
,
and
\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})=\chi_{\{u=0\}}
, this yields

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}(u-p_{2})\zeta\,Q\,G\right)&=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big\{\text{\small\bf H}\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}\,Q-\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}\,\text{\small\bf H}Q\big\}\,G\\
&\geq\frac{1}{r^{4}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}\text{\small\bf H}\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)_{r}\,Q\,G\\
&=\frac{1}{r^{7}}\int_{\{x\in B_{1/2},\ t=-r^{2}\}}r^{2}\chi_{\{u=0\}}\,Q\,G+O(e^{-1/r}).\end{split}

Note that
Q(x)\leq C_{1}|x_{n}||x|^{2}
with
C_{1}:=C_{n}\|Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}
(see (
9.1
)).
Also, since
\lambda^{2nd}=3
, it follows by Proposition
6.6
that

\{u=0\}\cap\{|x|<1/2,t=-r^{2}\}\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq C_{2}(|x^{\prime}|+r)^{2}\},

where
C_{2}
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
. Hence,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{7}}\int_{\{x\in B_{1/2},\ t=-r^{2}\}}r^{2}\chi_{\{u=0\}}\,Q\,G\geq-\frac{C_{1}}{r^{5}}\int_{\{x\in B_{1/2},\ t=-r^{2}\}}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq C_{2}(|x^{\prime}|+r)^{2}\}}|x_{n}||x|^{2}G\geq-C,\end{split}

where the constant
C
depends only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and the lemma follows.
∎

As a consequence, we find:

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and assume that
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
.
Then the limit

\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{(u-p_{2})_{r}}{r^{3}}\qquad\mbox{in }\,W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]\big)

exists in the weak topology, and it is of the form (
9.1
).

Proof.

Given any sequence
r_{k}\downarrow 0
, by Proposition
6.7
and Corollary
8.5
there exists subsequence such that

r_{k_{\ell}}^{-3}(u-p_{2})_{r_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup Q\qquad\mbox{in }\,W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]\big),

where
Q
is some 3-homogeneous solution of (
6.17
).
Now, assume that we have two limits along different sequences:

(9.3)

\big(r_{k}^{(1)}\big)^{-3}w_{r_{k}^{(1)}}\rightarrow Q^{(1)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\big(r_{k}^{(1)}\big)^{-3}w_{r_{k}^{(2)}}\rightarrow Q^{(2)}.

Then, by Lemma
9.3
, for fixed
j\in\{1,2\}
we have that

\left(\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-1\}}r^{-3}\big((u-p_{2})\zeta\big)Q^{(j)}G\right)+C\|Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}r

is nondecreasing in
r
.
Hence, using (
9.3
), and letting
r_{k}^{(j)}\to 0
, we deduce that

\int_{\{t=-1\}}Q^{(1)}Q^{(j)}G=\int_{\{t=-1\}}Q^{(2)}Q^{(j)}G\quad\text{for $j=1,2$}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\int_{\{t=-1\}}(Q^{(1)}-Q^{(2)})^{2}G=0.

This implies that
Q^{(1)}\equiv Q^{(2)}
on
\{t=-1\}
and hence, by homogeneity, in all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]
.
∎

We now investigate the structure of the second blow-ups at at “most points” of
\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
.
For this, we need a new dimension reduction lemma.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and let
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
.
Assume there exists a sequence of singular points
(x_{k},t_{k})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
, with
|x_{k}|\leq r_{k}
for some
r_{k}\downarrow 0
, and
t_{k}\leq 0
. Assume also that
\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\rightarrow q
, where
w:=u-p_{2}
, and that
y_{k}:=\frac{x_{k}}{r_{k}}\to y_{\infty}\neq 0
,
and let
q^{even}
denote the even symmetrisation of
q
with respect to the hyperplane
\{p_{2}=0\}
.

Then
y_{\infty}\in\{p_{2}=0\}
, and
q^{even}(x^{\prime},x_{n})
is translation invariant in the direction of
y_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
.
Since
(0,0)\in\Sigma\subset\{u=0\}
, it follows from Lemma
8.3
applied at
(x_{k},t_{k})
that

(0,0)\not\in B_{r}(x_{k})\times[t_{k}+Cr^{2},1)\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,1/2),

where
C
is independent of
k
. As a consequence, choosing
r=r_{k}
and recalling that
|x_{k}|\leq r_{k}
, we get

(9.4)

-Cr_{k}^{2}\leq-C|x_{k}|^{2}\leq t_{k}\leq 0.

Let
s_{k}:=\frac{t_{k}}{r_{k}^{2}}
. Up to taking a subsequence we may assume that
(y_{k},s_{k})\to(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})
, where
-C\leq s_{\infty}\leq 0
.
Also, by Lemma
7.5
we obtain that
y_{\infty}\in\{p_{2}=0\}
.

Next, define
P_{k}:=r_{k}^{2}\big(p_{2}(y_{k}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{k},t_{k}}\big)

(note that this is a harmonic polynomial of degree two), and set

\begin{split}w_{r_{k}}(y_{k}+\cdot,s_{k}+\cdot)+P_{k}&=u(x_{k}+r_{k}\cdot,t_{k}+r_{k}^{2}\,\cdot\,)-r_{k}^{2}p_{2}(y_{k}+\cdot)+r_{k}^{2}\big(p_{2}(y_{k}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{k},t_{k}}\big)\\
&=u(x_{k}+r_{k}\cdot,t_{k}+r_{k}^{2}\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{k},t_{k}}(r_{k}\,\cdot\,)=:\bar{w}_{k}\end{split}

Since
(x_{k},t_{k})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
we have
\phi(0^{+}\bar{w}_{k})=3
and therefore, by Lemma
5.6
,

(9.5)

c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{H(\varrho^{\prime},\bar{w}_{k}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))}{H(\varrho,\bar{w}_{k}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))}\leq C_{\delta}\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6+\delta}\qquad\negmedspace\negmedspace\mbox{and}\qquad c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{H(\varrho^{\prime},w_{r_{k}}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))}{H(\varrho,w_{r_{k}}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))}\leq C_{\delta}\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6+\delta},

whenever
0<\varrho<\varrho^{\prime}\ll r_{k}^{-1}
.
Also, by Lemma
6.3
, for all
0<\varrho\ll r_{k}^{-1}
we have

(9.6)

H(\varrho,\bar{w}_{k}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))\asymp{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(\bar{w}_{k})^{2}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad H(\varrho,w_{r_{k}}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))\asymp{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(w_{r_{k}})^{2},

where
X\asymp Y
here means
X\leq CY
and
Y\leq CX
with
C
depending only on
n
and
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

Now, recall
0\geq s_{k}\to s_{\infty}>-\infty
and
y_{k}\to y_{\infty}\in\overline{B}_{1}\cap\{p_{2}=0\}
, define
a_{k}:=H(1,w_{r_{k}}\zeta(r_{k}\cdot))^{1/2}
and
b_{k}:=\|P_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}
, and let us show that
b_{k}\leq Ca_{k}
as
k\to\infty
.
Indeed, using (
9.5
)-(
9.6
) with
\varrho=1
and
\varrho^{\prime}=R\gg 1
(we want
R^{2}
to be larger than, say,
-2s_{\infty}
), we get

\|\bar{w}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{R})}\leq\|w_{r_{k}}(y_{k}+\cdot,s_{k}+\cdot)+P_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{R})}+\leq\|w_{r_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2R})}+C(R)\|P_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}\leq C(R)(a_{k}+b_{k}).

We now claim that
b_{k}\leq Ca_{k}.
Indeed, if by contradiction
b_{k}\gg a_{k}
as
k\to\infty
then, up to subsequence (note that any sequence of quadratic polynomials bounded in
L^{2}
is pre-compact),

\lim_{k}\frac{\tilde{w}_{k}}{a_{k}+b_{k}}=\lim_{k}\frac{P_{k}}{b_{k}}=:\tilde{P}=\,[\mbox{second order harmonic polynomial}]\qquad\text{in $L^{2}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]\big)$. }

On the other hand, it follows by (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) that, for
0<\varrho<\varrho^{\prime}\ll r_{k}^{-1}
,

(9.7)

c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}\left(\frac{\bar{w}_{k}}{a_{k}+b_{k}}\right)^{2}}{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}\left(\frac{\bar{w}_{k}}{a_{k}+b_{k}}\right)^{2}}.

Taking the limit this yields

c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}(\tilde{P})^{2}}{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(\tilde{P})^{2}}\qquad\forall\,0<\varrho<\varrho^{\prime},

a contradiction since
\tilde{P}
is quadratic (and hence cannot satisfy a cubic growth).

This proves that
b_{k}\leq Ca_{k}
, hence

\left\|\frac{P_{k}(\cdot-y_{k})}{a_{k}}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}\leq C,\quad\mbox{ and therefore }\quad\frac{P_{k}(\cdot-y_{k})}{a_{k}}=:\bar{P}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{P}_{\infty}

as
k\to\infty
(up to a subsequence).
Now, a simple computation —see the proof of (3.11) in
[
FS17
, Lemma 3.3]
— shows that

(9.8)

\bar{P}_{\infty}\mbox{ is odd with respect to $\{p_{2}=0\}$}.

Therefore, since
\frac{w_{r_{k}}}{a_{k}}=\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}\to q
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
, using again (
9.7
) (now with
a_{k}
instead of
a_{k}+b_{k}
in the denominator) we obtain

c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{k},s_{k})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}(\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}+\bar{P}_{k})^{2}}{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{k},s_{k})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(\tilde{w}_{r_{k}}+\bar{P}_{k})^{2}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad c\bigg(\frac{\varrho^{\prime}}{\varrho}\bigg)^{\negmedspace 6}\leq\frac{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}(q+\bar{P}_{\infty})^{2}}{{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(q+\bar{P}_{\infty})^{2}}.

In particular, choosing
\varrho^{\prime}=1
and taking even parts, (
9.8
) implies that

(9.9)

\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(q+\bar{P}_{\infty})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C\varrho^{3}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})+\mathscr{C}_{\varrho}}(q^{even})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C\varrho^{3}\qquad\forall\,\varrho\in(0,1).

Note now that, thanks to Proposition
6.7
,

q
(and therefore also
q^{even}
) is a
3
-homogeneous solution of the parabolic Signorini problem in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]
with obstacle zero on
\{p_{2}=0\}
. Then, (
9.9
) shows that

(y_{\infty},s_{\infty})
is a singular point for
q^{even}
of homogeneity at least three. In addition, since
q^{even}
is 3-homogeenous we have

3\leq\phi\big(0^{+},q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)\big)\leq\phi\big(\infty,q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)\big)=\phi(\infty,q)=3.

This implies that
r\mapsto\phi\big(r,q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)\big)
is constantly equal to 3, therefore
q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)
is also 3-homogeneous. In particular

q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)\equiv\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{-3}q^{even}(y_{\infty}+R\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+R^{2}\,\cdot\,)\equiv q.

This implies
that
q^{even}
is translation invariant in the direction
y_{\infty}
,
7
7

7

A possible way to show this, is the following.
By homogeneity of
q^{even}(y_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}+\,\cdot\,)
and
q^{even}
, it holds

q^{even}(\delta y_{\infty}+x,\delta^{2}s_{\infty}+t)=\delta^{3}q^{even}(y_{\infty}+(x/\delta),s_{\infty}+(t/\delta^{2}))=\delta^{3}q^{even}(x/\delta,t/\delta^{2})=q^{even}(x,t)\qquad\forall\,\delta>0.

Subtracting
q(x,t)
to both sides, dividing by
\delta
, and sending
\delta\downarrow 0
we obtain
y_{\infty}\cdot\nabla q(x,t)\equiv 0
.
concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

For the sequel, it will be useful to introduce the following:

Definition 9.6
.

Given
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
a bounded solution of (
3.1
), we define
\Sigma^{*}\subset\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
as the set of singular points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
such that, in some coordinate system where
p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})^{2}
, we have that

p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*}:=\lim_{k}\frac{u(x_{\circ}+r\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+r^{2}\,\cdot\,)-r^{2}\,p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}}{r^{3}}

can be written as

a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}|x_{n}|\big(x_{n}^{2}+6t\big)+p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}},

for some
a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq 0
and
p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
an odd 3-homogeneous caloric polynomial.

The motivation of the previous definition is given by the following lemma, which follows from Lemma
9.5
and Proposition
7.6
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
).
Then

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\setminus\Sigma^{*})\big)\leq n-2.

Proof.

We begin by observing the following: Let
Q=Q(x,t)
be an 3-homogenous solution of the parabolic Signorini problem, which is even with respect to
\{x_{n}=0\}
and it is invariant with respect to translations parallel to
\{x_{n}=0\}
. Then
Q
depends on the variables
x_{n}
and
t
, and hence it must be of the form

(9.10)

Q(x,t)=a|x_{n}|\bigg(\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{6}+t\bigg),

for some
a\geq 0
.
We now show that this implies the following:

Claim.
Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\setminus\Sigma^{*}
, and
(x_{k}^{(j)},t_{k}^{(j)})
a sequence of singular points in
\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}
, with
t_{k}^{(j)}\leq t_{\circ}
, such that
y_{k}^{(j)}:=\frac{x_{k}^{(j)}-x_{\circ}}{r_{k}}\to y_{\infty}^{(j)}\neq 0
. Then

y_{\infty}^{(j)}\in\{x_{n}=0\}
, and the set
\big\{y_{\infty}^{(j)}\big\}_{j}
has rank at most
n-2
.

Indeed, applying Corollary
9.4
and Lemma
9.5
to the function
u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)
, we deduce that

\frac{u(x_{\circ}+r_{k}\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+r_{k}^{2}\,\cdot\,)-r_{k}^{2}\,p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}}{r_{k}^{3}}\to q

and
y_{\infty}^{(j)}\in\{x_{n}=0\}
.
Then, if the rank of
\big\{y_{\infty}^{(j)}\big\}_{j}
was
n-1
, Lemma
9.5
would imply that

q^{even}
is of the form (
9.10
).
Recalling Definition
9.6
, this would mean that
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}
, a contradiction.

Thanks to the Claim, we can apply Proposition
7.6
to deduce the desired dimensional bound on
\pi_{x}(\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\setminus\Sigma^{*})
(more precisely, since the control that we have now is for “points from the past” instead of on “points in the future”, we need to apply
Proposition
7.6
to the set
E:=\{(x,-t)\,:\,(x,t)\in\Sigma_{n-1}^{=3}\setminus\Sigma^{*}\}
).
∎

We now show that, for any
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}
, the coefficient
a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
must be strictly positive.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), and
\varrho\in(0,1)
. There exists
c_{\varrho}>0
such that the following holds: For any

(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}\cap B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho^{2},1)
, let
a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
be the coefficient of the non-caloric part of
p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*}
, as in Definition
9.6
. Then
a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\geq c_{\varrho}
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}\cap B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho^{2},1)
.
By Lemma
8.4
, there exist positive constants
c
and
r_{\circ}
such that

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{r}}\partial_{t}u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}-r^{2})\geq cr\qquad\mbox{ in }B_{r},\quad\forall\,r\in(0,r_{\circ}).

Equivalently, defining
w:=u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
and noting that
\partial_{t}w=\partial_{t}u\geq 0
, we have

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{1}\times\{-1\}}\partial_{t}(r^{-3}w_{r})\geq c>0\qquad\,\quad\forall\,r\in(0,r_{\circ}).

Taking the limit as
r\to 0
,
we deduce that

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{1}\times\{-1\}}\partial_{t}\big(a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}|x_{n}|\big(x_{n}^{2}+6t\big)+p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\big)\geq c>0.

Since
p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
is odd, so is
\partial_{t}p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
and therefore
{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{B_{1}\times\{-1\}}\partial_{t}p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=0
. The lemma follows.
∎

10.
A useful monotone quantity

Let us define a smoothed version of
H
given by

(10.1)

\tilde{H}(\varrho,w):=\int_{1}^{2}H(\varrho\theta,w)d\theta

The goal of this section is to establish the following monotonicity formula. This will be crucial in the next sections in order to establish higher order regularity at (most) singular points in
\Sigma^{*}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
), let
(0,0)\in\Sigma^{*}
,
and assume that

p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
and
p_{3}^{*}=\overline{a}|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)+p_{3}
,.

Given
C_{\circ}>0
and
M\in(6,7)
, there exist
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
and
R_{\circ}\geq 1
such that the following holds:
Assume that there exists
r\in(0,\varepsilon_{\circ})
such that

(10.2)

\|u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{\circ}\rho}\times(-2\varrho^{2},\varrho^{2}/2))}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}\varrho^{3}\quad\mbox{ for all }\varrho\in(0,r).

Denote
w:=(u-p_{2}-Q)\zeta
with
Q=x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)
, where
\bar{a}\in[0,C_{\circ}]
and
Q_{2}
is any 2-homogeneous polynomial satisfying
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{2})=0
and
\|Q_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}
.
Then

(10.3)

\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w):=\max_{R\in[1,1/\varrho]}R^{-M}\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(R\varrho,w)

is monotone nondecreasing for
\varrho\in(0,r)
.

The idea behind the quantity
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w)
is the following.
Ideally, we would like to have that
\varrho^{-6}H(\varrho,w)
is monotone at every point.
Unfortunately this is false at points in
\Sigma^{*}
because
p_{3}^{*}
is not caloric.
This means that we must look for a monotone quantity of lower order, and this is why we look at a quantity of the form
\varrho^{-5}H(\varrho,w)
.
Our monotone quantity
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}
is basically a modified version of this, in which we first consider an averaged version of
H
, given by (
10.1
), and then define
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}
as in (
10.3
) in order to control the growth between different scales (see (
10.5
)).
It is thanks to these (small but crucial) modifications that we can prove that the quantity is monotone.

Before proving the monotonicity formula, we need the following:

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
).
Given positive constants
c_{\circ}
,
r_{\circ}
,
C_{\circ}
, and
R_{\circ}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds:
Let
Q_{2}
be any 2-homogeneous polynomial such that
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{2})=0
and
\|Q_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}
.
Assume that

\frac{1}{r^{3}}\bigg(u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)-r^{2}\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{2}\bigg)\leq c_{\circ}|x_{n}|t+C_{\circ}|x_{n}|^{3}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x^{\prime})+\delta\quad\mbox{inside }\big\{|x|\leq 2R_{\circ},-4\leq t\leq-1\big\}.

for all
r\in(0,r_{\circ})
.
Then

u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\leq Cr^{4}\quad\mbox{ on }\ \{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{|x|\leq R_{\circ},t=-1\},

for all
r\in(0,r_{\circ})
.

Proof.

Fix
z\in\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\{|x|\leq R_{\circ},\,t=-1\}
, and define

\phi(x,t):=-nx_{n}^{2}+|x^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|^{2}-2(1+t)+x_{n}Q_{2}(x^{\prime})

and

v(x,t):=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\bigg(u(rx,r^{2}t)-r^{2}\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{2}\bigg).

Also, consider the set
U:=\{|x^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|\leq 1,\,|x_{n}|\leq\varrho,\,-2\leq t\leq-1\}
, with
\varrho>0
to be fixed.
We choose first
\varrho>0
and then
\delta>0
, both conveniently small, so that for all
x\in\partial_{par}U
we have

\begin{split}v(x)&\leq c_{\circ}|x_{n}|t+C_{\circ}|x_{n}|^{3}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+\delta\\
&\leq\begin{cases}-c_{\circ}\varrho+C_{\circ}\varrho^{3}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+\delta\leq-2n\varrho^{2}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)\leq\phi(x)\quad&\mbox{for }|x_{n}|=\varrho\\
-c_{\circ}|x_{n}|+C_{\circ}\varrho^{3}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+\delta\leq-2n\varrho^{2}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+1\leq\phi(x)&\mbox{for }|x_{n}|\leq\varrho,|x^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|=1\\
C_{\circ}\varrho^{3}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+\delta\leq-2n\varrho^{2}+x_{n}Q_{2}(x)+2\leq\phi(x)&\mbox{for }|x_{n}|\leq\varrho,t=-2.\end{cases}\end{split}

Now, consider the function

\psi(x):=\frac{1}{2r}x_{n}^{2}+\phi(x)+Cr,

where
C
is a large enough constant, chosen so that
\psi
is nonnegative in
U
(note that
\frac{1}{2r}x_{n}^{2}+Cr\geq\sqrt{C}|x_{n}|
, so such a constant
C
exists independently of
r
).
Since
\text{\small\bf H}\phi=0
, we have
\text{\small\bf H}\psi\leq\frac{1}{r}
in
U
. Also, by the argument above,
\frac{1}{r^{3}}u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\leq\psi
on
\partial_{par}U
.
Therefore, since
\psi\geq 0
in
U
and
\text{\small\bf H}\big(\frac{1}{r^{3}}u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big)\geq\frac{1}{r}
whenever
\frac{1}{r^{3}}u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)>0
, it follows from the comparison principle that

\frac{1}{r^{3}}u(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\leq\psi\quad\mbox{in }U.

In particular, choosing
x=z
and
t=-1
we get (recall that
z_{n}=0
)

\frac{1}{r^{3}}u(rz,-r^{2})\leq\psi(z,-1)=Cr,

and the lemma follows.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
10.1
.

Proof of Proposition
10.1
.

Note that
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\cdot,w)
is a Lipschitz function, so it suffices to prove that its derivative is nonnegative at every differentiability point.
Thus, we compute

\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w)
and we note that two possible alternatives arise:

(a)
\max_{R\in[1,1/\varrho_{\circ}]}R^{-M}\tilde{H}(R\varrho_{\circ},w)>\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w)
;

(b)
\max_{R\in[1,1/\varrho_{\circ}]}R^{-M}\tilde{H}(R\varrho_{\circ},w)=\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w)
.

If
(a)
holds, consider
R_{\circ}\in(1,1/\varrho_{\circ}]
such that
\max_{R\in[1,1/\varrho_{\circ}]}R^{-M}\tilde{H}(R\varrho_{\circ},w)=R_{\circ}^{-M}\tilde{H}(R_{\circ}\varrho_{\circ},w)
.
By continuity, for
s\in(0,1)
sufficiently close to
1
we have

\varrho_{\circ}^{5}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho_{\circ},w)=(R_{\circ})^{-M}\tilde{H}(R_{\circ}\varrho_{\circ},w)=\max_{R^{\prime}\in[s,1/\varrho_{\circ}]}(R^{\prime})^{-M}\tilde{H}(R^{\prime}\varrho_{\circ},w)\\
=s^{-M}\max_{R\in[1,1/(s\varrho_{\circ})]}R^{-M}\tilde{H}(Rs\varrho_{\circ},w)=s^{-M}(s\varrho_{\circ})^{5}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(s\varrho_{\circ},w).

Hence

\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(s\varrho_{\circ},w)=s^{M-5}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho_{\circ},w)\qquad\text{for $s\in(0,1)$ sufficiently close to $1$,}

and therefore
\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w)=\frac{M-5}{\varrho_{\circ}}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho_{\circ},w)\geq 0
. This proves the result whenever
(a)
holds.

Instead, when (b) holds, we note that
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w)\geq\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)
with equality for
\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}
. Therefore

\frac{\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho,w)-\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\varrho_{\circ},w)}{\varrho-\varrho_{\circ}}\geq\frac{\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)-\varrho_{\circ}^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w)}{\varrho-\varrho_{\circ}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\frac{d}{d\varrho}\bigg|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\cdot,w)=\frac{d}{d\varrho}\bigg|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\big(\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\rho,w)\big).

In addition, if (b) holds then we have the extra growth information

(10.4)

\tilde{H}(R\varrho_{\circ},w)\leq\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w)R^{M}\quad\mbox{for all }R\in[1,1/\varrho_{\circ}],

and in particular, taking
R=1/\varrho_{\circ}
, we get

(10.5)

\varrho_{\circ}^{M}\tilde{H}(1,w)\leq\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w).

Therefore, to conclude the proof of the lemma, it is enough to show that
\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\big(\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)\big)\geq 0
at a every scale
\varrho_{\circ}
where (
10.4
) holds. We divide the proof of this fact in four steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. Denote

v=(u-p_{2})\zeta,\qquad h=(x_{n}Q_{2}+\overline{a}\Psi)\zeta,\qquad\Psi(x,t):=|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t),

where
\zeta
is the usual spatial cut-off.
Recalling that
w=v-h
we first show that, at any small enough scale
\varrho_{\circ}
at which (
10.4
) holds, we have

(10.6)

\frac{d}{d\varrho}\bigg|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\big(\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)\big)\geq\frac{1}{\varrho_{\circ}^{6}}\bigg(\frac{\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},v-h)}{2}+4\int_{-2\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}^{-\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big\{v\text{\small\bf H}v-(v-h)\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\big\}G\biggr]\,dt\bigg)

Observe that, by (
10.1
), we have

(10.7)

\frac{d}{d\varrho}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w):=\int_{1}^{2}\frac{dH}{d\varrho}(\varrho\theta,w)\,\theta\,d\theta,

so we need to compute
\frac{d}{d\varrho}H(\varrho\theta,w)
.
Using the identity from Lemma
4.2

\langle h,Zv\rangle_{\varrho}=\langle Zh,v\rangle_{\varrho}+2\varrho^{2}\big(\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle\text{\small\bf H}h,v\rangle_{\varrho}\big),

we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\varrho}H(\varrho,v-h)&=\frac{2}{\varrho}\langle v-h,Z(v-h)\rangle_{\varrho}\\
&=\frac{2}{\varrho}\big(\langle v,Zv\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle h,Zv\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle v,Zh\rangle_{\varrho}+\langle h,Zh\rangle_{\varrho}\big)\\
&=\frac{2}{\varrho}\big(\langle v,Zv\rangle_{\varrho}-2\langle v,Zh\rangle_{\varrho}-2\varrho^{2}\big(\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle\text{\small\bf H}h,v\rangle_{\varrho}\big)+\langle h,Zh\rangle_{\varrho}\big)\\
&\geq\frac{2}{\varrho}\big(3\langle v,v\rangle_{\varrho}+2\varrho^{2}\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-6\langle v,h\rangle_{\varrho}-2\varrho^{2}\big(\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle\text{\small\bf H}h,v\rangle_{\varrho}\big)+3\langle h,h\rangle_{\varrho}\big)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}\\
&=\frac{2}{\varrho}\big(3H(\varrho,v-h)+2\varrho^{2}\big(\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}+\langle\text{\small\bf H}h,v\rangle_{\varrho}\big)\big)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}\\
&\geq\frac{2}{\varrho}\big(3H(\varrho,v-h)+2\varrho^{2}\big(2\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle v-h,\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\rangle_{\varrho}-2\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}\big)\big)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}.\end{split}

Here, we used that
Zh=3h
, that
\langle v,Zv\rangle_{\varrho}\geq 3\langle v,v\rangle_{\varrho}+2\varrho^{2}\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-Ce^{-1/\varrho}
(this follows from (
4.3
), Proposition
5.4
, and the fact that the frequency at
(0,0)
is
3
),
and that
\big|\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}h\rangle_{\varrho}\big|\leq Ce^{-1/\varrho}
.
Therefore

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big(\varrho^{-5}H(\varrho,w)\big)&=\frac{1}{\varrho^{6}}\big(\varrho{\textstyle\frac{d}{d\varrho}}H(\varrho,w)-5H(\varrho,w)\big)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{\varrho^{6}}\big(H(\varrho,v-h)+4\varrho^{2}\big(2\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle v-h,\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\rangle_{\varrho}-2\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}\big)\big)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}.\end{split}

Now recall that
v=(u-p_{2})\zeta
and hence

(u-p_{2})\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})\geq 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}\geq-Ce^{-1/\varrho}.

On the other hand, since
\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})=-\chi_{\{u=0\}}
,
h=(x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\psi)\zeta
, and
|x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\psi|\leq C|x_{n}||x|^{2}
, we obtain

\big|\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}\big|\leq\int_{\{t=-\varrho^{2}\}}|h|\,\chi_{\{u=0\}}G+Ce^{-1/\varrho}\leq C\int_{\{t=-\varrho^{2}\}}C|x_{n}|\,|x|^{2}\chi_{\{u=0\}}G+Ce^{-1/\varrho}.

Also, using that (as a consequence of Proposition
6.6
)

(10.8)

\{u=0\}\cap\{|x|<1/2,t=-\varrho^{2}\}\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq C(|x^{\prime}|+\varrho)^{2}\},

we obtain

(10.9)

\big|\langle h,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}\big|\leq\int_{\{t=-\varrho^{2}\}}C(\varrho+|x|)^{4}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq C(|x^{\prime}|+\varrho)^{2}\}}G+Ce^{-1/\varrho}\leq C\varrho^{5}.

Therefore, we have shown that

\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big(\varrho^{-5}H(\varrho,w)\big)\geq\frac{1}{\varrho^{6}}\bigg(H(\varrho,v-h)+4\varrho^{2}\big(\langle v,\text{\small\bf H}v\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle v-h,\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\rangle_{\varrho}\big)-C\varrho^{7}\bigg)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}

Applying this inequality with
H(\varrho\theta,w)
in place of
H(\varrho,w)
, and integrating with respect to
\theta\,d\theta
over
[1,2]
,
we obtain

\frac{d}{d\varrho}\big(\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)\big)\geq\frac{1}{\varrho^{6}}\bigg(\tilde{H}(\varrho,v-h)+4\int_{-2\varrho^{2}}^{-\varrho^{2}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big\{v\text{\small\bf H}v-(v-h)\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\big\}G\bigg]\,dt-C\varrho^{7}\bigg)-Ce^{-1/\varrho}.

Since
\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},v-h)\geq c\varrho_{\circ}^{M}\gg\varrho_{\circ}^{7}\gg Ce^{-1/\varrho_{\circ}}
(recall (
10.5
)), (
10.6
) follows.

\bullet

Step 2
. Let

w_{\varrho_{\circ}}:=(v-h)(\varrho_{\circ}\,\cdot\,,\varrho_{\circ}^{2}\,\cdot\,)=\big((u-p_{2}-p_{3}-\bar{a}\Psi)\zeta\big)(\varrho_{\circ}\,\cdot\,,\varrho_{\circ}^{2}\,\cdot\,).

We now show that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that, if
\varrho_{\circ}
is sufficiently small,

(10.10)

\int_{-2}^{-1}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|\min\{G,\delta\}\bigg)\,dt\leq\varepsilon\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},w_{\varrho_{\circ}}).

This will be used in order to bound the integral in (
10.6
) outside a large ball, as we will see in Step 4 below.

We first note that, inside
B_{1/2}\times(-1,0)
, it holds

v\text{\small\bf H}v=(u-p_{2})\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})\geq 0,\quad h\text{\small\bf H}h=0,\quad\text{and}\quad-v\text{\small\bf H}h=-u\text{\small\bf H}h\geq 0.

Therefore, using (
10.8
) similarly to the argument in the previous step, we get

\begin{split}(u-p_{2}-x_{n}Q_{2}-\bar{a}\Psi)\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2}-x_{n}Q_{2}-\bar{a}\Psi)&\geq-(x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\Psi)\text{\small\bf H}(u-p_{2})\\
&\geq-C(\varrho+|x|)^{4}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq C(|x^{\prime}|+\varrho)^{2}\}}\quad\mbox{in }B_{1/2}\times(-\varrho^{2},0).\end{split}

Hence, after scaling and taking into account the error introduced by the cut-off
\zeta
, for all
\varrho\in(0,1/2)
it holds

w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\geq-C\varrho_{\circ}^{2}\min\big\{(\varrho+\varrho_{\circ}|x|)^{4}\chi_{\{\varrho_{\circ}|x_{n}|\leq C(\varrho+\varrho_{\circ}|x^{\prime}|)^{2}\}},1\big\}\quad\mbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\varrho^{2}/\varrho_{\circ}^{2},0).

Equivalently, setting
\varrho=R\varrho_{\circ}
, for all
R\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2\varrho_{\circ}}\big)
we have

w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\geq-C\varrho_{\circ}^{6}\min\big\{(R+|x|)^{4}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq C\varrho_{\circ}(R+|x^{\prime}|)^{2}\}},\varrho_{\circ}^{-4}\big\}\quad\mbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-R^{2},0).

Integrating this bound for
R\in(1,2)
, we obtain

(10.11)

\int_{-4}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big(w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big)_{-}G\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}C\varrho_{\circ}^{6}(4+|x|)^{4}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq C\varrho_{\circ}(4+|x|)^{2}\}}G\leq C\varrho_{\circ}^{7}.

Now, note that the following identity holds for any function
\phi=\phi(x,t)\in C^{1}_{t}C^{1,1}_{x}
with compact support and for any smooth
\xi=\xi(x,t)
:

(10.12)

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}\phi\,\text{\small\bf H}\phi\,\xi+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}|\nabla\phi|^{2}\xi+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}\phi^{2}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}\phi^{2}(\Delta+\partial_{t})\xi.

In particular, choosing
\phi=w_{\varrho_{\circ}}
and
\xi=G
, we get

(10.13)

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\,\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\,G+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|^{2}G+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G=0.

Integrating between
t=-\theta
and
t=-1
, we obtain

(10.14)

2\int_{-\theta}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\,G\biggr)\,dt+2\int_{-\theta}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|^{2}\,G\biggr)\,dt+\sup_{t\in(-\theta,-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\\
\leq\int_{\{t=-\theta\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\leq\int_{-4}^{-3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G,

where
\theta\in(3,4)
is chosen (thanks to Fubini) so that
\int_{\{t=-\theta\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\leq\int_{-4}^{-3}\int_{B_{2R}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}
.
Hence, since
\int_{-4}^{-3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\leq\tilde{H}(2,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})\leq C\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})
(by the definition of
\tilde{H}
and using (
10.4
) with
R=2
), recalling (
10.11
) we deduce

2\int_{-\theta}^{-1}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|^{2}\,G\bigg)\,dt+\sup_{t\in(-\theta,-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\leq C\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})+C\varrho_{\circ}^{7}\leq C\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}}),

where the last inequality follows from (
10.5
) (indeed, since
M<7
,
\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})\gg\varrho_{\circ}^{7}
for
\varrho_{\circ}>0
small).

In particular, again by Fubini, there exists
\tilde{\theta}\in(2,3)
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-\tilde{\theta}\}}|\nabla w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|^{2}\,G+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-\tilde{\theta}\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}G\leq C\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}}).

We now claim that this implies, thanks to the log-Sobolev inequality, that

(10.15)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{\tilde{\theta}\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}\min\{G,\delta\}\leq\varepsilon(\delta)\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}}),

where
\varepsilon(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.

Indeed, the function
f:=\frac{w_{\varrho_{\circ}}(\tilde{\theta}^{1/2}\,\cdot\,,\tilde{\theta}\,\cdot)}{C\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})^{1/2}}
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla f|^{2}\,dm\leq 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f|^{2}\,dm\leq 1,

where
dm=G(\,\cdot\,,-1)dx
is the Gaussian measure.
Hence, given
\lambda>1
, we can apply the Gaussian log-Sobolev inequality
to
F:=\max\{|f|,1\}
(cp. Lemma
6.4
) to get

\log\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}\log F^{2}dm\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla F|^{2}dm+\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr)\,\log\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{2}dm\biggr)\\
\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla f|^{2}dm+\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}dm\biggr)\,\log\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{2}dm\biggr)\leq 1+2\log 2<3.

Thus, for any
R>0
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}}f^{2}dm\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}\leq\lambda\}}dm+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}}f^{2}\chi_{\{f^{2}>\lambda\}}dm\leq\lambda\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}}dm+\frac{3}{\log\lambda}.

Choosing
\lambda=\lambda_{\varepsilon}
large enough so that
3/\log\lambda_{\varepsilon}<\varepsilon/4
, and then
R_{\varepsilon}>0
so that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\varepsilon}}}dm<\varepsilon/4
, we deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\varepsilon}}}f^{2}dm<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Therefore, (
10.15
) follows by taking
\delta
small enough.

Now, since
(\Delta+\partial_{t})\min\{G,\delta\}\leq 0
, we can use again (
10.12
) with
\phi=w_{\varrho_{\circ}}
and
\xi=\min\{G,\delta\}
to get

(10.16)

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\,\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\,\min\{G,\delta\}+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|^{2}\min\{G,\delta\}+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t\}}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}^{2}\min\{G,\delta\}\leq 0.

Integrating between
t=-\tilde{\theta}
and
t=-1
, exactly as before (but now taking advantage of (
10.12
)) we obtain

\begin{split}2\int_{-\tilde{\theta}}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|\,\min\{G,\delta\}\biggr)\,dt\leq\varepsilon(\delta)\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}})+C\varrho_{\circ}^{7}\leq 2\varepsilon(\delta)\tilde{H}(1,w_{\varrho_{\circ}}),\end{split}

if
\varrho_{\circ}
is sufficiently small.
Since
\tilde{\theta}\in(2,3)
, this concludes the proof of (
10.10
).

\bullet

Step 3
. Let
u_{\varrho}:=u(\varrho\,\cdot\,,\varrho^{2}\,\cdot\,)
. We now show that, for any fixed
R_{\circ}>0
, there exists
r>0
such that

(10.17)
