0\leq-\int_{-2}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}u_{\varrho}\text{\small\bf H}(\varrho^{3}\Psi)\biggr)\,dt\leq C\varrho^{7}\qquad\forall\,\varrho\in(0,r).

Indeed, thanks to Lemma
9.8
(recall that
(p_{3}^{*})^{even}
denotes the even symmetrization with respect to
\{p_{2}=0\}=\{x_{n}=0\}
), it holds

(p_{3}^{*})^{even}=\bar{a}(t|x_{n}|+{\textstyle\frac{1}{6}}|x_{n}|^{3})\qquad\text{with }\bar{a}\geq c_{\circ}>0.

Therefore (
10.2
) implies that

\frac{1}{\varrho^{3}}\bigg(u(\varrho\,\cdot\,,\varrho^{2}\,\cdot\,)-r^{2}\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{2}\bigg)\leq c_{\circ}|x_{n}|t+C|x_{n}|^{3}+p_{3}^{odd}(x)+\varepsilon_{\circ}\qquad\mbox{in }\{|x|\leq 2R_{\circ},-4\leq t\leq-1\},

and so Lemma
10.2
implies

0\leq u(\varrho\,\cdot\,,\varrho^{2}\,\cdot\,)\leq C\varrho^{4}\quad\mbox{on }\{x_{n}=0\}\cap B_{R_{\circ}}.

Recalling that
\Psi(x,t)=t|x_{n}|+|x_{n}|^{3}/6
(in particular, its heat operator is concentrated on
\{x_{n}=0\}
), (
10.17
) follows easily.

\bullet

Step 4
. We finally conclude the proof of the lemma by combining (
10.6
), (
10.10
), and (
10.17
).

Indeed, recall that we want to show that
\frac{d}{d\varrho}|_{\varrho=\varrho_{\circ}}\big(\varrho^{-5}\tilde{H}(\varrho,w)\big)\geq 0
at every scale
\varrho_{\circ}
where (
10.4
)-(
10.5
) holds.
In view of (
10.6
), it is enough to prove that

(10.18)

\int_{-2\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}^{-\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big\{v\text{\small\bf H}v-(v-h)\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\big\}G\geq-\frac{1}{10}\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},v-h).

We split the integral

\int_{-2\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}^{-\varrho_{\circ}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big\{v\text{\small\bf H}v-(v-h)\text{\small\bf H}(v-h)\big\}G=\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big\{v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}-w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big\}G

into two pieces, according to the partition
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-2,-1)=B_{R_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)\cup(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}})\times(-2,-1)
.

Note that, using Lemma
5.2
, (
10.10
), and the bound
G\leq Ce^{-cR^{2}}
in
(B_{2R}\setminus B_{R})\times(-2,-1)
, we get

\begin{split}\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}})}\big\{v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}-w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big\}G&\geq-\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}})}|w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}|G-Ce^{-c\varrho_{\circ}}\geq-\frac{1}{20}\tilde{H}(\varrho_{\circ},v-h),\end{split}

provided that
R_{\circ}
is taken sufficiently large.
(Here we used that, given
\delta_{\circ}>0
, we have
\min\{G,\delta_{\circ}\}=G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R_{\circ}}
if
R_{\circ}
is sufficiently large.)

On the other hand, to estimate the integral in
B_{R_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)
we use that
w_{\varrho}=v_{\varrho}+\varrho^{3}(x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\Psi)
inside the integration domain, that
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{2})=0
, and
\Psi\text{\small\bf H}\Psi\equiv 0
. This implies that

\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}\big\{v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}-w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big\}G=\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}\big\{v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}(\varrho_{\circ}^{3}\bar{a}\Psi)+(\varrho_{\circ}^{3}(x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\Psi))\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big\}G

Now, as in (
10.9
) we have the bound

\bigg|\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}(\varrho_{\circ}^{3}(x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}\Psi))\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}G\bigg|\leq C\varrho_{\circ}^{7}.

Also, using (
10.17
),

0\leq-\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}(\varrho_{\circ}^{3}\Psi)G=-\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}u_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}(\varrho_{\circ}^{3}\Psi)\leq C\varrho_{\circ}^{7},

thus, recalling (
10.5
),

\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{R_{\circ}}}\big\{v_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}v_{\varrho_{\circ}}-w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\text{\small\bf H}w_{\varrho_{\circ}}\big\}G\geq-C\varrho_{\circ}^{7}\gg-\frac{c}{20}\varrho_{\circ}^{M}\geq-\frac{1}{20}H(\varrho_{\circ},v-h).

This concludes the proof of (
10.18
) and therefore the proof of the proposition.
∎

11.
The set
\Sigma^{*}
:
\varepsilon
-flatness vs accelerated decay

To continue our analysis, we now make a finite partition of
\Sigma^{*}
as follows: given
\delta>0
, we consider

(11.1)

\Sigma^{*}=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}:=\bigg\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}\ :H(1,p^{*}_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\in\frac{\delta}{2}[\ell,\ell+1)\bigg\}.

Since
1/C_{\varrho}\leq H(1,p^{*}_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\leq C_{\varrho}
for all
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}\cap\big(B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1)\big)

(by Corollary
8.5
), for any given
\delta>0
the number of sets in the previous partition restricted to compact subsets of
B_{1}\times(-1,1)
is bounded (their number is roughly
C/\delta
).

As we will see, the advantage of considering this partition is the following key property:

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
).
For any
\varepsilon>0
,
R_{\circ}>1
, and
\varrho\in(0,1)
, there exists
\bar{\delta}>0
such that the following holds provided
\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
:

Given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{\delta,\ell}^{*}\cap(B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1))
, there exists
r_{\circ}=r_{\circ}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
such that

\big\|u(x_{1}+\cdot,t_{1}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}-p_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}^{*}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{\circ}r}\times(-2r^{2},-r^{2}/2))}\leq\varepsilon r^{3}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,r_{\circ})

for all
(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
such that
|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|\leq r_{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
be a small constant to be fixed later, and let
(x_{1},t_{1})
be as in the statement.
Define

\boldsymbol{v}^{r}:=\frac{u(x_{1}+r\cdot,t_{1}+r^{2}\cdot)-r^{2}p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}}{r^{3}}.

We divide the proof in two steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. We show that

(11.2)

\|\boldsymbol{v}^{r}-p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{2R_{\circ}}\times(-4,-1/2))}\leq C(R_{\circ})\bar{\varepsilon}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,r_{\circ}).

Indeed, by definition of
\Sigma_{\delta,\ell}^{*}
, since
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}),(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma_{\delta,\ell}^{*}
it holds

(11.3)

\bigg|\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}^{*})^{2}G-\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*})^{2}G\bigg|\leq\delta.

Also, by Lemma
9.3
, the quantity

\mathcal{I}_{x_{1},t_{1}}(r,Q):=\frac{1}{r^{6}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}\big(u(x_{1}+\,\cdot\,,t_{1}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}\big)\zeta\,Q\,G

satisfies

\frac{d}{dr}\mathcal{I}_{x_{1},t_{1}}(r,Q)\geq-C\|Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}

for any
(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\cap B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1)
and for any given 3-homogenous solution
Q
of (
6.17
).

Hence, on the one hand we have

(11.4)

\begin{split}\int_{\{t=-1\}}\boldsymbol{v}^{r}\,p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}G=\mathcal{I}_{x_{1},t_{1}}(r,p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})\geq\mathcal{I}_{x_{1},t_{1}}(0^{+},p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})-Cr=\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}G-Cr.\end{split}

On the other hand, given
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we can fix
s_{\circ}>0
—depending on
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
— such that

\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*})^{2}G+\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq s_{\circ}^{-6}H\big(s_{\circ},(u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\zeta\big).

Note that, since

u(x_{1}+\,\cdot,t_{1}+\cdot\big)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}\longrightarrow u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot,t_{\circ}+\cdot\big)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\quad\mbox{as }(x_{1},t_{1})\to(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}),

then for
(x_{1},t_{1})
sufficiently close to
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
and for
r\in(0,s_{\circ})
we will have

\begin{split}\int_{\{t=-1\}}(p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*})^{2}G+2\varepsilon^{\prime}&\geq s_{\circ}^{-6}H\big(s_{\circ},(u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\zeta\big)+\varepsilon^{\prime}\\
&=s_{\circ}^{-6}H\big(1,(u(x_{\circ}+s_{\circ}\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+s_{\circ}^{2}\,\cdot\,)-s_{\circ}^{2}p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}})\zeta\big)+\varepsilon^{\prime}\\
&\geq s_{\circ}^{-6}H\big(1,(u(x_{1}+s_{\circ}\,\cdot\,,t_{1}+s_{\circ}^{2}\,\cdot\,)-s_{\circ}^{2}p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}})\zeta\big)\\
&=s_{\circ}^{-6}H\big(s_{\circ},(u(x_{1}+\cdot,t_{1}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}})\zeta\big)\\
&\geq r^{-6}H\big(r,(u(x_{1}+\cdot,t_{1}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}})\zeta\big)+O(e^{-c/s_{\circ}})\\
&=\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{r}\big)^{2}G+O(e^{-c/s_{\circ}}).\end{split}

Combining this estimate with (
11.4
) and (
11.3
), and choosing first
s_{\circ}
small enough and then
(x_{1},t_{1})
sufficiently close to
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
, we obtain

\begin{split}2\int_{\{t=-1\}}\boldsymbol{v}^{r}\,p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}G\geq\int_{\{t=-1\}}\big((\boldsymbol{v}^{r})^{2}+(p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}\big)G-\bar{\varepsilon}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,s_{\circ}).\end{split}

In other words,

\int_{\{t=-1\}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{r}-p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}G\leq\bar{\varepsilon}\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,s_{\circ}),

and (
11.2
) follows.

\bullet

Step 2
. We upgrade the
L^{2}
bound from (
11.2
) to an
L^{\infty}
bound.

For this, recall that
(p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}
is always divisible by
p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}
,therefore

\mathcal{P}_{1}:=p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}p_{3}}+p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}}+{\textstyle\frac{(p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}}{4p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}}}\geq 0

satisfies

\text{\small\bf H}\mathcal{P}_{1}\leq 1+\text{\small\bf H}\left({\textstyle\frac{(p^{*}_{3,x_{1},t_{1}})^{2}}{4p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}}}\right)\leq 1+C(|x|^{2}+t).

Thus, since
\text{\small\bf H}u=1
in
\{u>0\}
,

\text{\small\bf H}\big(u(x_{1}+r\cdot,t_{1}+r^{2}\cdot)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(r\cdot,r^{2}\cdot)\big)_{+}\geq-Cr^{4}\qquad\mbox{in }B_{2R_{\circ}r}\times(-3r^{2},-r^{2}/2).

On the other hand since
\text{\small\bf H}\mathcal{P}_{1}=1+O(|x|^{2}+t)
inside
\{\mathcal{P}_{1}>0\}
, while
\text{\small\bf H}u\leq 1
and
u\geq 0
, we have

\text{\small\bf H}\big(u(x_{1}+r\cdot,t_{1}+r^{2}\cdot)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(r\cdot,r^{2}\cdot)\big)_{-}\geq-Cr^{4}\qquad\mbox{in }B_{2R_{\circ}r}\times(-3r^{2},-r^{2}/2).

This proves that

\text{\small\bf H}\big|u(x_{1}+r\cdot,t_{1}+r^{2}\cdot)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(r\cdot,r^{2}\cdot)\big|\geq-Cr^{4}\qquad\mbox{in }B_{2R_{\circ}r}\times(-3r^{2},-r^{2}/2).

Also, (
11.2
) implies that

\big\|u(x_{1}+r\,\cdot\,,t_{1}+r^{2}\,\cdot\,)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{L^{2}(B_{2R_{\circ}r}\times(-3r^{2},-r^{2}/2))}\leq\bar{\varepsilon}r^{3}+Cr^{4}.

Therefore, it follows from the one-sided Harnack inequality for the heat equation that

\big\|u(x_{1}+r\,\cdot\,,t_{1}+r^{2}\,\cdot\,)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(r\,\cdot\,,r^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{\circ}r}\times(-2r^{2},-r^{2}/2))}\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}r^{3}+Cr^{4}.

This implies the conclusion of the lemma by taking
\bar{\varepsilon}
and
r
sufficienlty small.
∎

We have the following:

Corollary 11.2
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded solution of (
3.1
).
Given
\varrho\in(0,1)
,
C_{\circ}
, and
M\in(6,7)
,
\bar{\delta}>0
such that the following holds provided
\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
:

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}
be as in (
10.3
), and recall (
11.1
).
For any given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{\delta,\ell}^{*}\cap\big(B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1)\big)
, there exists
r_{\circ}=r_{\circ}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
such that

\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}\big(r,(u(x_{1}+\cdot,t_{1}+\cdot)-p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}-Q_{x_{1},t_{1}})\zeta\big)\qquad\text{is monotone nondecreasing for $r\in(0,r_{\circ})$}

for all
(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
with
|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|\leq r_{\circ}
. Here
Q_{x_{1},t_{1}}=x_{n}Q_{2}+\bar{a}|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)
in coordinates such that
p_{2,x_{1},t_{1}}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
,
where
\bar{a}\in[0,C_{\circ}]
and
Q_{2}
is any 2-homogeneous polynomial satisfying
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{2})=0
and
\|Q_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
10.1
and Lemma
11.1
.
∎

The next proposition will be crucial in our argument. It provides us with the following powerful decay property:
if

\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
is not
\varepsilon
-flat at some small scale
r
, then
we have an accelerated decay for
u
at such scale.
This result will be at the core of the argument in the next section, where we will prove that we have an accelerated decay at
\Sigma^{*}
, outside a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
n-2
.

Recall that, given a function
q
, we denote by
q^{even}
the even symmetrization of
q
with respect to the hyperplane
\{p_{2}=0\}
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a solution of (
3.1
), let
\varrho>0
, and let
\delta>0
be given by Corollary
11.2
.
Let
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\big(B_{1-\varrho}\times(-1+\varrho,1)\big)
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and
choose coordinates so that
p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
and
(p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*})^{\rm even}=a_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\Psi
, where

\Psi(x,t):=|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t).

Let
\hat{\alpha}:=1/3
, and for
r\in(0,\varrho]
define

A(r):=\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\big\|(u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}-p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*}-a\Psi)_{r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Theta(r):=\max_{s\in[r,\varrho]}(r/s)^{3+\hat{\alpha}}A(s)\,.

Then the following two properties hold.

(1)
Fix
\varepsilon>0
. For any
\varepsilon_{1}
there exists

r_{\varepsilon_{1}}>0
such that, for every
r\in(0,r_{\varepsilon_{1}})
, the following holds:

Consider the “
\varepsilon
-flatness property”

(11.5)

\pi_{x}(\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\{t\leq t_{\circ}\})\cap B_{r}(x_{\circ})\subset L+\varepsilon B_{r}\qquad\text{for some linear space $L$ with ${\rm dim}(L)\leq n-2$.}

If (
11.5
) fails, then

A(r)\leq\varepsilon_{1}\,\Theta(r).

(2)
For any
\varepsilon_{2}>0
there exists
\lambda_{\varepsilon_{2}}\in(0,1)
such that, for all
r\in(0,\varrho)
and
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{\varepsilon_{2}}]
,

\Theta(\lambda r)\leq\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}}\,\Theta(r).

Proof.

Assume for notational simplicity that
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=(0,0)
and
\varrho=1
. We begin by proving (1).

\bullet
Proof of (1).
Set

\overline{a}_{r}:={\rm arg\,min}_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\big\|(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-a\Psi)_{r}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})},\qquad w^{r}:=\frac{(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{r}\Psi)_{r}}{\Theta(r)}.

Note that, by construction, for any
s>0
we have
\Theta(2s)\leq 2^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\Theta(s)
. Hence

\big\|(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{2^{j}r}\Psi)_{2^{j}r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}=A(2^{j}r)\leq\Theta(2^{j}r)\leq(2^{j})^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\Theta(r)\qquad\forall\,j\geq 1.

Rescaling and using the triangle inequality between two consecutive dyadic scales, this gives

\begin{split}c\big|\overline{a}_{2^{j+1}r}-\overline{a}_{2^{j}r}\big|2^{3j}&=\big\|(\overline{a}_{2^{j+1}r}\Psi-\overline{a}_{2^{j}r}\Psi)_{2^{j}r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\\
&\leq\big\|\big(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{2^{j+1}r}\Psi\big)_{2^{j}r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{2})}+\big\|\big(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{2^{j}r}\Psi\big)_{2^{j}r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\\
&=2^{\frac{n+2}{2}}A(2^{j+1}r)+A(2^{j}r)\leq C2^{(3+\hat{\alpha})j}\Theta(r).\end{split}

Hence, summing the geometric series and using again the triangle inequality, we obtain
|\overline{a}_{2^{j}r}-\overline{a}_{r}|\leq C2^{j\alpha\circ}\Theta(r)
,
which implies the growth control

(11.6)

\big\|w^{r}(R\,\cdot\,,R^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq CR^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\qquad\forall R\in(1,1/r).

Now, assume by contradiction that there exist a constant
\varepsilon_{1}>0
and a sequence
r_{k}\downarrow 0
for which (
11.5
) fails at the scales
r=r_{k}
but
A(r_{r})\geq\varepsilon_{1}\,\Theta(r_{k})
, or equivalently

(11.7)

\|w^{r_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}>\varepsilon_{1},

while the negation of (
11.5
) gives the existence of
x_{k}^{(1)},x_{k}^{(2)},\dots,x_{k}^{(n-1)}\in\pi_{x}(\Sigma^{*}\cap\{t\leq 0\})\cap B_{r_{k}}
such that
y_{k}^{(i)}:=x_{k}^{(i)}/r_{k}\in\overline{B_{1}}
satisfy

{\rm dist}\big(\{y_{k}^{(i)}\}_{1\leq i\leq n-1},L\big)\geq\varepsilon\,\quad\ \forall\,L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\mbox{ linear space with }{\rm dim}(L)=n-2.

The outline of the proof will go as follows.

In
Step 1
, we show that
w^{r_{k}}
converges (up to a subsequence) strongly in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
to a some function
w^{0}\not\equiv 0

which satisfying the growth condition

(11.8)

\|w^{0}(R\,\cdot\,,R^{2}\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq R^{3+\hat{\alpha}},\qquad\forall R\in(1,\infty)

and solves

(11.9)

\begin{cases}\text{\small\bf H}w^{0}=0\quad&\mbox{ in }\{x_{n}\neq 0\}\\
w^{0}=0&\mbox{ on }\{x_{n}=0\}.\end{cases}

This will imply that
w^{0}
is a caloric polynomial degree 3 in each side of
\{x_{n}=0\}
.

In
Step 2
, we use Proposition
10.1
to show that

(11.10)

\mbox{ $w^{0}$ is homogeneous of degree $3$}.

In
Step 3
, we use the monotonicity formulae from Lemma
9.3
to show that

(11.11)

w^{0}
is even in the variable
x_{n}
,

so that, up to a rotation fixing
\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
,
w^{0}
must be of the form

(11.12)

w^{0}(x,t)\equiv a\Psi+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\,|x_{n}|(-3x_{i}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}).

In addition we show that, thanks to the choice of
\bar{a}_{r}
,
w^{0}
satisfies

(11.13)

\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}w^{0}\Psi=0

and hence
a=0
.

Finally, in
Step 4
we exploit again the monotonicity formula from Proposition
10.1
, but now at points
(x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)})
, in order to reach a contradiction with (
11.12
)-(
11.13
) to complete the proof.

We now develop Steps 1-4.

\bullet

Step 1
. We prove first the compactness of the functions
w^{r_{k}}
in
C^{0}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
.
To prove this we show that, for any given
R>0
, there exists
k_{\circ}
such that, for
k\geq k_{\circ}
, we have

(11.14)

\int_{B_{R}\times(-R^{2},0)}\bigl(|\nabla w^{r_{k}}|^{2}+|\partial_{t}w|^{2}\bigr)\leq C_{R}.

This will give compactness
w^{r_{k}}
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}
, and we will later upgrade this convergence to a locally uniform one.

Since
\Theta(r_{k})\geq cr_{k}^{3+\hat{\alpha}}
(thanks to (
11.7
)), arguing similarly to (
10.11
) we get

(11.15)

w^{r_{k}}\text{\small\bf H}w^{r_{k}}\geq-C(r_{k})^{-2\hat{\alpha}}R^{4}\chi_{\{|x_{n}|\leq Cr_{k}R^{2}\}}\quad\mbox{ in }\mathscr{C}_{r}.

Using (
10.12
) with
\phi=w^{r_{k}}
and with
\xi
replaced by
\xi_{R}(x):=\xi(x/R)
, where
\xi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{2})
is a nonnegative cut-off function satisfying
\xi\equiv 1
in
B_{1}
, we obtain

(11.16)

\int_{B_{2R}\times\{t\}}\big(|w^{r_{k}}\text{\small\bf H}w^{r_{k}}|+|\nabla w^{r_{k}}|^{2}\big)\xi_{R}+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2R}\times\{t\}}(w^{r_{k}})^{2}\xi_{R}\leq\frac{C_{n}}{R^{2}}\int_{B_{2R}\times\{t\}}(w^{r_{k}})^{2}+Cr_{k}^{1-2\hat{\alpha}}R^{n+5}.

for
t\in(-R^{2},0)
and
R\in(0,1/2r_{k})
.
Integrating (
11.16
) in time and using (
11.6
), this yields

(11.17)

\frac{1}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}|w^{r_{k}}|^{2}+\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}|\nabla w^{r_{k}}|^{2}\leq C(R).

Also, since
\partial_{tt}(u-p_{2}-p_{3,r}^{*})=\partial_{tt}u\geq-C
(see Proposition
3.4
), it follows by scaling that
\partial_{tt}w_{r_{k}}\geq-Cr_{k}^{1-\hat{\alpha}}
.

We now use the following elementary bound: for
f:(0,R)\to\mathbb{R}
,

{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{0}^{R}R^{2}|f^{\prime}|^{2}\leq C\bigg({{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{0}^{R}|f|^{2}}+\sup_{(0,R)}R^{4}(f^{\prime\prime})^{2}_{-}\bigg),

Applying this with
f(\,\cdot\,)=u(x,\,\cdot\,)
for each fixed
x\in B_{R}
, and then integrating in the
x
variable, it follows by (
11.17
) that

\int_{\mathscr{C}_{R}}|\partial_{t}w^{r_{k}}|^{2}dx\,dt\leq C(R)+o_{k}(1),

where
o_{k}(1)\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
This gives (
11.14
), and thus
w^{r_{k}}\rightarrow w^{0}
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
.
Notice also that
w^{0}
is nonzero by (
11.7
).
Moreover, reasoning exactly as in Step 2 in the proof of Lemma
11.1
, such
L^{2}_{\rm loc}
convergence implies
C^{0}_{\rm loc}
convergence.

The growth condition (
11.8
) follows from (
11.6
), so it remains to show that
w^{0}
is satisfies (
11.9
).

First, using Lemma
10.2
,

w^{r_{k}}|_{\{x_{n}=0\}}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{ in }C^{0}_{\rm loc}(\{x_{n}=0\}).

On the other hand, since
\text{\small\bf H}w^{r_{k}}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus(\{u_{r_{k}}=0\}\cup\{x_{n}=0\})
and the Hausdorff distance of
\{u_{r_{k}}=0\}
from
\{x_{n}=0\}
converges locally to zero, we obtain that

\text{\small\bf H}w^{r_{k}}\to 0
in every compact subset of
\{x_{n}\neq 0\}
.
It follows that
w^{0}
solves (
11.9
) (in the weak sense).

Finally, by the growth condition (
11.8
) and the Liouville theorem for the heat equation in a half-space, we deduce that
w^{0}
is a caloric polynomial of degree 3 on each side of
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
Furthermore, since
w^{0}|_{\{x_{n}=0\}}=0
, both such polynomials are divisible by
x_{n}
.

\bullet

Step 2
. We show that
w^{0}
must be homogeneous of degree three, i.e., it cannot have linear or quadratic terms.
This will follow by using that, by Proposition
10.1
, we have that

(11.18)

\rho\mapsto\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}\big(\rho,(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\zeta\big)\quad\mbox{is monotone increasing}.

Indeed,
rescaling (
11.18
), we have

(11.19)

\rho\mapsto\max_{R\in[1,1/(r_{k}\rho)]}R^{-M}\rho^{-5}\tilde{H}\big(R\rho,w^{r_{k}}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})\big)\quad\mbox{is monotone increasing}.

Now, taking
2(3+\hat{\alpha})<M<7
(recall that
\hat{\alpha}=1/3
), thanks to the growth control (
11.6
)
we have

\max_{R\geq 1}R^{-M}\tilde{H}\big(R,w^{r_{k}}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})\big)\leq C_{1},

with
C_{1}
independent of
k
.
Hence, by monotonicity, for all
\rho\in(0,1)
we have

\rho^{-5}\tilde{H}\big(\rho,w^{r_{k}}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})\big)\leq\max_{R\in[1,1/(r_{k}\rho)]}R^{-M}\rho^{-5}\tilde{H}\big(R\rho,w^{r_{k}}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})\big)\leq C_{1},

and letting
k\to\infty
we get

(11.20)

\tilde{H}\big(\rho,w^{0}\big)\leq C_{1}\rho^{5}.

Since we already know that
w^{0}
must be a degree three polynomial when restricted to each side of
\{x_{n}=0\}
, using (
11.20
) we conclude that it must be 3-homogenous.

\bullet

Step 3
. We now show that
w^{0}
must be of the form

(11.21)

w^{0}(x,t)\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\,|x_{n}|(-3x_{i}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}).

We first show that
w^{0}
satisfies (
11.11
). Indeed, by Lemma
9.3
, we have

\frac{1}{r^{3}}\int_{\{t=-r^{2}\}}(u-p_{2})\zeta\,P\,G\geq\int_{\{t=1\}}p_{3}^{*}\,P\,G-C(P)r

for all
P
odd 3-homogeneous caloric polynomial.
Therefore, using that
\Psi^{odd}=0
, we have

\int_{\{t=-1\}}w^{r_{k}}\zeta(r_{k}\,\cdot\,)\,P\,G=\int_{\{t=-r_{k}^{2}\}}\frac{u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{r}\Psi}{\Theta(r_{k})}\,\zeta\,P\,G-Cr_{k}^{4}/\Theta(r_{k}).

Since
\Theta(r_{k})\geq cr_{k}^{3+\hat{\alpha}}
, it follows that
r_{k}^{4}/\Theta(r_{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, and therefore

\int_{\{t=-1\}}w^{0}\,P\,G\geq 0,\quad\mbox{for every 3-homogeneous odd caloric polynomial $P$}.

This implies that
w^{0}
must be even, and therefore must be of the form (
11.12
).

In addition, by definition of
\overline{a}_{r}
and
w^{r}
we obtain, for all
r>0
, the following orthogonality condition holds:

\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}w_{r}\,\Psi=0.

Taking the limit as
r=r_{k}\downarrow 0
we obtain (
11.13
), and (
11.21
) follows.

\bullet

Step 4
.
Let
\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}\in SO(n)
denote the rotation mapping
\{p_{2,x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)}}=0\}
to
\{x_{n}=0\}=\{p_{2}=0\}
such that
\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}-{\rm Id}
has minimal Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
Since
p_{2,x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)}}-p_{2}(x_{k}^{(i)}+r_{k}\cdot,t_{k}^{(i)}+r^{2}\cdot)=O(r_{k}^{3})
(this follows, for instance, using Corollary
8.5
and the triangle inequality), we have that

|\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}-{\rm Id}|\leq Cr_{k}
.
Also, since
(x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)})\in\Sigma_{n-1}
, we have

p_{2,x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)}}=p_{2}\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}
.
Furthermore, thanks to Proposition
10.1
,
we know that

(11.22)

\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}\big(r,(u(x_{k}^{(i)}+\cdot,t_{k}^{(i)}+\cdot)-(p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k})\zeta\big)\quad\mbox{is monotone increasing}.

Note that, since
(x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)})\in\Sigma_{n-1}
, we have

p_{2,x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)}}=p_{2}\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}
.

Define now

D_{k}^{(i)}:=\big(r_{k}^{2}p_{2}+r_{k}^{3}(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\big)(x_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)-\big(r_{k}^{2}p_{2}+r_{k}^{3}(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\big)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}.

Then

\begin{split}W_{k}^{(i)}(x):&=u(x_{k}^{(i)}+r_{k}\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+r_{k}\,\cdot\,)-r_{k}^{2}p_{2,x_{k}^{(i)},t_{k}^{(i)}}-r_{k}^{3}(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}\\
&=(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)(x_{k}^{(i)}+r_{k}\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+r_{k}\,\cdot\,)+D_{k}^{(i)}\\
&=\Theta(r_{k})w^{r_{k}}(y_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,s_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)+D_{k}^{(i)}.\end{split}

We want to compute the limit
D_{k}^{(i)}/\Theta(r_{k})
for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.
There are two alternatives:

(i)

either there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
\Theta(r_{k})=o\left(\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\right)
as
k\to\infty
,

(ii)

or there exists
c>0
such that
\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq c\Theta(r_{k})
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.

We want to reach a contradiction in both cases.

- Case (i)
.
Fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
\Theta(r_{k})=o\left(\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\right)
.
Then, up to a subsequence we have

\lim_{k}\frac{W_{k}^{(i)}}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}=\lim_{k}\frac{D_{k}^{(i)}}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}=:\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty}.

Note that, since
D_{k}^{(i)}
belongs to a finite dimensional space, the convergence is strong and
\|\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}=1
.

We want to understand the structure of
D_{\infty}^{(i)}
.
First, since
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
we have

\frac{r_{k}^{2}\,p_{2}(x_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)-r_{k}^{2}\,p_{2}\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}\to x_{n}(a^{\prime}\cdot x^{\prime})+c_{1}x_{n},

where
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
x^{\prime}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})
.

On the other hand,

\begin{split}&\frac{r_{k}^{3}\,(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)(x_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)-r_{k}^{3}\,(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}=\\
&=\frac{r_{k}^{3}\big((p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)(x_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)-(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\big)}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}+\frac{r_{k}^{3}\big((p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)-(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}\big)}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}\end{split}

Hence, since
(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)^{even}
is a positive multiple of
|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)
, we get

\frac{r_{k}^{3}\big((p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)(x_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,t_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)-(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\big)}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}\to c_{2}{\rm sign}(x_{n})x_{n}^{2}+c_{3}|x_{n}|+\big[\mbox{odd quadratic polynomial}\big].

Also, recalling that
\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq r_{k}^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\gg r_{k}^{4}
and
|\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}-{\rm Id}|\leq Cr_{k}
, we have

\frac{r_{k}^{3}\big((p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)-(p_{3}^{*}+\overline{a}_{r_{k}}\Psi)\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{k}^{(i)}\big)}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}\to 0.

This proves that

(11.23)

\tilde{D}_{\infty}^{(i)}=c_{2}{\rm sign}(x_{n})x_{n}^{2}+c_{3}|x_{n}|+\big[\mbox{odd quadratic polynomial}\big].

On the other hand, using (
11.22
) and recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}
in (
10.3
), we obtain that

(11.24)

\rho\mapsto\max_{R\in[1,1/(r_{k}\rho)]}R^{-M}\rho^{-5}\tilde{H}\big(R\rho,W_{k}^{(i)}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})/\|D_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\big)\quad\mbox{is monotone increasing},

and
by the same argument as in (
11.20
), we get

\tilde{H}\big(\rho,W_{k}^{(i)}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})/\|D_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\big)\leq C\rho^{5}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\tilde{H}(\rho,\tilde{D}_{\infty})\leq C\rho^{5}.

Recalling (
11.23
) we deduce that
\tilde{D}_{\infty}=0
, a contradiction.

- Case (ii)
. In this case, for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
we have

\lim_{k}\frac{W_{k}^{(i)}}{\Theta(r_{k})}=w^{r_{k}}(y_{k}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,s_{k}^{(i)}+\cdot)+\lim_{k}\frac{D_{k}^{(i)}}{\Theta(r_{k})}

where
y_{k}^{(i)}:=x_{k}^{(i)}/r_{k}\in\overline{B_{1}}
and
-C\leq s_{k}^{(i)}=t_{k}^{(i)}/r_{k}^{2}\leq 0
(cp. (
9.4
)).

Up to taking a subsequence we can define

\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k}\frac{D_{k}^{(i)}}{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}
, so that
\lim_{k}\frac{D_{k}^{(i)}}{\Theta(r_{k})}=\lim_{k}\frac{\|D_{k}^{(i)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}}{\Theta(r_{k})}\tilde{D}_{\infty}^{(i)}
and therefore

(11.25)

\lim_{k}\frac{W_{k}^{(i)}}{\Theta(r_{k})}=w^{0}(y_{\infty}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)+c^{(i)}\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty},

where the points
y_{\infty}^{(i)}\in\overline{B_{1}}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
are linearly independent,
-C\leq s_{\infty}^{(i)}\leq 0
,
c^{(i)}\geq 0
, and
\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty}
is of the form (
11.23
)
(by the same argument as the one above, in Case (i)).

Also, thanks to (
11.22
) and recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{h}^{\frac{5}{2}}
in (
10.3
), we have that

(11.26)

\rho\mapsto\max_{R\in[1,1/(r_{k}\rho)]}R^{-M}\rho^{-5}\tilde{H}\big(R\rho,W_{k}^{(i)}\zeta(\,\cdot\,/r_{k})/\Theta(r_{k})\big)\quad\mbox{is monotone increasing},

and as in Case (i) we get

(11.27)

\tilde{H}(\rho,w^{0}(y_{\infty}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,,s_{\infty}^{(i)}+\,\cdot\,)+c^{(i)}\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty})\leq C\rho^{5}\qquad\forall\,i=1,\ldots,n-1.

Recall that
w^{0}
is of the form (
11.21
), while each
\tilde{D}^{(i)}_{\infty}
is of the form (
11.23
).
Hence, since the points
y_{\infty}^{(i)}
are
n-1
linearly independent in
\{x_{n}=0\}
, one can easily check that the only possibility is
w^{0}\equiv 0
and
c^{i}=0
for all
i
.
However, recalling (
11.14
), taking the limit in (
11.7
) we obtain
\|w^{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq\varepsilon_{1}
, a contradiction.

This completes the proof of part (1) of the proposition.

\bullet
Proof of (2).
This follows from a modified (and simpler) version of the argument given for part (1).
We will prove that, for any given
\varepsilon_{2}>0
, there exists
C_{\circ}\geq 1
such that
we have

(11.28)

A(\lambda r)\leq C_{\circ}\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\,\Theta(r)\qquad\forall\,r,\lambda\in(0,1).

First notice that (
11.28
) implies the conclusion. Indeed, by the definition of
\Theta
and (
11.28
),

\begin{split}\Theta(\lambda r)&=\max_{s\in[\lambda r,1]}(\lambda r/s)^{3+\hat{\alpha}}A(s)=\max\Big\{\lambda^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\Theta(r),\max_{s\in[\lambda r,r]}(\lambda r/s)^{3+\hat{\alpha}}A(s)\Big\}\\
&\leq\max\left\{\lambda^{3+\hat{\alpha}}\Theta(r),C_{\circ}\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\,\Theta(r)\right\}=C_{\circ}\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\,\Theta(r)\leq\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}}\,\Theta(r),\end{split}

for
\lambda>0
sufficiently small.

To prove (
11.28
) we reason by contradiction and compactness.
Assume by contradiction that we have sequences
r_{k}\in(0,1)
and
\lambda_{k}\in(0,1)
such that

(11.29)

A(\lambda_{k}r_{k})\geq k\lambda_{k}^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\,\Theta(r_{k}),

and define

\Omega_{k}(\lambda)=\max_{\lambda^{\prime}\in[\lambda,1]}(\lambda/\lambda^{\prime})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\Theta(\lambda^{\prime}r_{k}).

Using (
11.29
) and the definition of
\Theta
we have

(11.30)

\lambda_{k}^{-3+\varepsilon_{2}/2}\frac{\Theta(\lambda_{k}r_{k})}{\Theta(r_{k})}\to\infty\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

Note that

(11.31)

for any
\theta_{\circ}\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\theta_{\circ}}>0
such that
\Theta(\theta_{\circ}r)\leq C_{\theta_{\circ}}\Theta(r)

for all
r\in(0,1)
.

Also,
by (
11.30
) and the definition of
\Omega_{k}
, there exists
\lambda^{\prime}_{k}\in[\lambda_{k},1]
such that

\Omega_{k}(\lambda_{k})=(\lambda_{k}/\lambda^{\prime}_{k})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\Theta(\lambda^{\prime}_{k}r_{k})=\max_{\lambda^{\prime}\in[\lambda_{k}^{\prime},1]}(\lambda_{k}/\lambda^{\prime})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\Theta(\lambda^{\prime}r_{k})\geq(\lambda_{k})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\Theta(r_{k}).

Thanks to these two facts we deduce that
\lambda^{\prime}_{k}\to 0
.
Also, by the maximality of
\lambda_{k}^{\prime}
,

\Omega_{k}(\tau)=(\tau/\lambda^{\prime}_{k})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\Theta(\lambda^{\prime}_{k}r_{k})\qquad\forall\,\tau\in[\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k}^{\prime}],

and by the definitions of
\Theta
and
\Omega_{k}
we deduce that

(11.32)

A(\lambda^{\prime}_{k}r_{k})=\Theta(\lambda^{\prime}_{k}r_{k})=\Omega_{k}(\lambda_{k}^{\prime}).

We now define

W^{k}:=\frac{(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}-\overline{a}_{\lambda_{k}r_{k}}\Psi)_{\lambda^{\prime}_{k}r_{k}}}{\Omega_{k}(\lambda^{\prime}_{k})}.

By (
11.32
) we have

\|W^{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq 1.

Also, by construction,

(11.33)

\big\|W^{k}(R\,\cdot\,,R^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq\begin{cases}CR^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\quad&\mbox{for }R\in[1,1/\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\\
C(1/\lambda_{k}^{\prime})^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}(R/\lambda_{k}^{\prime})^{3+\alpha}\quad&\mbox{for }R\in[1/\lambda_{k}^{\prime},1/(\lambda_{k}^{\prime}r_{k})].\\
\end{cases}

Hence, repeating the same reasoning as in Step 1 above, we find that
W^{k}\to\widetilde{W}
where

\|\widetilde{W}\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\geq 1,\qquad\text{\small\bf H}\widetilde{W}=0\quad\mbox{in }\{x_{n}\neq 0\},\qquad\widetilde{W}=0\quad\mbox{on }\{x_{n}=0\}.

Furthermore, thanks to (
11.33
) and the fact that
\lambda_{k}^{\prime}\to 0
,
\widetilde{W}
satisfies the sub-cubic growth

\big\|\widetilde{W}(R\,\cdot\,,R^{2}\,\cdot\,)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq CR^{3-\varepsilon_{2}/2}\quad\mbox{for all }R\geq 1.

This implies that
\widetilde{W}
is a caloric polynomial of degree 2 on each side of the hyperplane
\{x_{n}=0\}
.
On the other hand, using (
11.33
) again, we can repeat the argument in Step 2 above to show that
\tilde{H}\big(\rho,\widetilde{W}\big)\leq C_{1}\rho^{5}
for all
\rho>0
(cp. (
11.20
)).
This implies that
\widetilde{W}\equiv 0
, a contradiction.
∎

12.
An estimate of order
3+\beta
at “most” singular points

In this section we prove that, at “most” points of
\Sigma^{*}
, we have a decay of order
3+\beta
as in the following definition.

Definition 12.1
.

Denote by
\Sigma^{\diamond}
the set of points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}
such that, for some positive numbers
\beta
and
r_{\circ}
(which may depend on the point), we have

(12.1)

\big\|\big(u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)-p_{2,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}-p_{3,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{*}\big)_{r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq r^{3+\beta}\quad\mbox{for all }r\in(0,r_{\circ}).

We want to show the following:

Proposition 12.2
.

Assume that
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
is a solution of (
3.1
).
Then,

{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(\Sigma^{*}\setminus\Sigma^{\diamond})\big)\leq n-2.

We will prove Proposition
12.2
by carefully exploiting Proposition
11.3
, combined with some delicate covering arguments.

Let
\delta>0
be given by Corollary
11.2
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Also, let
\varepsilon>0
to be fixed later.
Given
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
, let
\kappa_{\circ}=\lfloor\log_{2}r_{\circ}\rfloor+1
.
For
j\geq k_{x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}
we define

(12.2)

N_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ},j):=\#\big\{i\in\{\kappa_{\circ},\ldots,j\}\ :\mbox{ \eqref{eq:Pr} applied to $u(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,\ t_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,)$ does \emph{not} hold at scale $r=2^{-i}$}\big\},

and

(12.3)

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}):=\liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{N_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ},j)}{j}.

Notice that
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is nonincreasing in
\varepsilon
.

The goal of this section will be to prove the following two properties:

(i)

if
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{\diamond}
;

(ii)

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\pi_{x}
-projection of the set
\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\,:\,\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=0\ \forall\,\varepsilon>0\}
has Hausdorff dimension at most
n-2
.

The implication (i) will follow by Proposition
11.3
(2), thanks to the definition of
\omega(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
.
It is the content of the following:

Lemma 12.3
.

Assume that
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
Then there exist
r_{\circ},\beta>0
such that (
12.1
) holds.

Proof.

For notational simplicity we assume that
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=(0,0)
.
By assumption there exists
N_{\circ}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\omega_{\varepsilon}(0,0):=\liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{N_{\varepsilon}(0,0,j)}{j}\geq\frac{2}{N_{\circ}}.

Hence, there exists
j_{\circ}
such that

\frac{N(0,0,j)}{j}\geq\frac{1}{N_{\circ}}\qquad\forall\,j\geq j_{\circ}.

This means that there exists a set
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\subset\{\kappa_{\circ},\ldots\}
such that

(12.4)

\mbox{ \eqref{eq:Pr} does not hold at scales $r=2^{-i}$ for all $i\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}$, }\qquad\#\big\{\mathcal{I}_{{\varepsilon}}\cap\{\kappa_{\circ},\dots,j\}\big\}\geq\frac{j}{N_{\circ}}\quad\forall\,j\geq j_{\circ}.

In particular, given
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, up to taking
j_{\circ}
larger if needed (depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
), it follows from Proposition
11.3
(1) that

(12.5)

A(2^{-i})\leq\varepsilon_{1}\Theta(2^{-i}),\qquad\mbox{whenever }i\in\mathcal{I}_{{\varepsilon}}.

Also, given
\varepsilon_{2}>0
, it follows from Proposition
11.3
(2) that

(12.6)

A(\lambda 2^{-i})\leq\lambda^{3-\varepsilon_{2}}\,\Theta(2^{-i}),\quad\mbox{whenever }i\not\in\mathcal{I}_{{\varepsilon}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{\varepsilon_{2}}).

We now start to fix the different parameters.

First, we set
\beta:=\frac{1}{8N_{\circ}}
and choose
\varepsilon_{2}>0
small enough so that

(12.7)

(3-\varepsilon_{2})\bigg(1-\frac{1}{N_{\circ}}\bigg)+\frac{10}{3N_{\circ}}\geq 3+2\beta.

Then, we fix
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2^{-M}<\lambda_{\varepsilon_{2}}
and we note that, by the definition of
A
(see Proposition
11.3
)

i\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad A(\lambda 2^{-i})\leq\lambda^{-(n+2)}A(2^{-i})\leq\lambda^{-(n+2)}\varepsilon_{1}\Theta(2^{-i}).

Hence, choosing
\varepsilon_{1}>0
small so that
2^{(n+2)M}\varepsilon_{1}\leq 2^{-\frac{10}{3}M}
, it follows from the bound above and the definition of
\Theta
(see Proposition
11.3
and recall that
\hat{\alpha}=1/3
) that

(12.8)

i\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Theta(2^{-i-M})\leq 2^{-\frac{10}{3}M}\Theta(2^{-i}).

Now, to conclude the proof, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j_{k}:=\kappa_{\circ}+(kN_{\circ}+1)M\geq j_{\circ}
, we note that there exists
m_{k}\in\{1,\dots,M\}
such that

\#\big(\mathcal{I}_{{\varepsilon}}\cap(M\mathbb{Z}+\kappa_{\circ}+m_{j})\cap\{\kappa_{\circ},\dots,j_{k}\}\big)\geq k.

Hence, if we set
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}:=\big(\mathcal{I}_{{\varepsilon}}\cap(M\mathbb{Z}+\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k})\cap\{\kappa_{\circ},\ldots,j_{k}\}\big)
,
it follows from (
12.8
) that

i\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Theta(2^{-i-M})\leq 2^{-4M}\Theta(2^{-i}).

On the other hand, by Proposition
11.3
(2) we have

i\in\bigl((M\mathbb{Z}+\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k})\cap\{\kappa_{\circ},\ldots,j\}\bigr)\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Theta(2^{-i-M})\leq 2^{-(3-\varepsilon_{2})M}\Theta(2^{-i}).

Combining these two informations and recalling that
\#\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\geq k
, it follows from (
12.7
) that

\Theta(2^{-(\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k}+kN_{\circ}M)})\leq 2^{-\left(\frac{10}{3}M\,\#\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}+(3-\varepsilon_{2})[kN_{\circ}M-\#\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}]\right)}\Theta(2^{-(\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k})})\\
\leq 2^{-[10/3+(3-\varepsilon_{2})(N_{0}-1)]kM}\Theta(2^{-(\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k})})\leq 2^{-(3+2\beta)kN_{\circ}M}\Theta(2^{-(\kappa_{\circ}+m_{k})}).

Since
m_{k}\in\{0,\ldots,M-1\}
, using (
11.31
) we get

\Theta(2^{-(\kappa_{\circ}+M+kN_{\circ}M)})\leq C_{M}2^{-(3+2\beta)kN_{\circ}M}\Theta(2^{-\kappa_{\circ}})\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},

for some constant
C_{M}
depending only on
M
.
Thus, by (
11.31
) and the definition of
\Theta
we get

A(r)\leq\Theta(r)\leq C_{N_{0},M,\kappa_{0}}r^{3+2\beta}\quad\mbox{for }r\in(0,2^{-\kappa_{\circ}}),

and the lemma follows by choosing
r_{\circ}
sufficiently small.
∎

We now want to show property (ii).
This will follow from the following GMT results.

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
m\leq n
be positive integers. Given
\alpha\in(0,1)
there exists
\rho_{\circ}>0
so that,
for any
\rho\leq\rho_{\circ}
, there exist small constants
\varepsilon_{\alpha,\rho},\omega_{\alpha,\rho}>0
such that the following statement holds for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\alpha,\rho}
and
\omega\leq\omega_{\alpha,\rho}
.

Let
E\subset B_{1/2}(z)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For every
x\in E
and
j\geq 1
, define

(12.9)

\begin{split}N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,j)&:=\#\big\{i\in\{0,\dots,j\}\ :\ E\cap B_{\rho^{i}}(x)\subset y+L+B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\mbox{ for some $y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and $L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ linear subspace with ${\rm dim}(L)\leq m$}\big\}.\end{split}

Assume that, for some
j\geq 1
, we have

(12.10)

N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,j)\geq(1-\omega)j\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in E.

Then
E
can be covered by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha)j}
balls of radius
\rho^{j}
.

To prove it, we will need the following simple result (see
[
FRS20
, Lemma 7.2]
):

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
B_{r}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open ball, and
L
be a
m
-dimensional linear subspace (not necessarily passing through
x
).
Let
\beta_{1}>m
.
Then there exists
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\tau}(m,\beta_{1})>0
such that the following holds.

Let
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfy

F\subset B_{r}(x)\cap\{y\,:\,{\rm dist}(y,L)\leq\tau r\},\qquad\text{for some $0<\tau\leq\hat{\tau}$, $x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$, $r>0$.}

Then
F
be covered with
\gamma^{-\beta_{1}}
balls of radius
\gamma r
centered at points of
F
, where
\gamma:=5\tau
.

We now prove Lemma
12.4
.

Proof of Lemma
12.4
.

We will prove the following:

Claim
. Let
\ell\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq\ell
. If
E\subset B_{1/2}(z)
satisfies

(12.11)

N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x;\ell)\geq k\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in E,

then
E
can be covered by
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)k}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell}
.

To prove the Claim, we shall proceed by induction on
\ell\geq 1
.
However, we remark first that the case
k=0
and
\ell
arbitrary, we simply use that
E\subset B_{1/2}
to deduce that
E
can be covered by
C_{n}\rho^{n}\leq\rho^{-(n+\alpha)}
balls of radius
\rho
(for
\rho
small).
Hence, in the induction procedure, we can assume that
k\geq 1
.

\bullet
The case
\ell=1
.

In this case the only option is
\ell=k=1
.
Then the assumption
N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,1)\geq 1
implies

E\cap B_{1/2}(x)\subset y+L+B_{\varepsilon}
, so Lemma
12.5
with
\tau=\rho/5
and
r=1/2
yields that
E
can be covered by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha)}
balls of radius
\rho
(provided
\rho
is small enough so that
\tau\leq\hat{\tau}
, and then
\varepsilon
is chosen sufficiently small so that
\varepsilon\leq\tau r
).

\bullet
The inductive step.

Assume that the claim is true for
\ell-1\geq 1
and for all
k=1,\ldots\ell-1
.
We now prove it for
\ell
, and
k=1,\ldots,\ell
.
There are two cases to consider.

- Case 1.
Assume there exists
x\in E
such that

(12.12)

E\cap B_{1}(x)\subset x+L+B_{\varepsilon}.

Then, since
E\subset B_{1/2}(z)
, as before we can apply Lemma
12.5
to deduce that
E
can be covered (provided
\rho
and
\varepsilon
are chosen small enough) by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha)}
balls of radius
\rho/2
.
Let us call these balls
\{B_{\rho/2}(z_{q})\}_{q\in\mathcal{I}}
,
\#\mathcal{I}\leq\rho^{-(m+\alpha)}
.

Now, for any
q\in\mathcal{I}
we define
E_{q}:=\frac{1}{\rho}\big(E\cap B_{\rho/2}(z_{q})\big)\subset B_{1/2}(z_{q}/\rho)
and we observe that, thanks to (
12.11
),

N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E_{q},x;\ell-1)\geq k-1\qquad\forall\,q\in\mathcal{I}.

Therefore, by induction hypothesis, each set
E_{q}
can be covered by
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)(k-1)}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell-1}
.
This implies that
the union of all these balls multiplied by a factor
\rho
covers
E
, and the total number of such balls is
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)(k-1)}\#\mathcal{I}\leq\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)}
, as desired.

- Case 2.
Assume that for
none
of the points
x\in E
(
12.12
) holds.
In particular this implies that
k\leq\ell-1
and therefore, thanks to (
12.11
), for all
x\in E
we have

(12.13)

k\leq N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x;\ell)=\#\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\ :\ E\cap B_{\rho^{i}}(x)\subset x+L+B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon}\big\}.

(Note that, in the formula above,
i\geq 1
instead of
i\geq 0
as in the definition of
N^{\varepsilon}
.)

Now, let us cover the ball
B_{1/2}(z)
by
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)}
balls
\{B_{\rho/2}(z_{q})\}_{q\in\mathcal{I}}
of radius
\rho/2
.
In particular these balls cover
E
.
As in Case 1 we define
E_{q}:=\frac{1}{\rho}\big(E\cap B_{\rho/2}(z_{q})\big)\subset B_{1/2}(z_{q}/\rho)
,
and it follows from (
12.13
) that

N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E_{q},x;\ell-1)\geq k\qquad\forall\,q\in\mathcal{I}.

By induction hypothesis, each set
E_{q}
can be covered by
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-1-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)k}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell-1}
,
and therefore
E
can be covered by
\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-1-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)k}\#\mathcal{I}\leq\rho^{-(n+\alpha)(\ell-k)}\rho^{-(m+\alpha)}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell}
.
Thus, the Claim is proved.

Using the Claim, we finally show the result.
Indeed, setting
\ell=j
and
k=\lfloor(1-\omega)j\rfloor
, we know that for any
\hat{\alpha}>0
the set
E
can be covered by
\rho^{-(n+\hat{\alpha})(j-\lfloor(1-\omega)j\rfloor)-(m+\hat{\alpha})\lfloor(1-\omega)j\rfloor}\leq\rho^{-(m+\hat{\alpha}+(n-m)\omega)j-1}
balls of radius
\rho^{j}
.
Choosing
\hat{\alpha}<\alpha-(n-m)\omega
, the result follows by ensuring that
\rho
is sufficiently small.
∎

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Given
\varepsilon,\omega>0
small and
m\leq n
,
assume that

(12.14)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{N^{\varepsilon,1/2}_{m}(E,x,j)}{j}\geq 1-\omega\qquad\forall\,x\in E,

where
N^{\varepsilon,1/2}_{m}
is defined as in (
12.9
).
Then

{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(E)\leq m+\alpha,

where
\alpha(n,\varepsilon,\omega)\to 0
as
(\varepsilon,\omega)\to(0,0)
.

Proof.

Up to taking countable unions of we may assume that
E\subset B_{1/2}(z)
for some
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We being by observing that, as a consequence of (
12.14
) and the definition
N^{\varepsilon,1/2}_{m}
,

(12.15)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{N^{\varepsilon,2^{-M}}_{m}(E,x,j)}{j}\geq 1-M\omega\qquad\forall\,M\geq 1.

Hence we fix
\rho:=2^{-M}
with
M
sufficiently large so that
2^{-M}\leq\rho_{\circ}
, where
\rho_{\circ}
is given by Lemma
12.4
.

Now, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
large, we define

j(x,k):=\min\{\,j\geq k\ :\ N^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,j)\geq(1-2M\omega)j\,\}.

Thanks to (
12.15
), we can partition
E
as

E=\bigcup_{\ell=k}^{\infty}E_{\ell}\qquad\mbox{ with}\quad E_{\ell}:=\{x\in E\ :\ j(x,k)=\ell\}.

Hence, given
\alpha>0
, provided
\varepsilon,\omega
are small enough we can apply
Lemma
12.4
to deduce that
E_{\ell}\subset E\subset B_{1/2}(z)
can be covered by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha/2)\ell}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell}
.
Therefore,
E
can be covered by balls of radius
r_{i}=\rho^{\ell_{i}}
with
\ell_{i}\geq k
and
\#\{i:\ \ell_{i}=\ell\}\leq\rho^{-(m+\alpha/2)\ell}
.
This implies that

\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}_{2^{-k}}(E)\leq C_{m+\alpha}\sum_{i}r_{i}^{m+\alpha}\leq C_{m+\alpha}\sum_{\ell=k}^{\infty}\rho^{-(m+\alpha/2)\ell}(\rho^{\ell})^{m+\alpha}=C_{m+\alpha}\sum_{\ell=k}^{\infty}(\rho^{\alpha/2})^{\ell},

and the right hand side can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
k
large. This proves that

\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}(E)=0
, and therefore
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(E)\leq m+\alpha
, as desired.

∎

We will also need the following modification of Lemma
12.6
.

Proposition 12.7
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Given
\varepsilon,\omega>0
small,
m\leq n
,
(x,t)\in E
, and
j\geq 1
, define

(12.16)

\begin{split}\widetilde{N}^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,t,j):=\#\big\{&i\in 0,1,\dots,j\ :\ \pi_{x}\big(E\cap(\overline{B_{\rho^{i}}}(x)\times(-\infty,t])\big)\subset x+L+\overline{B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon}}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\mbox{ for some linear subspace $L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ with ${\rm dim}(L)=m$}\big\}.\end{split}

Assume that

(12.17)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{\widetilde{N}^{\varepsilon,1/2}_{m}(E,x,t,j)}{j}\geq 1-\omega\qquad\forall\,(x,t)\in E.

Then

{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(E)\big)\leq m+\alpha,

where
\alpha(n,\varepsilon,\omega)\to 0
as
(\varepsilon,\omega)\to(0,0)
.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha>0
, and let
\rho=2^{-M}
with
M
chosen large enough so that
\rho^{\alpha/4}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\rho\leq\rho_{\circ}
, with
\rho_{\circ}
given by Lemma
12.4
.
Up to taking countable unions, we may assume that
\pi_{x}(E)\subset\overline{B_{1/4}}(z)\times[-1,1]
.
Also, as in the proof of the previous lemma, (
12.17
) implies

(12.18)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\widetilde{N}^{\varepsilon,\rho}(E,x,j)\geq 1-M\omega.

Hence, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
large, we define

j(x,k):=\min\{\,j\geq k\ :\widetilde{N}^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E,x,j)\geq(1-2M\omega)j\,\},

and we partition

E=\bigcup_{\ell=k}^{\infty}E_{\ell}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad E_{\ell}:=\{x\in E\ :\ j(x,k)=\ell\}.

Since

\widetilde{N}^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{m}(E_{\ell},x,t,\ell)\geq(1-2N\omega)\ell\qquad\forall\,(x,t)\in E_{\ell},

for any
(x,t)\in E_{\ell}
there exist a subset
\sigma_{x,t}\subset\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
satisfying

\#\sigma_{x,t}\geq(1-2N\omega)\ell\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{x}\big(E\cap(\overline{B_{\rho^{i}}}(x)\times(-\infty,t])\big)\subset x+L_{x,i}+\overline{B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon}}\quad\forall\,i\in\sigma_{x,t},

where
L_{x,i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is some liner subspace with
{\rm dim}(L)=m
.

Let us further decompose each
E_{\ell}
as follows:

E_{\ell}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}\\
\sigma\subset\{0,\ldots,\ell\}\end{subarray}}E_{\ell,\sigma}\qquad\mbox{where}\quad E_{\ell,\sigma}:=\{(x,t)\in E_{\ell}\ :\ \sigma_{x,t}=\sigma\},

and let us show that

(12.19)

N^{\varepsilon/\rho,\rho}_{m}\left(\pi_{x}(E_{\ell,\sigma}),x,t,\ell\right)\geq(1-2M\omega)\ell-1\geq(1-3M\omega)\ell\qquad\forall\,(x,t)\in E_{\ell,\sigma},

provided that
\ell
is large enough, where
N^{\varepsilon,r}_{m}
is defined as in (
12.9
) .

Indeed, by the definition of
E_{\ell,\sigma}
we have

(12.20)

\pi_{x}\big(E_{\ell,\sigma}\cap(\overline{B_{\rho^{i}}}(x)\times(-\infty,t])\big)\subset x+L_{x,t,i}+\overline{B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon}}\quad\forall\,(x,t)\in E_{\ell,\sigma},\ \forall\,i\in\sigma.

The first important observation is that (
12.20
) holds also for all
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
in the closure
\overline{E_{\ell,\sigma}}
of
E_{\ell,\sigma}
, provided that we define
L_{\bar{x},\bar{t},i}
for
(\bar{x},\bar{t})\in\overline{E_{\ell,\sigma}}\setminus E_{\ell,\sigma}
as a limit of hyperplanes
L_{x,t,i}
for
(x,t)\in E_{\ell,\sigma}
converging to
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
.

Now, given
(x,t)\in E_{\ell,\sigma}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
such that
(i-1)\in\sigma
, we choose

\bar{t}:=\max\pi_{t}\big(\overline{B_{\rho^{i}}(x)}\times[-1,1]\cap\overline{E_{\ell,\sigma}}\big)
and let
(\bar{x},\bar{t})\in\overline{B_{\rho^{i}}(x)}\times[-1,1]\cap\overline{E_{\ell,\sigma}}
a corresponding point of “maximal time”.
Since
(i-1)\in\sigma
, then (
12.20
) holds for the point
(\bar{x},\bar{t})
with
i
replaced by
i-1
. Thus, using that
B_{\rho^{i}(x)}\subset B_{\rho^{i-1}}(\bar{x})
, we get

\ \pi_{x}\big(E_{\ell,\sigma}\big)\cap B_{\rho^{i}}(x)\subset\pi_{x}\left(\overline{E_{\ell,\sigma}}\cap(\overline{B_{\rho^{i-1}}}(\bar{x})\times(-\infty,\bar{t}])\right)\subset\bar{x}+L_{\bar{x},t,i}+\overline{B_{\rho^{i-1}\varepsilon}}.

In other words, for any given
(x,t)\in E_{\ell,\sigma}
and for all scales
i\in\{1,2,\dots,\ell\}
such that
(i-1)\in\ \sigma
(a set of cardinality at least
(1-3M\omega)\ell
), we have

\pi_{x}\big(E_{\ell,\sigma}\big)\cap B_{\rho^{i}}(x)\subset y+L+\overline{B_{\rho^{i}\varepsilon/\rho}},

for some
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a linear subspace
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of dimension
m
.
Thus (
12.19
) follows.

To conclude, choosing
\omega
and
\varepsilon
small enough and applying Lemma
12.4
, we deduce that
\pi_{x}(E_{\ell,\sigma})\subset B_{1/2}(z)
can be covered by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha/4)\ell}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell}
.
Therefore, since the set of all possible choices of
\sigma\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
has
2^{\ell+1}
elements, we see that each set
\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})
can be covered by
2^{\ell+1}\rho^{-(m+\alpha/4)\ell}
balls of radius
\rho^{\ell}
.
Since
\rho
was chosen so that
\rho^{\alpha/4}\geq\frac{1}{4}
, this implies that
\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})
can be covered by
\rho^{-(m+\alpha/2)\ell}
balls of radius
\varrho^{\ell}
.

Adding these bounds over
\ell\geq k
and letting
k\to\infty
, we conclude that
\pi_{x}(E_{\ell})
has zero
\mathcal{H}^{m+\alpha}
-measure (cp. proof of Lemma
12.6
).
∎

We can finally prove Proposition
12.2
.

Proof of Proposition
12.2
.

Let
\delta>0
be given by Corollary
11.2
, and split
\Sigma^{*}=\cup_{\ell}\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
. It suffices to show that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\setminus\Sigma^{\diamond})\leq n-2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and any
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
, let
\omega_{\varepsilon}
be given by (
12.3
).
By Lemma
12.3
, if
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})>0
then
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{\diamond}
.
On the other hand, Proposition
12.7
applied with
m=n-2
and
E=\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}
implies that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(\{(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}:\omega_{\varepsilon}(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})=0\ \text{for all}\,\varepsilon>0\})\leq n-2
.
Thus
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma^{*}_{\delta,\ell}\setminus\Sigma^{\diamond})\leq n-2
.
∎

13.
Enhanced decay towards polynomial Ansatz

In this section we will show that, once we have an estimate of order
3+\beta
for some
\beta>0
at a singular point, then we can actually prove a
C^{\infty}
estimate.

Assume with not loss of generality that
(0,0)\in\Sigma^{\diamond}
(see Definition
12.1
).
Then there exist
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
and
C_{\circ}>0
such that

(13.1)

\big\|(u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*})_{r}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq C_{\circ}r^{3+2\beta}\quad\mbox{for all }r\in(0,1).

Moreover, we may choose coordinates so that
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
and
p_{3}^{*}=a|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)+p_{3}
.

In the following result, and throughout this section, we denote

(13.2)

\Omega^{\beta}_{R}:=\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,|x|<R(-t)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}},\ t\leq 0\right\}.

Recall that
\mathscr{C}_{r}:=B_{r}\times(-r^{2},0)
denotes a parabolic cylinder.
Also, , to avoid unnecessary parentheses, given
z\in\mathbb{R}
we denote
z_{+}^{2}=(z_{+})^{2}
(and analogously for
z_{-}^{2}
).

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a solution of (
3.1
) and assume
(0,0)\in\Sigma^{\diamond}
.
Then there exists
r_{\circ}>0
, depending only on
n
,
\beta
, and
C_{\circ}
, such that inside

V:=\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}

the positivity set
\{u>0\}
has two connected components and
u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
in
V
, where
u^{(i)}
are two solutions of the parabolic obstacle problem inside
V
with disjoint supports satisfying

(13.3)

\frac{u^{(1)}(rx)}{r^{2}}\to\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{+}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{u^{(2)}(rx)}{r^{2}}\to\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{-}^{2}\qquad\text{as }r\to 0.

This result will allow us to break the function
u
—which has a singular point at the origin— into two separate functions
u_{1}
and
u_{1}
that behave
as if
the origin was a
regular
point for each of them.
This is because, with the parabolic scaling, the domain
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}
near the origin is almost equivalent to a full cylinder.

To prove Theorem
13.1
we need a barrier argument to show that the contact set
\{u=0\}
splits
\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}
into two disconnected pieces.
This barrier is constructed in the following:

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
u:B_{1}\times(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
be a solution of (
3.1
), and assume that
(0,0)\in\Sigma^{\diamond}
, i.e.,
u
satisfies
13.1
.
Choose coordinates so that
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
and
p_{3}^{*}=a|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)+p_{3}
.

Then there exists
r_{\circ}>0
, depending only on
n
,
\beta
, and
C_{\circ}
, such that

\{x_{n}+p_{3}/x_{n}=0\}\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}\subset\{u=0\}.

(Recall that
p_{3}(x,t)
is an odd polynomial, hence it is divisible by
x_{n}
.)

Proof.

First of all we note that, by exactly the same argument as the one given in Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
11.1
, the
L^{2}
bound from (
13.1
) can be upgraded to and
L^{\infty}
(up to enlarging the constant
C_{\circ}
).
Therefore, we may assume that

(13.4)

\big\|u-p_{2}-p_{3}^{*}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{r})}\leq C_{\circ}r^{3+2\beta}\quad\mbox{for all }r\in(0,1),

where
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
.

Now, let
\boldsymbol{P}(x,t):=\frac{1}{2}(x_{n}+p_{3}(x,t)/x_{n})^{2}
and fix
(\bar{x},\bar{t})\in\{\boldsymbol{P}=0\}\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}
, with
r_{\circ}
small to be chosen.
We need to prove that
u(\bar{x},\bar{t})=0
.

First observe that, since
p_{3}^{*}=a|x_{n}|(x_{n}^{2}+6t)+p_{3}
with
a\geq c_{1}>0
(see Lemma
9.8
), (
13.4
) implies that

(13.5)

\frac{1}{r^{3}}(u-\boldsymbol{P})_{r}\leq c|x_{n}|t+C|x_{n}|^{3}+C_{\circ}r^{2\beta}\quad\mbox{in }\mathscr{C}_{2}.

We will use a modification of the proof of Lemma
10.2
, using as barrier

\Psi_{\bar{y},\bar{s}}(x,t):=r^{-3}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}(x,t)+r^{\beta}\big(|x^{\prime}-\bar{y}^{\prime}|^{2}-(t-\bar{s})-2n\,r^{-2}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}(x,t)\big),

where
(\bar{y},\bar{s})\in\{\boldsymbol{P}_{r}=0\}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}
is any given point satisfying
\bar{s}\leq r^{\beta}
.

Note that
\text{\small\bf H}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}=r^{2}+O(r^{4})
inside
\mathscr{C}_{1}
.
Hence, for
r
sufficiently small we have

\text{\small\bf H}\Psi_{\bar{y},\bar{s}}\leq\frac{1}{r}+Cr+Cr^{\beta}\big(2(n-1)+1-2n(1+O(r^{2}))\big)\leq\frac{1}{r}.

Consider now the domain
U_{\varrho}:=\left\{|x^{\prime}-\bar{y}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{10},|x_{n}|\leq\varrho,\frac{1}{10}\leq t-\bar{s}\leq 0\right\}
with
\varrho:=r^{2\beta/3}
.
Using (
13.5
) and the fact that
r^{-2}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}+O(r)
and
\bar{s}\leq r^{\beta}
, on
\partial_{par}U_{\varrho}\cap\{|x_{n}|=\varrho\}
we have

(13.6)

r^{-3}u_{r}-\Psi_{\bar{y},\bar{s}}\leq 2n\,r^{\beta}r^{-2}\boldsymbol{P}_{r}+\frac{c}{2}\bar{s}\varrho+C\varrho^{3}+C_{\circ}r^{2\beta}\\
\leq 2n\,r^{\beta}(\varrho^{2}+Cr)-\frac{c}{2}r^{\beta}\varrho+C\varrho^{3}+C_{\circ}r^{2\beta}\leq Cr^{2\beta}-\frac{c}{2}r^{5\beta/3}<0

for
r
small enough.
Also it is easy to show that also
r^{-3}u_{r}-\Psi_{\bar{x},\bar{t}}\leq 0
on the remaining pieces of
\partial_{par}U_{\varrho}
, thanks to the positive term
r^{\beta}\big(|x^{\prime}-\bar{y}^{\prime}|-(t-\bar{s})\big)
appearing in the definition of
\Psi_{\bar{x},\bar{t}}
.

Hence, since
r^{-3}\text{\small\bf H}u_{r}=\frac{1}{r}\geq\text{\small\bf H}\Psi_{\bar{x},\bar{t}}
inside
\{u_{r}>0\}
and
\Psi_{\bar{x},\bar{t}}
is nonnegative, it follows from the maximum principle that

0\leq r^{-3}u_{r}\leq\Psi_{\bar{y},\bar{s}}\quad\mbox{in }U_{\varrho}.

In particular, evaluating at
(\bar{y},\bar{s})
we obtain

0\leq r^{-3}u_{r}(\bar{y},\bar{s})\leq\Psi_{\bar{x},\bar{t}}(\bar{x},\bar{t})=0
.
Since
(\bar{y},\bar{s})\in\{\boldsymbol{P}_{r}=0\}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}
was an arbitrary point satisfying
\bar{s}\leq r^{\beta}
, after rescaling we obtain

u=0\quad\mbox{on }\{\boldsymbol{P}=0\}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r}\cap\left\{t\leq-r^{2+\beta}\right\}

whenever
r
is sufficiently small, as desired.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
13.1
.

Proof of Theorem
13.1
.

Using Lemma
13.2
we see that
\{u>0\}
is split into two connected components inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}
— the number of connected components cannot be larger since
\partial_{nn}u\geq 0
in
B_{r_{\circ}}\times(-r_{\circ}^{2},r_{\circ}^{2})
thanks to Lemma
3.8
.
Then
u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
, where
u^{(1)}
and
u^{(2)}
are respectively supported in
\{x_{n}+p_{3}(x,t)/x_{n}>0\}
and
\{x_{n}+p_{3}(x,t)/x_{n}<0\}
inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}
.
Finally, (
13.3
) follows from the convergence
r^{-2}u(rx)\to\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
and the fact that, after rescaling, the positivity set of
u^{(1)}
(resp.
u^{(2)}
) converges to
\{x_{n}>0\}
(resp.
\{x_{n}<0\}
).
∎

Once
u
is split into two separate functions
u^{(1)}
and
u^{(2)}
in
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}}
, we now look for a
C^{\infty}
expansion for each of these two functions.
For this, we need first to construct a series of ansatz for the Taylor expansion of these functions.

Definition 13.3
.

Let
k\geq 3
, and let
(Q_{\ell})_{2\leq\ell\leq k-1}
be a family of polynomials such that
Q_{\ell}=Q_{\ell}(x,t)
is (parabolically) homogeneous of degree
\ell
and satisfies

\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{\ell})\equiv 0.

We define the polynomial
\mathscr{A}_{k}=\mathscr{A}_{k}[Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}]
as follows.

For the base case
k=3
, we set

\mathscr{A}_{3}=\mathscr{A}_{3}[Q_{2}](x,t):=x_{n}+Q_{2}(x,t)+x_{n}R_{2}(x,t),

where
R_{2}
is the unique 2-homogeneous caloric polynomial satisfying
8
8

8

The fact that (
13.7
) has exactly one solution can be shown as follows: write

\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(Q_{2})^{2}\bigg)=:T_{2,2}(x^{\prime})+x_{n}T_{2,1}(x^{\prime})+x_{n}^{2}T_{2,0},

where
T_{2}^{\ell}
is an
\ell
-homogeneous polynomial in the variables
x^{\prime}
, and

R_{2}=S_{2,2}(x^{\prime})+x_{n}S_{2,1}(x^{\prime})+x_{n}^{2}S_{x,0},

where
S_{2,\ell}
is a
\ell
-homogeneous polynomial in the variables
x^{\prime}
.
Then (
13.7
) amounts to

2S_{2,2}(x^{\prime})+6x_{n}S_{2,1}(x^{\prime})+x_{n}^{2}(12S_{2,0}+\text{\small\bf H}S_{2,2})=-T_{2,2}(x^{\prime})-x_{n}T_{2,1}(x^{\prime})-x_{n}^{2}T_{2,0},

which leads to a linear system with triangular structure, for which the unique solution is given by

S_{2,2}=-\frac{1}{2}T_{2,2},\qquad S_{2,1}=-\frac{1}{6}T_{2,1},\qquad S_{2,0}=-\frac{1}{12}(T_{2,0}+\text{\small\bf H}S_{2,2}).

(13.7)

\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}(Q_{2})^{2}+x_{n}^{2}R_{2}\bigg)=0.

Note that (
13.7
) is equivalent to

\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{3}^{2}\bigg)=1+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{3}\big).

For
k\geq 3
, we inductively define

\mathscr{A}_{k+1}\big[(Q_{\ell})_{2\leq\ell\leq k}\big]:=\mathscr{A}_{k}\big[(Q_{\ell})_{2\leq\ell\leq k-1}\big]+Q_{k}+x_{n}R_{k},

where
R_{k}
is a
k
-homogeneous caloric polynomial characterized by the identity

(13.8)

\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{k+1}^{2}\bigg)=1+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{k+1}\big).

As before, this equation has exactly one solution
R_{k}
.
Indeed, since
\mathscr{A}_{k}=x_{n}+O(|x|^{2}+|t|)
, it follows by induction that

\begin{split}\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{k+1}^{2}\bigg)=\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{k}^{2}+x_{n}Q_{k}+Q_{2}Q_{k-1}+x_{n}^{2}R_{k}\bigg)+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{k+1}\big)\end{split}

Hence, if we define

\text{\small\bf H}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{k}^{2}+x_{n}Q_{k}+Q_{2}Q_{k-1}\bigg)=:1+\sum_{j=0}^{k}x_{n}^{k-j}T_{k,j}(x^{\prime})+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{k+1}\big)

and write

R_{k}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}x_{n}^{k-j}S_{k,j}(x^{\prime}),

then (
13.8
) is equivalent to

\sum_{j=0}^{k}(k-j+2)(k-j+1)x_{n}^{k-j}S_{k,j}(x^{\prime})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}x_{n}^{k-j}\text{\small\bf H}S_{k,j+2}=-\sum_{j=0}^{k}x_{n}^{k-j}T_{k,j}(x^{\prime}).

This leads to a triangular system, whose unique solution is given by

S_{k,k}=-\frac{1}{2}T_{k,k},\quad S_{k,k-1}=-\frac{1}{6}T_{k,k-1},\quad S_{k,j}=-\frac{1}{(k-j+2)(k-j+1)}(T_{k,j}+\text{\small\bf H}S_{k,j+2})\quad\text{for }j\in\{0,\ldots,k-2\}.

The previous definition aims to construct a Taylor expansion which is compatible with the PDE satisfied by our solution.
However, the previous formulas do not take care of possible translations and rotation. This is the purpose of the next definition.

Definition 13.4
.

Let
k\geq 3
, and let
(Q_{\ell})_{2\leq\ell\leq k-1}
be a family of parabolically homogeneous polynomials of degree
\ell
satisfying

\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{\ell})\equiv 0.

Then, given
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and a rotation
\boldsymbol{R}\in SO(n)
, we define

\mathscr{P}_{k}=\mathscr{P}_{k}\big[Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1},\tau,\boldsymbol{R}\big](x,t)

by

\mathscr{P}_{k}(x,t):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k}\big[Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big]\right)_{+}^{2}(\boldsymbol{R}(x+\tau\boldsymbol{e}_{n}),t),

where
\mathscr{A}_{k}
is given in Definition
13.3
.

Our next goal will be to prove an
\varepsilon
-regularity result in the domain
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}
.
Notice that this result is for “one-sided” solutions, i.e., it will be applied separately to
u^{(1)}
and
u^{(2)}
.

Theorem 13.5
.

Given
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta>0
,
k\geq 4
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exist positive constants
\bar{r}
and
\varepsilon_{\circ}
, depending only on
n
,
\beta
, and
k
, such that the following holds.

Let
u
satisfy
\text{\small\bf H}u=\chi_{\{u>0\}}
,
u\geq 0
, and
\partial_{t}u\geq 0
inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}
. Assume that
u(0,0)=0
and

\bigg\|u-\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{+}^{2}\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\beta}_{1}\cap\mathscr{C}_{r_{\circ}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}.

Then, for some Ansatz
\mathscr{P}_{k}=\mathscr{P}_{k}\big[(Q_{\ell})_{2\leq\ell\leq k-1},\tau,\boldsymbol{R}\big]
we have

\|u-\mathscr{P}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r}\times(-r^{2},-\delta r^{2}))}\leq r^{k+1+\alpha}

for all
r\in(0,\bar{r})
.

As a corollary of this result, it follows that the free boundary is
C^{\infty}
near every
regular
free boundary point.
However, thanks to the fact that our result looks at the solution only inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{1}
, thanks to Theorem
13.1
it can be applied as well to the case of singular points in
\Sigma^{\diamond}
.

Before proving Theorem
13.5
, we will need several ingredients.
We start with a compactness result.

Lemma 13.6
.

Given
n
,
k
,
\beta
,
\alpha
, and
\varepsilon_{\circ}
, there exists a positive constant
M_{\circ}
such that the following holds.
Assume that
N_{\circ}\geq M_{\circ}
and that
w:\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{M_{\circ}}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(13.9)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}|w_{2^{m}}|^{2}+|\nabla w_{2^{m}}|^{2}+|\partial_{t}w_{2^{m}}|^{2}\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq(2^{m})^{k+1+\alpha}\quad\mbox{for }0\leq m\leq M_{\circ},

where
w_{2^{m}}(x,t):=w(2^{m}x,4^{m}t)
.
Assume in addition that

(13.10)

\begin{cases}|\text{\small\bf H}w|\leq\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}\ &\mbox{ in }\ \Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\big(B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-M_{\circ},-{\textstyle\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}})\big)\cap\big\{x_{n}\geq{\textstyle\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}}\big\}\\
w=0&\mbox{ in }\ \Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\big(B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-M_{\circ},-{\textstyle\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}})\big)\cap\big\{x_{n}\leq-{\textstyle\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}}\big\}.\end{cases}

Finally, suppose that

(13.11)

\bigg|\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}}(x_{n})_{+}w\,Q\,G\,dx\,dt\bigg|\leq\frac{1}{M_{\circ}}

for any
Q=Q(x,t)
parabolically
\ell
-homogeneous polynomial for some
0\leq\ell\leq k
which satisfies
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q)=0
.
Then

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}|w|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}.

Proof.

We split the proof in two steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. We show the following classification result for ancient solutions in the whole space.
Let
w:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0)\to\mathbb{R}
be such that both
|w|^{2}
and
|\nabla w|^{2}
are locally integrable and satisfy

(13.12)

\bigg(\int_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}|w_{2^{m}}|^{2}+|\nabla w_{2^{m}}|^{2}+|\partial_{t}w_{2^{m}}|^{2}\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq(2^{m})^{k+1+\alpha}\quad\mbox{for all }m\geq 1,

and assume in addition that

(13.13)

\text{\small\bf H}w=0\quad\mbox{ in }\{x_{n}>0\},\qquad w|_{\{x_{n}\leq 0\}}\equiv 0,

and

(13.14)

\int_{-2}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(x_{n})_{+}w\,Q\,G\,dx\,dt=0

for any polynomial
Q=Q(x,t)
which parabolically
\ell
-homogeneous for some
0\leq\ell\leq k
and satisfies
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q)=0
.
Then,

w\equiv 0.

To show this we notice that, thanks to (
13.12
) and (
13.13
), it follows from the Liouville Theorem for the heat equation that the odd extension of
w
across
\{x_{n}=0\}
is a caloric polynomial of degree at most
k+1
vanishing on
x_{n}=0
. Since (
13.14
) implies that
w
must be orthogonal to all such polynomials (recall that
w
vanished for
x_{n}\leq 0
), we conclude that
w\equiv 0
.

\bullet

Step 2
. Since
H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
embeds compactly in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}((\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
,
the desired result follows immediately by compactness as
M_{\circ}\to\infty
.
∎

We also need the following estimate.
Notice that when the integrals below are taken in
\mathscr{C}_{2}
and
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, then the estimate is more standard —and we proved it in Lemma
6.1
.
Now, we need such estimate in the new domains
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}
, and thus it becomes a bit more delicate.

Lemma 13.7
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1)
, and let
u_{i}:\mathscr{C}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2,
be two solutions of
(
6.1
)
inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{2}
, with
|\varepsilon_{i}(x,t)|\leq\bar{\varepsilon}<\frac{1}{100}
. Set
w:=u_{1}-u_{2}
.
Then, for any
\theta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
there exist constants
R_{\circ},C>0
, depending only on
n
,
\beta
, and
\theta
, such that

(13.15)

\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta^{2}R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}|\nabla w|^{2}+|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|+|\partial_{t}w|^{2}\leq C\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{2}}w^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg)\qquad\forall\,R\geq R_{\circ}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma (
6.1
) we have

w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|w|\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{t}w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|.

The idea is to follow the proof of Lemma (
6.1
), but multiplying the spatial cut-offs
\tilde{\eta}(x)
and
\eta(x)
by an extra appropriately chosen space-time cut-off in order to make sure that we only
evaluate the equation inside its domain
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{2}
.

Set
\alpha:=\frac{1}{2+\beta}
and note that
\alpha\in(1/3,1/2)
.
The new cut-off will be of the form

\xi^{\beta}_{R}=\xi^{\beta}_{R}(x,t):=\xi\left(\frac{|x|}{R(-t)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}}\right)\quad\mbox{for some suitable cut-off $\xi\in C^{1,1}([0,1])$ satisfying $\xi|_{[0,\theta]}\equiv 1$}.

More precisely, we choose
\xi\in C^{1,1}([0,1])
which satisfies the following properties:

(13.16)

\xi(s):=\begin{cases}1\quad&\mbox{for }s\in[0,\theta]\\
\mbox{concave and decreasing}&\mbox{for }s\in\big[\theta,{\textstyle\frac{\theta+1}{2}}\big]\\
(1-s)^{2+p}&\mbox{for }s\in\big[{\textstyle\frac{\theta+1}{2}},1\big],\end{cases}

where
p\in(2,\infty)
satisfies
\frac{p}{p+1}=2\alpha
.
It is important to notice that, since
\xi^{\prime}\leq 0
,

(13.17)

\partial_{t}\xi^{\beta}_{R}(x,t)\leq 0.

We claim that

(13.18)

\Delta\xi^{\beta}_{R}+\partial_{t}\xi^{\beta}_{R}\leq C_{\circ},\quad\mbox{ for }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-4,0),

where
C_{\circ}
depends only on
n
,
\theta
,
\beta
, but not on
R
.

Indeed, defining
s=s(x,t):=\frac{|x|}{R(-t)^{\alpha}}
, since the inequality is trivial for
s\leq\theta
we have

\Delta\xi^{\beta}_{R}+\partial_{t}\xi^{\beta}_{R}=\frac{1}{R^{2}(-t)^{2\alpha}}\bigg(\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)+\frac{(n-1)}{s}\xi^{\prime}(s)\bigg)+\xi^{\prime}(s)\frac{\alpha s}{(-t)}\leq\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)}{R^{2}(-t)^{2\alpha}}+\frac{\xi^{\prime}(s)}{6(-t)},

where we used that
\xi^{\prime}\leq 0
and that
\alpha s\geq 1/6
for
s\geq\theta\geq 1/2
.
Since
\xi
is concave and decreasing inside
\big[\theta,\frac{\theta+1}{2}\big]
, the right hand side above is negative for
s\in\big[\theta,\frac{\theta+1}{2}\big]
. Hence, to prove (
13.18
) we only need to bound the previous expression for
s\in\big[\frac{\theta+1}{2},1\big]
, where we have
\xi(s)=(1-s)^{2+p}
. Recalling that
2\alpha=\frac{p}{p+1}
and writing
\tau:=(1-s)^{p+1}
,
for
R\geq 1
we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{R^{2}(-t)^{2\alpha}}\xi^{\prime\prime}(s)+\frac{\xi^{\prime}(s)}{-6t}&=(2+p)\bigg(\frac{(1+p)}{R^{2}(-t)^{2\alpha}}(1-s)^{p}+\frac{1}{6t}(1-s)^{p+1}\bigg)\\
&=\frac{(2+p)}{6}\bigg(\frac{6(1+p)}{R^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\tau}{-t}\bigg)^{2\alpha}-\bigg(\frac{\tau}{-t}\bigg)\bigg)\leq C_{p,\theta}\qquad\forall\,\tau\in(0,1),\end{split}

where we used that
2\alpha\in(0,1)
. This concludes the proof of (
13.18
).

We also note that

(13.19)

32|\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{R}|+\partial_{t}\xi^{\beta}_{R}\leq 0\quad\mbox{ for }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-4,0).

Indeed, as before we only need to check the inequality for
s\geq\theta
, and we have

32|\nabla\xi_{\beta}|+\partial_{t}\xi_{\beta}=\frac{32}{R(-t)^{\alpha}}|\xi^{\prime}(s)|-\xi^{\prime}(s)\frac{\alpha s}{t}\leq\frac{32}{R(-t)^{\alpha}}|\xi^{\prime}(s)|+\xi^{\prime}(s)\frac{1}{6(-t)}\leq 0,

provided that
R\geq R_{\circ}
is large enough.

We can now prove (
13.15
). As in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, let

(13.20)

\tilde{\eta}=\tilde{\eta}(x)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{5/3})\quad\mbox{such that $\tilde{\eta}\equiv 1$ in $\overline{B}_{4/3}$, $0\leq\tilde{\eta}\leq 1$ in $B_{5/3}$, and $|\nabla\tilde{\eta}|+|D^{2}\tilde{\eta}|\leq 10$.}

Then

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\tilde{\eta}\,\xi^{\beta}_{R}+2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(w\,\text{\small\bf H}w+|\nabla w|^{2})\,\eta\,\tilde{\xi}^{\beta}_{R}&=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}w^{2}(\partial_{t}+\Delta)\big(\tilde{\eta}\xi^{\beta}_{R}\big)\\
&=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\big(\tilde{\eta}(\partial_{t}+\Delta)\xi^{\beta}_{R}+2\nabla\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{R}+\Delta\tilde{\eta}\,\xi^{\beta}_{R}\big).\end{split}

Note that, thanks to (
13.18
),
\tilde{\eta}(\partial_{t}+\Delta)\xi^{\beta}_{R}\leq C_{\circ}\chi_{B_{5/3}}(x)\chi_{\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}
. Also, the term
\nabla\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{R}
is also bounded because
\frac{1}{R(-t)^{\alpha}}\sim 1
on the intersection of the supports of

\nabla\tilde{\eta}
and
\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{R}
.
Therefore, we obtain

(13.21)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\tilde{\eta}\,\xi^{\beta}_{R}+2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(w\,\text{\small\bf H}w+|\nabla w|^{2})\,\tilde{\eta}\,\xi^{\beta}_{R}\leq C\int_{B_{5/3}\times\{t\}}w^{2}\chi_{\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}.

Integrating (
13.21
) with respect to
t\in(-T_{*},0)
where
T_{*}\in(3/2,2)
satisfies

\int_{(B_{5/3}\times\{-T_{*}\})\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}w^{2}\xi^{\beta}_{R}\leq 10\int_{(B_{5/3}\times(-2,0))\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}w^{2}\xi^{\beta}_{R},

and recalling that
w\,\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2\bar{\varepsilon}|w|
and that, by construction,
\tilde{\eta}\equiv 1
in
B_{4/3}
and
\xi^{\beta}_{R}\equiv 1
in
\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}
, we obtain

(13.22)

\begin{split}\int_{-3/2}^{0}\int_{B_{4/3}}\big(|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)\chi_{\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}}\leq C\bigg(\int_{(B_{5/3}\times(-2,0))\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}w^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}|w|\bigg)\leq C\bigg(\int_{(B_{5/3}\times(-2,0))\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{R}}w^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg).\end{split}

This proves the estimates for
|\nabla w|^{2}
and
|w\,\text{\small\bf H}w|
in (
13.15
).

To control
|\partial_{t}w|^{2}
, we choose a spatial cut-off
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{4/3})
such that
\eta\equiv 1
in
\overline{B}_{1}
. Since
\partial_{t}w\text{\small\bf H}w\geq-2|\partial_{t}w|\bar{\varepsilon}
we obtain (using the inequality
4ab\leq\frac{2}{M}a^{2}+2Mb^{2}
and choosing
M=8
)

\begin{split}2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}&(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}-\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\partial_{t}\big[\eta^{2}\,(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}\big]=\\
&=2\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}2\nabla w\cdot\nabla\partial_{t}w\,\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}\\
&=\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}2(\partial_{t}w-\Delta w)\,\partial_{t}w\,\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}-4\nabla w\cdot\nabla\eta\,\partial_{t}w\,\eta\,(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}-4\partial_{t}w\,\nabla w\cdot\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}\,\eta^{2}\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}\\
&\leq\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}4\bar{\varepsilon}|\partial_{t}w|\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}4M|\nabla w|^{2}\big(|\nabla\eta|^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+|\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}|^{2}\eta^{2}\big)+\frac{4}{M}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}\\
&\leq\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+C\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}+32\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\big(|\nabla\eta|^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+|\nabla\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}|^{2}\eta^{2}\big).\end{split}

Hence, recalling (
13.19
), since
\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}
is supported in
\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}
, we obtain

\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}+\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{2}\times\{t\}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}\leq C\bigg(\int_{(B_{4/3}\times\{t\})\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}}|\nabla w|^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg).

Integrating with respect to
t\in(-T_{\diamond},0)
where
T_{\diamond}\in(1,3/2)
is chosen so to satisfy

\int_{(B_{4/3}\times\{T_{\diamond}\})\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2}\leq 10\int_{(B_{4/3}\times(-3/2,1))\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta R}}|\nabla w|^{2}\eta^{2}(\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R})^{2},

and recalling that
\eta\equiv 1
in
B_{1}
and
\xi^{\beta}_{\theta R}\equiv 1
in
\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta^{2}R}
, we finally get

\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta^{2}R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}|\partial_{t}w|^{2}\leq C\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{R}\cap\mathscr{C}_{2}}|\nabla w|^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigg).

This completes the proof of the estimate (
13.15
), and the Lemma follows.
∎

Recall now the set of Ansatz given in Definition
13.4
. We note that our parametrization of possible Ansatz
given in Definition
13.4
is non-injective: for every
{\boldsymbol{S}}\in SO(n)
that fixes
\{x_{n}=0\}
we have

(13.23)

\mathscr{P}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R}{\boldsymbol{S}^{-1}},{\boldsymbol{S}}_{*}Q_{2},\dots,{\boldsymbol{S}}_{*}Q_{k-1}\big]=\mathscr{P}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big],\quad\mbox{where}\ {\boldsymbol{S}}_{*}P(x,t):=P({\boldsymbol{S}}x,t).

In Lemma
13.9
below, we will consider the set of Ansatz that are a “small perturbation” of
\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{+}^{2}
,
and it will be convenient to provide an injective and smooth parametrization of such subset.

Definition 13.8
.

Given
\delta>0
small,
let

\mathcal{N}_{\delta}:=\big\{\mbox{$\mathscr{P}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big]$ : $|\tau|\leq\delta$, \,$|\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{n}-\boldsymbol{e}_{n}|\leq\delta$, \,$\max_{\ell}\|Q_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(Q_{1})}\leq\delta$}\big\}.

Our goal is to find a smooth bijective parameterization of
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
.
Keeping (
13.23
) in mind, given
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
with
|e-\boldsymbol{e}_{n}|\leq\delta
we define

\boldsymbol{R}_{e}:=\arg\min\big\{\|\boldsymbol{R}-{\rm Id}\|_{HS}\ :\ \boldsymbol{R}\in SO(n),\quad\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{n}=e\big\}.

Note that
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}
is a rotation of angle
\angle(\boldsymbol{e}_{n},e)
in the plane generated by
\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
and
e
and leaves all the vectors orthogonal to this plane invariant (when
e=\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
then
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}={Id}
).
