So, it makes sense to consider the map

(13.24)

\big(\tau,e,Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big)\ \longmapsto\ \mathscr{P}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R}_{e},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big],

and we want to show that it is a bijection for
\delta
small.

For technical convenience, instead of considering
\mathscr{P}_{k}
, it makes sense to replace it with the polynomial of degree
2k
that equals
\mathscr{P}_{k}
inside
\{\mathscr{P}_{k}>0\}
, namely

(13.25)

\mathscr{P}_{k}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k}\big[Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big]\right)_{+}^{2}(\boldsymbol{R}(x+\tau\boldsymbol{e}_{n}),t)\quad\leadsto\quad\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k}\big[Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big]\right)^{2}(\boldsymbol{R}(x+\tau\boldsymbol{e}_{n}),t).

In this way, the map (
13.24
) takes value in the smooth manifold of
2k
homogeneous polynomials.
In the next lemma we compute the differential of this map at
\tau=0
,
e=\boldsymbol{e}_{n}
,
(Q_{\ell})=0
, and prove that it is a smooth diffeomorphism for
\delta
small.
Also, we show that
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}
is completely determined, in a continuous way, by its coefficients of degree
\leq k
.

Lemma 13.9
.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{k},\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
be as in Definitions
13.4
and
13.8
.
For
\mathscr{P}_{k}\in\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
, let
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}
denote the polynomial of degree
2k
which equals
\mathscr{P}_{k}
in
\{\mathscr{P}_{k}>0\}
, see (
13.25
).
Then, the differential of the map

(13.26)

\big(\tau,e,Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big)\ \mapsto\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R}_{e},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big]

at the point
\mathcal{O}:=\big(\tau=0,e=\boldsymbol{e}_{n},Q_{2}=0,\dots,Q_{k-1}=0\big)
has the following diagonal structure:

(13.27)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big[\varepsilon\tau^{\prime},\boldsymbol{R}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}},0,\dots,0\big]&=x_{n}\tau^{\prime}\\
\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big[0,\boldsymbol{R}_{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}+\varepsilon e^{\prime}}{|\boldsymbol{e}_{n}+\varepsilon e^{\prime}|}},0,\dots,0\big]&=x_{n}(x\cdot e^{\prime})\\
\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big[0,\boldsymbol{R}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}},0,\dots,\varepsilon Q_{\ell}^{\prime},\dots,0\big]&=x_{n}Q_{\ell}^{\prime}\qquad\mbox{for }2\leq\ell\leq k-1\end{split}

where
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
, and
Q_{\ell}^{\prime}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
\ell
satisfying
\text{\small\bf H}(x_{n}Q_{\ell}^{\prime})=0
.
In particular, (
13.26
) gives a diffeomorphic parametrization of
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
.

Moreover, for
0\leq i\leq 2k
, let
\varpi_{i}[\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}]
denote the
i
-homogeneous part of
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}
. Then the differential of the map

\big(\tau,e,Q_{2},Q_{3},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big)\ \mapsto\left(\varpi_{1}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big),\varpi_{2}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big),\dots,\varpi_{k}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big)\right)

at
\mathcal{O}
has maximal rank. In particular there exists
\delta_{k}>0
small enough such that, inside
\mathcal{N}_{\delta_{k}}
, the whole polynomial
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}
is determined (in a continuous way) by its coefficients of terms of degree
\leq k
.

Proof.

The result follows from a direct computation, recalling that

\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}\big[\tau,\boldsymbol{R}_{e},Q_{2},Q_{3},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big](x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\big(x_{n}+Q_{2}+x_{n}R_{2}+\dots+Q_{k-1}+x_{n}R_{k-1}\big)^{2}(\boldsymbol{R}_{e}x+\tau\boldsymbol{e}_{n},t).

Also, for
\delta
small, the map is a diffeomorphism thanks to the implicit function theorem.
∎

Combining all the previous results we can now prove the following result, from which we will deduce
Theorem
13.5
.

Proposition 13.10
.

Given
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta>0
, and
k\geq 3
, let
\delta=\delta_{k}
be given by Lemma
13.9
. There exist positive constants
N_{\circ}
and
\varepsilon_{\circ}
, depending only on
n
,
\beta
, and
k
, such that the following holds.

Let
u
satisfy
\text{\small\bf H}u=\chi_{\{u>0\}}
,
u\geq 0
, and
\partial_{t}u\geq 0
inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}
.
Assume that, for some integer
j\geq N_{\circ}
and a sequence of Ansatz
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(\ell)}
, we have

(13.28)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{2}(2^{-m}x,4^{-m}t)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\quad\mbox{for }0\leq m\leq j,

where

(13.29)

\left\|\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{+}^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}.

Suppose also that

(13.30)

\partial\big\{u(2^{-j}\,\cdot\,,4^{-j}\,\cdot\,)=0\big\}\cap\big(B_{N_{\circ}}\times\big(-N_{\circ},{\textstyle-\frac{1}{N_{\circ}}}\big)\big)\subset\big\{|x_{n}|\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}\big\}.

Then, at the next scale
j+1
, there exists a new Ansatz
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j+1)}\in\mathcal{N}_{\delta_{k}}
such that

(13.31)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j+1)}\right)^{2}\big(2^{-(j+1)}x,4^{-(j+1)}t\big)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}\big(2^{-(j+1)}\big)^{k+\alpha}.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{\circ}\in(0,1)
be chosen so that
\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta_{\circ}R}
is mapped to
\Omega^{\beta}_{R}
under the parabolic doubling of the scaling
(x,t)\mapsto(2x,4t)
. More precisly,

(x,t)\in\Omega^{\beta}_{\theta_{\circ}R}\ \Leftrightarrow\ |x|\leq\theta_{\circ}R(-t)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}\ \Leftrightarrow\ |2x|\leq\theta_{\circ}2^{\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}R(-4t)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}\ \Leftrightarrow\ (2x,4t)\in\Omega^{\beta}_{R}

leads to

\theta_{\circ}:=2^{-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}.

Given
M_{\circ}
large, we define
N_{\circ}=N_{\circ}(\beta,M_{\circ})
as

N_{\circ}:=M_{\circ}\theta_{\circ}^{-M_{\circ}}\gg M_{\circ}.

Defining
N_{\circ}
in this way we guarantee that

(13.32)

B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)\subset\Omega^{\beta}_{M_{\circ}}=\big\{(2^{-M_{\circ}}x,4^{-M_{\circ}}t)\ :\ (x,t)\in\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\big\}.

We divide the proof in four steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. We will first show that assumptions (
13.28
) and (
13.29
) imply

(13.33)

\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{\delta_{k}},\qquad\bigg\|\,\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}-\frac{1}{2}(x_{n})_{+}^{2}\,\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\leq\delta\quad\mbox{ for all }0\leq m\leq j,

where
\delta\leq\delta_{k}
can be made arbitrarily small (by decreasing
\varepsilon_{\circ}
).

Recall that, thanks to Lemma
13.9
, the Ansatz
\mathscr{P}^{(m)}_{k}=\mathscr{P}^{(m)}_{k}\big[(Q_{\ell}^{(m)})_{2\leq\ell\leq k-1},\sigma^{(m)},\tau^{(m)},\boldsymbol{R}^{(m)}\big]
is determined by its coefficients of degree
\leq k
(since these determine the spatial and time translations, the rotations, and the
Q_{\ell}
’s).

Set
K_{\circ}:=\mathscr{C}_{1/2}\cap\{x_{n}>\frac{1}{10},\,t\leq-\frac{1}{10}\}
and note that
K_{\circ}\subset\subset\{x_{n}>0\}\cap\Omega^{\beta}_{M_{\circ}}
.
Then, for
\varepsilon_{\circ}
sufficiently small,
K_{\circ}
will belong to the positivity set of both
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}
and
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m+1)}
.
Hence, defining
2^{-(m+1)}K_{\circ}:=\{(2^{-(m+1)}x,4^{-(m+1)}t)\,:\,(x,t)\in K_{\circ})\}
and using (
13.28
) for two consecutive scales, we obtain

\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{2^{-(m+1)}K_{\circ}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{2}dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{2^{-(m+1)}K_{\circ}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m+1)}\right)^{2}dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-(m+1)})^{k+\alpha},

therefore

\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{2^{-(m+1)}K_{\circ}}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m+1)}-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{2}dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha},

or equivalently

(13.34)

\bigg({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{K_{\circ}}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m+1)}-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{2}(2^{-(m+1)}x,4^{-(m+1)}t)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}.

This implies, with the notation of Lemma
13.9
, that

(13.35)

\left\|\varpi_{i}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}^{(m+1)}\big)-\varpi_{i}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}^{(m)}\big)\right\|\leq C\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha-i},

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes any of the (equivalent) norms on the linear space of polynomials of degree
2k
.
Summing this bound over
m
, we deduce that

\left\|\varpi_{i}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}^{(m)}\big)-\varpi_{i}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}^{(0)}\big)\right\|\leq C\varepsilon_{\circ}\quad\mbox{for all }1\leq i\leq k\mbox{ and }m\leq j.

Since
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2},\dots,\varpi_{k}
determine all of
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k}
in a continuous way (recall Lemma
13.9
), thanks to (
13.29
) we can guarantee that (
13.33
) holds by choosing
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
sufficiently small.

\bullet

Step 2
. Define

(13.36)

\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}:=\arg\min_{\mathscr{P}}\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)}(u-\mathscr{P})^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)G(x,t)\,dx\,dt.

We claim that, for some constant
C_{\circ}
depending on
n
and
\beta
, but not on
M_{\circ}
, we have

(13.37)

\biggl(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}\right)^{2}(2^{-m}x,4^{-m}t)\,dx\,dt\biggr)^{1/2}\leq C_{\circ}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad m\leq j.

Indeed, as a consequence of (
13.35
), summing the geometric series from
m
to
j-1
we get

(13.38)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j)}-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{2}(2^{-m}x,4^{-m}t)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad m\leq j,

that combined with (
13.28
) gives

(13.39)

\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}\right)^{2}(2^{-m}x,4^{-m}t)\,\,dx\,dt\leq C_{2}\left(\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\right)^{2}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad m\leq j.

Recalling (
13.32
), the bound above implies that

\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)\cap\mathscr{C}_{2^{\ell}}}\left(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}\right)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)\,\,dx\,dt\leq C_{2}2^{\ell p}\left(\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\right)^{2}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\ell\leq j,

for some large exponent
p=p(n,k)>0
. (Note that, since
j\geq N_{\circ}
,
B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)\subset\mathscr{C}_{2^{j}}
.) In particular this implies that

\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)\cap\left(\mathscr{C}_{2^{\ell}}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{2^{\ell-1}}\right)}\left(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}\right)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)\,\,dx\,dt\leq C_{2}2^{\ell p}\left(\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-m})^{k+\alpha}\right)^{2}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\ell\leq j,

and since the Gaussian Kernel is smaller than
Ce^{-2^{\ell}}
on the domain of integration (hence the exponential decay of
G(x,t)
“beats” the polynomial growth
2^{\ell p}
), we can find a constant
C_{3}
independent
9
9

9

This will be very important later since we will need to take
M_{\circ}
sufficiently large, and it will be crucial that the constant
C_{3}
stays bounded independently of
M_{\circ}
.
of
M_{\circ}
such that

(13.40)

\bigg(\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)}\big(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}\big)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)G(x,t)\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{3}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}\qquad\text{for}\quad j\geq N_{\circ}.

for
j\geq j_{\circ}
.
Hence, by definition of
\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}

\bigg(\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)}\big(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}\big)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)G(x,t)\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{3}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha},

and by triangle inequality we deduce that

\bigg(\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)}\big(\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}-\mathscr{P}^{(j)}\big)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)G(x,t)\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{3}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}.

Since
\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}
and
\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}
are positive parts of polynomials whose positivity set is approximately
\{x_{n}>0\}
(see Step 1), this implies

(13.41)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(\mathscr{P}^{(j)}_{k}-\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}\right)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{4}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}.

Combining this bound with (
13.38
) and (
13.39
), (
13.37
) follows.

\bullet

Step 3
. We now show that

(13.42)

\big|\text{\small\bf H}\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}-1\big|\leq C\left(|x|^{2}+|t|\right)^{k/2}\quad\mbox{inside }\mathscr{C}_{1},

where
C
is independent of
M_{\circ}
and
j
are.

Indeed, using (
13.41
) and (
13.35
) we obtain that the coefficients of
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j+1)}
are bounded, independently of
j
. Hence, by construction of
\mathscr{P}_{k}
(see (
13.8
)) we obtain (
13.42
).

\bullet

Step 4
. Define

w(x,t)=\frac{\big(u-\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}\big)(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)}{C_{\circ}\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}}.

We want to show that, if
M_{\circ}
is chosen large enough and
\delta>0
(from Step 1) is small enough, then Lemma
13.6
applies.

Indeed, assumption (
13.9
) follows from (
13.37
), (
13.42
), and Lemma
13.7
.
Also, since
\text{\small\bf H}u=\chi_{\{u>0\}}
and
\partial\{u(2^{-j}\,\cdot,4^{-j}\,\cdot\,)>0\}
is contained in a
1/M_{\circ}
-neighborhood of
\{x_{n}=0\}
(thanks to (
13.33
), (
13.41
), and (
13.37
)),
assumption (
13.10
) follows from (
13.42
).
Finally, by definition of
\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}
we have the optimality condition

(13.43)

\int_{B_{M_{\circ}}\times(-2,-1)}\big((u-\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k})\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\big)(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)G(x,t)\,dx\,dt=0,

where
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
is any element of the tangent space to the “manifold of Ansätze”
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}
at the “point”
\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}
(see Lemma
13.9
).
Since the defining parameters
\big(\tau,e,Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k-1}\big)
of
\mathscr{P}^{(j+1)}_{k}
are a small perturbation of
\mathcal{O}:=\big(\tau=0,e=\boldsymbol{e}_{n},Q_{2}=0,\dots,Q_{k-1}=0\big)

(cf. (
13.33
) and (
13.41
)), (
13.27
) and (
13.43
) imply the validity of assumption (
13.11
), provided that
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small.

Hence, by Lemma
13.6
,

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}|w|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\widetilde{\varepsilon_{\circ}},

where
\widetilde{\varepsilon_{\circ}}
is an arbitrarily small constant. In particular,
choosing
\widetilde{\varepsilon_{\circ}}=2^{-(k+\alpha)}/C_{\circ}
we obtain (
13.28
) for
m=j+1
, as wanted.
∎

We finally prove Theorem
13.5
.

Proof of Theorem
13.5
.

By choosing first
r_{\circ}>0
sufficiently small, and then
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
as small as needed, Proposition
13.10
can be applied to the function
\tilde{u}=r_{\circ}^{-2}u(r_{\circ}\,\cdot\,,r_{\circ}^{2}\,\cdot\,)

with
j=N_{\circ}
. But then the conclusion of Proposition
13.10
allows us to iterate it for any
j\geq N_{\circ}
, and we get

(13.44)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(\tilde{u}-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j)}\right)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}\quad\mbox{for all }N_{\circ}\leq j<\infty.

Also, Step 1 in the proof Proposition
13.10
(see in particular (
13.35
)) shows that the coefficients of
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(j)}
converge as
j\to\infty
,
hence
\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(m)}\to\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(\infty)}
for a certain limiting Ansatz.
Arguing as in Step 2 in the proof Proposition
13.10
(see in particular (
13.39
)), we also get

(13.45)

\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}}\left(\tilde{u}-\mathscr{P}_{k}^{(\infty)}\right)^{2}(2^{-j}x,4^{-j}t)\,dx\,dt\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{\circ}(2^{-j})^{k+\alpha}\quad\mbox{for }N_{\circ}\leq j<\infty.

Finally, for
r\ll 1
, we can use Lemma
6.1
to improve the obtained control in
L^{2}
inside
\Omega^{\beta}_{N_{\circ}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{1}
into an
L^{\infty}
-control inside the cylinder
B_{r}\times(-r^{2},-\delta r^{2})
, as wanted.
∎

14.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
and of its consequences

Combining the results of the previous sections, we can now prove our main Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Since this is a local regularity result, it suffices to prove it inside
B_{1}\times[-1,1]
.

Combining Proposition
5.7
, Proposition
7.8
, Lemma
9.7
, and Proposition
12.2
, we find that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{x}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\diamond})\big)\leq n-2
.
Therefore, by Corollary
8.8
and Lemma
8.9
, we deduce that
{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\diamond})\leq n-2
.

Then, we write
\Sigma^{\diamond}=\cup_{m\geq 1}\Sigma^{\diamond}_{m}
, where
\Sigma^{\diamond}_{m}
is defined as the set of points in
\Sigma_{\diamond}\cap\overline{B_{1-1/m}}\times[-1+1/m,1-1/m]
for which (
13.1
) holds for some
\beta\geq 1/m
and
C_{\circ}\leq m
.
We claim that each set
\Sigma^{\diamond}_{m}
can be covered by a
(n-1)-
manifold of class
C^{\infty}
.

Indeed, thanks to Theorems
13.1
and
13.5
, for every
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{\diamond}_{m}
and for all
r\in(0,r_{m,k})
, we have
u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
, where
u^{(i)}
have disjoint support and look like “half-space solutions” inside
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})+K_{r}
, where

K_{r}:=B_{r}\times\textstyle\big(-r^{2},-\frac{r^{2}}{100}\big).

Moreover, for any
k\geq 3
, there exist two sequences of Ansätze
\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(1)}
and
\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(2)}
, satisfying

(14.1)

\big\|u^{(i)}(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot)-\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(K_{r})}\leq C_{m,k}r^{k+\alpha}.

This implies in particular that the sequence of coefficients of
\mathscr{P}_{k}
is constant in
k
: more precisely

(14.2)

\varpi_{\ell}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}\big)=\varpi_{\ell}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k^{\prime},x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}\big)\quad\mbox{for all }\,\ell\leq\min(k,k^{\prime}),

where
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}
and
\varpi
are defined as in Lemma
13.9
.
In addition, as discussed in the proof of Theorem
13.5
, we have a uniform control on the coefficients:

(14.3)

\big|\varpi_{\ell}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}\big)\big|\leq C_{m,\ell}\quad\mbox{for all }0\leq\ell\leq k.

Furthermore, by non-degeneracy (see (
3.2
)), for all
(x_{1},t_{1})\in\big((x_{\circ},t_{\circ})+K_{r}\big)\cap\partial\{u^{(i)}>0\}
we have

(14.4)

\sup_{|z|\leq\varrho}u(x_{1}+z,t_{1})\geq c_{m}\varrho^{2}>0.

Assume now with no loss of generality that
(0,0)\in\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}
and
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{n}^{2}
,
and consider any other singular point
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
with
r_{m}\ll 1
. Then we have

p_{2,x_{\circ}t_{\circ}}=\frac{1}{2}(e\cdot x)^{2},\quad\mbox{where}\quad|e-\boldsymbol{e}_{n}|\leq\frac{1}{1000}.

Also, we can relabel the Ansatz
\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}
(simply interchanging
(1)
and
(2)
, when necessary) so that

\partial_{n}\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(1)}\sim(x_{n})_{+}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\partial_{n}\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(2)}\sim(x_{n})_{-}.

Recalling the notation
\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}
introduced in (
13.25
), let
g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}=g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t)
be the unique polynomials of degree
k-1
which solve the polynomial equations

(14.5)

\partial_{n}{\widetilde{\mathscr{P}}}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(x,t)=0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad x_{n}=g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t)+O\big((|x|+|t|^{1/2})^{k}\big).

Also, using (
14.4
), the corresponding nondegeneracy for
\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}
, and (
14.1
),
we have

\partial\{u^{(i)}(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot)>0\}\cap K_{r}\subset\partial\big\{\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}>0\big\}+B_{Cr^{(k+\alpha)/2}}(0).

Hence, since
\partial\{\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}>0\}=\{\partial_{n}\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}=0\}
, combining these two informations we obtain

(14.6)

\partial\{u^{(i)}(x_{\circ}+\,\cdot\,,t_{\circ}+\,\cdot)>0\}\cap K_{r}\subset\big\{|x_{n}-g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t)|\leq Cr^{(k+\alpha)/2}\big\}.

Now, given a polynomial
P=P(x,t)
, we denote by
\Gamma_{i}[P]
the
i
-homogeneous part of
P
with respect to the usual Euclidean structure in
(x,t)
(namely, the variables
x
and
t
have the same homogeneity).
Note that (
14.2
) implies, in particular,

(14.7)

\Gamma_{\ell}[g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}]=\Gamma_{\ell}[g_{k^{\prime},x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}]\quad\mbox{for all }\,\ell\leq\min(\lfloor k/2\rfloor,\lfloor k^{\prime}/2\rfloor),

while (
14.3
) gives

(14.8)

\big|\Gamma_{\ell}[g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}](x_{\circ}+\cdot,t_{\circ}+\cdot)\big|\leq C_{m,\ell}\quad\mbox{for all }0\leq\ell\leq\lfloor k/2\rfloor.

This suggests the following definition of the
\ell
-jet at
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
:

(14.9)

J^{(i)}_{\ell,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x^{\prime},t):=\sum_{l=0}^{\ell}\Gamma_{\ell}[g_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}](x^{\prime}-x^{\prime}_{\circ},t-t_{\circ}),

where
k
is an arbitrary number such that
\lfloor k/2\rfloor\geq\ell
, say
k=2(\ell+1)
.
Note that, with this definition, it follows from (
14.6
) that

(14.10)

\partial\{u^{(i)}>0\}\cap\big((x_{\circ},t_{\circ})+K_{r})\subset\big\{|(x-x_{\circ})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{n}-J_{\ell,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime},t-t_{\circ})|\leq Cr^{\ell+1}\big\}.

We also recall that, thanks to Proposition
8.3
, for every pair of singular points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}),(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
we have

(14.11)

|t_{1}-t_{\circ}|\leq C_{m}|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|^{2}.

Thanks to this bound, we can apply (
14.1
) at two points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}),(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
inside
K_{r}
, with
r:=|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|^{1/2}
,
and the two sets

(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})+K_{r}
and
(x_{1},t_{1})+K_{r}
intersect in a domain which contains
B_{10r^{2}}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}-r^{2}/2,t_{\circ}-r^{2}/4]
. Hence, for any
k\geq 3,

\big\|\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{1},t_{1}}^{(i)}(\cdot-x_{1},\cdot-t_{1})-\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(\cdot-x_{\circ},\cdot-t_{\circ})\big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{10r^{2}}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}-r^{2}/2,t_{\circ}-r^{2}/4])}\leq C_{m,k}r^{k+\alpha}.

Since
\mathscr{P}
are essentially “half-polynomials”, it is easy to check that the bound above implies that

\big\|\partial_{n}\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{1},t_{1}}^{(i)}(\cdot-x_{1},\cdot-t_{1})-\partial_{n}\mathscr{P}_{k,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}^{(i)}(\cdot-x_{\circ},\cdot-t_{\circ})\big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{10r^{2}}(x_{\circ})\times[t_{\circ}-r^{2}/2,t_{\circ}-r^{2}/4])}\leq C_{m,k}r^{k-2+\alpha}.

Now, given
\ell
, we apply the estimate above with
k:=2(\ell+2)
.
Then, recalling the definition of
J^{(i)}
(see (
14.5
) and (
14.9
)),
since all terms of Euclidean homogeneity at least
\ell+1
have a size bounded by
C_{m,\ell}(r^{2})^{\ell+1}
, recalling that
r^{2}=|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|
we deduce that

\big\|J^{(i)}_{\ell,x_{1},t_{1}}-J^{(i)}_{\ell,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{10|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|}(x_{\circ}^{\prime})\times[t_{\circ}-|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|/2,t_{\circ}-|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|/4])}\leq C_{m,\ell}|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|^{\ell+1}\qquad\forall\,\ell\geq 1.

Recalling (
14.11
), it is easy to check that this bound is exactly what is required to apply Whitney’s extension theorem in order to produce two
C^{\infty}
functions
\mathcal{G}^{(i)}:B^{\prime}_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m})\to\mathbb{R}
(recall that
B^{\prime}_{r_{m}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
)
such that, for all
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
as above and for all
\ell
, we have

\mathcal{G}^{(i)}(x,t)=J^{(i)}_{\ell,x_{\circ},t_{\circ}}(x,t)+O\big((|x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime}|+|t-t_{\circ}^{\prime}|)^{\ell+1}\big).

In particular, thanks to (
14.10
),
every point in
\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
belongs both to
\{x_{n}=G^{(1)}(x^{\prime},t)\}
and to
\{x_{n}=G^{(2)}(x^{\prime},t)\}
.
This shows that
\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
can be covered by a
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold of class
C^{\infty}
, as wanted.

In order to conclude the proof of the theorem we need to prove that, after removing a set of parabolic dimension
n-2
, we can obtain a set
\Sigma^{\infty}
such that
\pi_{t}(\Sigma^{\infty})
has zero Hausdorff dimension.
To this aim, we define
\Sigma^{\infty}
as the set of points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma_{m}^{\diamond}\cap(B_{r_{m}}\times(-r_{m},r_{m}))
at which the two functions

\mathcal{G}^{(1)}(x^{\prime},t_{\circ})
and
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}(x^{\prime},t_{\circ})
are tangent in
x^{\prime}
at infinite order.
The first important observation is that the set of points which do
not
belong to
\Sigma^{\infty}
has parabolic dimension at most
n-2
.

Indeed, thanks to Lemma
9.8
the two
n
-dimensional (in the Euclidean sense) manifolds
\{x_{n}=\mathcal{G}^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t)\}
intersect transversally “in time” at every singular point
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})
(namely, their derivatives in the time variable never match). Hence, their intersection has at most Euclidean dimension
n-1
.
Also, by definition of
\Sigma^{\infty}
, if
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\Sigma^{\diamond}\setminus\Sigma^{\infty}
then the two functions
\mathcal{G}^{(1)}(x^{\prime},t_{\circ})
and
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}(x^{\prime},t_{\circ})
, when expanded at
x^{\prime}=x_{\circ}
, have different coefficients at some order.
This reduces the bound on the dimension by
1
, hence
\Sigma^{\diamond}\setminus\Sigma^{\infty}
has Euclidean dimension at most
n-2
. Thanks to (
14.10
) and Lemma
8.9
, this implies that

\dim_{\rm par}(\Sigma^{\diamond}\setminus\Sigma^{\infty})\leq n-2
, as desired.

Finally, let us show for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for all pair of points
(x_{\circ},t_{\circ}),(x_{1},t_{1})\in\Sigma^{\infty}
we have

(14.12)

|t_{1}-t_{\circ}|\leq C_{m,k}|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|^{k}.

Indeed, by definition of
\Sigma^{\infty}
and the discussion above, there exist functions
h,A^{(1)},A^{(2)}
such that

(14.13)

\mathcal{G}^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t+t_{\circ})=h(x^{\prime})+A^{(i)}(x^{\prime},t)+O(|x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime}|^{k}).

where
A^{(i)}(x^{\prime},0)\equiv 0
.
In addition, thanks to Lemma
9.8
,

(14.14)

(A^{(2)}-A^{(1)})(x^{\prime},t)\geq ct>0\qquad\mbox{ for }|x^{\prime}-x_{\circ}^{\prime}|+t\ll 1,\quad t>0.

Then, since
\mathcal{G}^{(1)}(x_{1}^{\prime},t_{1})=\mathcal{G}^{(2)}(x_{1}^{\prime},t_{1})
and assuming (with no loss of generality) that
t_{\circ}<t_{1}
,
we can evaluate (
14.13
) at
(x_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}-t_{\circ})
to obtain

A^{(1)}(x_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}-t_{\circ})-A^{(2)}(x_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}-t_{\circ})=O(|x_{1}-x_{\circ}|^{k}).

Combining this bound with (
14.14
) we get (
14.12
).
As shown for instance in
[
FRS20
, Proposition 7.7(a)]
,
(
14.12
) implies that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{t}(\Sigma^{\infty})\big)=0
.
∎

Finally, as a consequence of Theorem
1.3
, we deduce Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.4
.

Thanks to Theorem
1.3
we know that
\Sigma=\Sigma^{\infty}\cup(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\infty})
, where
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{t}(\Sigma^{\infty})\big)=0
and
{\rm dim}_{\rm par}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\infty})\leq n-2
.
Hence, recalling the definition of parabolic dimension, this implies that
{\rm dim}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi_{t}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\infty})\big)\leq\frac{n-2}{2}
, and the result follows.
∎
